=== cpp2-01 | const correctness
--- teach
The basics course ended with pointers: a raw pointer like `int*` to [[look at something|look-only-pointers]], and a `std::unique_ptr` to own it. Looking at a thing without changing it is the idea this lesson starts from.

This course turns what you learned in the basics into careful, everyday modern C++: the standard library's containers and algorithms, classes that guard their own rules, and the debugging habits that catch C++'s quiet bugs. It opens with the keyword that makes code safe to pass around: `const`.

### A promise the compiler holds you to

Picture lending a friend your model rocket inside a glass case. They can look at every fin and every decal, but they cannot pick it up and bend anything. `const` (short for constant) is that glass case.

You met it on variables in the basics course. After `const double pi = 3.14159;`, any line that tries to change `pi` is a compile error. So `const` is a promise, and it is one the [[compiler holds you to|const-in-flight-rules]]: break it, and the program does not build. On plain variables it is handy. On **references** and on **member functions** it matters far more.

### const on a reference parameter

You have written this kind of parameter since the vector lesson:

```cpp
int total(const std::vector<int>& v);   // reads v, never copies it
```

The `&` (read "reference to") means the function works on the caller's own vector, with no copy. The `const` means it promises not to change it. Together they tell whoever calls `total` three things: "I need to look at your object, I will not change it, and I will not copy it."

### const on a member function

In the classes lesson, `Account` had `int balance() const`. That `const`, written *after* a member function's parameter list, is the same promise made by a member function: calling it leaves the object unchanged.

Here is a small class with one member function of each kind:

```cpp
class Wallet {
public:
    void add(int cents) { cents_ += cents; }   // changes the object
    int cents() const { return cents_; }       // only looks
private:
    int cents_ = 0;
};
```

- `add` changes `cents_` (`+=` is "add to"), so it has no `const`.
- `cents()` only reads, so it gets one. A member function like this, which hands back a piece of the object's data, is called a **getter**.
- `int cents_ = 0;` gives the member its starting value right where it is declared, so every new wallet starts empty.

### Why it matters: a const reference only allows const calls

Here is a function that takes a wallet by `const` reference. It is a **free function**: an ordinary function that is not a member of any class.

```cpp
void show(const Wallet& w) {
    std::cout << w.cents();   // fine: cents() is const
    w.add(5);                 // compile error: add() is not const
}
```

Through a `const Wallet&`, the compiler lets you call [[only the member functions marked const|const-view]]. It stops at the second line. clang, the compiler in this app, says:

```text
error: 'this' argument to member function 'add' has type 'const Wallet', but function is not marked const
```

That is exactly what you want: `show` promised not to change the wallet, and the compiler caught it trying.

### The mistake people really make

**Watch out:** the common mistake is the reverse. You write a getter and **forget** the `const`:

```cpp
int cents() { return cents_; }   // no const
```

The class still works on its own, so you do not notice. But the moment someone passes a `Wallet` around the normal way, by `const Wallet&`, every call to that getter fails to compile. The getter only reads, but you never promised that, so the compiler has to assume it might write.

### The rule of thumb

- Parameters you only read: `const T&`. Here `T` stands for any type, as in `const std::string&` or `const Wallet&`. For small things like `int` or `double`, a plain copy is fine.
- Member functions that only read: mark them `const`.
- Loops that only read: `for (const auto& x : items)`. The `auto` lets the compiler work out the element type, the `&` skips the copy, and the `const` promises not to change anything.

### Inside a const member function

A `const` member function cannot change any member. It can also only call *other* `const` member functions of the same object, because calling one without `const` might change the object behind its back.

How does the compiler keep track? Every member function gets a hidden pointer called **this**: the address of the object it was called on. In `w.cents()`, `this` points at `w`. Inside a `const` member function, [[this points at a const object|this-pointer]], so every change through it is refused. That is why the error above talks about the "'this' argument".

### Two tools the task needs

The task keeps its readings in a vector, so here are two small tools you will meet in its solution and all through this course.

First, `v.size()` returns a `std::size_t`, an **unsigned** whole-number type: one that can never be negative. When your function returns an `int`, [[convert it|size-t-to-int]] with the `static_cast` from the arithmetic lesson:

```cpp
int count() const { return static_cast<int>(readings_.size()); }
```

Second, `std::min_element`, from the header `<algorithm>`, finds the smallest element for you. You hand it `v.begin()` and `v.end()`, which mark the start and the end of the vector, so together they mean "search the whole thing". It gives back an **[[iterator|iterator-preview]]**: a position in the vector, used like a pointer. So put `*` in front to get the value, the way `*p` followed a pointer in the pointers lesson:

```cpp
std::vector<int> r = {12, -3, 7};
int low = *std::min_element(r.begin(), r.end());   // -3
```

`std::max_element` works the same way and finds the largest. The next lessons cover algorithms and iterators properly.

::: context look-only-pointers const works on pointers too
The same promise fits pointers. `const int* p` is a look-only pointer: you may read `*p`, but `*p = 0;` is a compile error. Careful code writes the "raw pointers to look at things" rule from the pointers lesson exactly this way. Reading the type from right to left helps: `const int* p` is "p is a pointer to an int that is const". A `const` after the star, as in `int* const p`, means something else: the pointer itself can never be moved to point somewhere new, though the int it points at can still change.
:::

::: context const-in-flight-rules Why flight-software rules love const
`const` costs nothing while the program runs: all the checking happens while it compiles. Yet it catches a whole family of bugs, such as a function that was only meant to read a table of sensor limits but changed one by accident. That is why careful coding standards ask for it. MISRA C++, a rule book used for car, aircraft and spacecraft software, says that a variable which is never changed shall be declared `const`, and that a pointer or reference parameter shall be to `const` when the function does not change what it refers to. The C++ Core Guidelines say the same: make objects and member functions `const` by default.
:::

::: context const-view A read-only window onto the same object
A `const Wallet&` does not make the wallet itself unchangeable. It is a read-only window onto it. The caller still holds the wallet under its own name and can call `add` whenever it likes. Only code that sees the wallet through the `const` window is limited to the `const` member functions.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="hb" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto">

    </marker>
  </defs>

  <text x="295" y="95" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">cents_ = 250</text>
  <text x="15" y="64" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">w.add(5)</text>
  <line x1="95" y1="60" x2="243" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#hb)"/>

  <line x1="105" y1="100" x2="243" y2="100" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#hb)"/>

  <line x1="105" y1="140" x2="188" y2="140" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>

```
:::

::: context this-pointer The hidden pointer called this
When you write `w.cents()`, C++ quietly hands the function a pointer to `w`, named `this`. Inside the function, `cents_` is short for `this->cents_`. The arrow `->` follows a pointer and then picks a member; it means the same as `(*this).cents_`. In an ordinary member function of `Wallet`, `this` has the type `Wallet*`. In a `const` one it has the type `const Wallet*`, a look-only pointer, so any line that writes a member is refused.
:::

::: context size-t-to-int Why convert size() to an int
A `std::size_t` is 8 bytes on a 64-bit computer, so it can count up to 18446744073709551615. An `int` is 4 bytes and stops at 2147483647. `static_cast<int>` is safe as long as the vector holds fewer elements than that, which a list of thermometer readings always will. The cast also says out loud "I know these two types differ". Mixing them without saying so draws warnings: with this app's `-Wall -Wextra`, comparing an `int` counter with `v.size()` gives "comparison of integers of different signs".
:::

::: context iterator-preview Why the task promises at least one reading
`std::min_element` searches from `begin()` up to, but not including, `end()`. If there are several equal smallest values, it points at the first one. For an empty vector there is nothing to find, so it gives back `end()`, a mark one past the last element that is not an element at all. Putting `*` in front of that is undefined behavior, like following `nullptr`. That is why the task lets you assume at least one reading. Iterators get their own lesson soon, "Iterators and range-for".
:::
--- task
Finish the `Thermometer` class in the starter so it is const-correct. It keeps its readings in a private vector called `readings_`.

- `void record(int reading)` adds a reading. The starter's version is already right: it changes the object, so it gets no `const`.
- `int count() const` returns how many readings there are.
- `int lowest() const` returns the smallest reading, and `int highest() const` the largest. You may assume there is at least one reading.

Then fix the free function `range_of` so its parameter is `const Thermometer& t`, and make it return `highest - lowest`. For the readings 12, -3 and 7 that is 12 - (-3) = 15. For a single reading it is 0.

Add `#include <algorithm>` at the top for `std::min_element` and `std::max_element`. The checks use your class through a `const Thermometer&`, and also make a `const Thermometer` with no readings, whose `count()` must be 0. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

class Thermometer {
public:
    void record(int reading) { readings_.push_back(reading); }
    int count() { return 0; }
    int lowest() { return 0; }
    int highest() { return 0; }
private:
    std::vector<int> readings_;
};

int range_of(Thermometer t) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

class Thermometer {
public:
    void record(int reading) { readings_.push_back(reading); }
    int count() const { return static_cast<int>(readings_.size()); }
    int lowest() const { return *std::min_element(readings_.begin(), readings_.end()); }
    int highest() const { return *std::max_element(readings_.begin(), readings_.end()); }
private:
    std::vector<int> readings_;
};

int range_of(const Thermometer& t) {
    return t.highest() - t.lowest();
}
--- hint
Every function that only reads gets `const` after its parameter list, like `int cents() const` in `Wallet`. And `range_of` should take `const Thermometer& t`, so it neither copies nor changes the thermometer.
--- hint
`std::min_element(readings_.begin(), readings_.end())` (from `<algorithm>`) gives back an iterator to the smallest reading; put `*` in front to get the value. For the count, convert `readings_.size()` with `static_cast<int>`.
--- hint
The shapes are `int count() const { return static_cast<int>(readings_.size()); }` and `int lowest() const { return *std::min_element(readings_.begin(), readings_.end()); }`; `highest` is the same with `std::max_element`. Then `range_of` returns `t.highest() - t.lowest();`.
--- check test | count, lowest and highest work through a const reference
[] { Thermometer t; t.record(12); t.record(-3); t.record(7); const Thermometer& view = t; return view.count() == 3 && view.lowest() == -3 && view.highest() == 12; }()
--- check case | range_of 12, -3, 7 is 15
[] { Thermometer t; t.record(12); t.record(-3); t.record(7); return range_of(t); }()
=> 15
--- check case | One reading has a range of 0
[] { Thermometer t; t.record(5); return range_of(t); }()
=> 0
--- check case | A fresh thermometer has no readings
[] { const Thermometer t; return t.count(); }()
=> 0

+++ practice | A logbook you can read through a const reference
--- task
Finish the class `Logbook`. It keeps its entries in a private `std::vector<std::string>` called `entries_`.

- `void add(const std::string& entry)` adds an entry at the end. The starter's version is already right.
- `int size() const` returns how many entries there are.
- `std::string latest() const` returns the newest entry, the one added last. With no entries it returns `""`.

After adding `"launch"` and then `"orbit"`, `size()` is 2 and `latest()` is `"orbit"`. The checks call `size()` and `latest()` through a `const Logbook&`, and on a `const Logbook` with no entries. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Logbook {
public:
    void add(const std::string& entry) { entries_.push_back(entry); }
    int size() { return 0; }
    std::string latest() { return ""; }
private:
    std::vector<std::string> entries_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Logbook {
public:
    void add(const std::string& entry) { entries_.push_back(entry); }
    int size() const { return static_cast<int>(entries_.size()); }
    std::string latest() const {
        if (entries_.empty()) return "";
        return entries_[entries_.size() - 1];
    }
private:
    std::vector<std::string> entries_;
};
--- hint
Both getters only read, so each one needs `const` after its parameter list.
--- hint
The newest entry sits at the last index, which is `entries_.size() - 1`. Check `entries_.empty()` first, because an empty vector has no last index.
--- check test | size and latest work through a const reference
[] { Logbook log; log.add("launch"); log.add("orbit"); const Logbook& view = log; return view.size() == 2 && view.latest() == "orbit"; }()
--- check case | One entry is also the latest
[] { Logbook log; log.add("docked"); const Logbook& view = log; return view.latest(); }()
=> "docked"
--- check test | An empty const logbook has size 0 and no latest entry
[] { const Logbook log; return log.size() == 0 && log.latest() == ""; }()

+++ practice | Reading a const map without changing it
--- task
Write `int stock_of(const std::map<std::string, int>& stock, const std::string& item)`. It returns how many of `item` are in `stock`, or `0` if `item` is not a key.

The parameter must stay a `const` reference, so the function can be called with a `const` map, and it must never add a key. `stock_of({{"bolt", 12}, {"nut", 30}}, "nut")` is 30, and `stock_of({{"bolt", 12}}, "gear")` is 0.

The starter uses `stock[item]` on a reference without `const`. That is exactly what the checks refuse. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>

int stock_of(std::map<std::string, int>& stock, const std::string& item) {
    return stock[item];
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>

int stock_of(const std::map<std::string, int>& stock, const std::string& item) {
    for (const auto& [name, qty] : stock) {
        if (name == item) return qty;
    }
    return 0;
}
--- hint
`[ ]` on a map may add a key, so C++ does not let you use it through a `const` map. You need another way to reach the value.
--- hint
A range-for loop only reads, so it works on a `const` map: `for (const auto& [name, qty] : stock)`. Return `qty` when `name` matches, and `0` after the loop.
--- check case | A key that is there
[] { const std::map<std::string, int> stock = {{"bolt", 12}, {"nut", 30}}; return stock_of(stock, "nut"); }()
=> 30
--- check case | A missing key gives 0
[] { const std::map<std::string, int> stock = {{"bolt", 12}}; return stock_of(stock, "gear"); }()
=> 0
--- check case | A key whose amount is 0
[] { const std::map<std::string, int> stock = {{"fuse", 0}, {"wire", 4}}; return stock_of(stock, "fuse"); }()
=> 0
--- check case | An empty map
[] { const std::map<std::string, int> stock; return stock_of(stock, "bolt"); }()
=> 0

+++ practice | The fullest tank, as a look-only pointer
--- task
The starter has a class `Tank` with a name and a number of litres. Make its two getters `const`, then write two free functions. No `main`.

- `int total_fuel(const std::vector<Tank>& tanks)` returns the litres of all the tanks added up, and `0` for no tanks.
- `const Tank* fullest(const std::vector<Tank>& tanks)` returns a pointer to the tank with the most litres. If two tanks tie, return the first of them. For an empty vector, return `nullptr`.

For tanks `A` with 40, `B` with 75 and `C` with 75, `total_fuel` is 190 and `fullest` points at `B`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Tank {
public:
    Tank(const std::string& name, int litres) : name_(name), litres_(litres) {}
    std::string name() { return name_; }
    int litres() { return litres_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
    int litres_;
};

int total_fuel(const std::vector<Tank>& tanks) {
    return 0;
}

const Tank* fullest(const std::vector<Tank>& tanks) {
    return nullptr;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Tank {
public:
    Tank(const std::string& name, int litres) : name_(name), litres_(litres) {}
    std::string name() const { return name_; }
    int litres() const { return litres_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
    int litres_;
};

int total_fuel(const std::vector<Tank>& tanks) {
    int total = 0;
    for (const auto& t : tanks) total += t.litres();
    return total;
}

const Tank* fullest(const std::vector<Tank>& tanks) {
    const Tank* best = nullptr;
    for (const auto& t : tanks) {
        if (best == nullptr || t.litres() > (*best).litres()) best = &t;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Inside `total_fuel`, the loop `for (const auto& t : tanks)` gives you each tank as a `const Tank&`, so `t.litres()` only compiles once `litres()` is marked `const`.
--- hint
For `fullest`, keep a `const Tank* best = nullptr;`. For each tank `t`, point `best` at it with `best = &t;` when `best` is still `nullptr` or when `t` has strictly more litres than `(*best).litres()`. Strictly more keeps the first of a tie.
--- check case | total_fuel adds every tank
total_fuel({Tank("A", 40), Tank("B", 75), Tank("C", 75)})
=> 190
--- check case | No tanks hold no fuel
total_fuel({})
=> 0
--- check case | fullest picks the first of a tie
[] { std::vector<Tank> t{Tank("A", 40), Tank("B", 75), Tank("C", 75)}; return fullest(t)->name(); }()
=> "B"
--- check test | fullest points into the caller's own vector
[] { std::vector<Tank> t{Tank("A", 40), Tank("B", 10)}; return fullest(t) == &t[0]; }()
--- check test | fullest of nothing is nullptr
fullest({}) == nullptr

+++ practice | Stats that cope with no numbers at all
--- task
Finish the class `Stats`, which keeps whole numbers in a private vector. No `main`.

- `void add(int x)` stores a number. The starter's version is already right.
- `int count() const` returns how many numbers there are.
- `int spread() const` returns the largest number minus the smallest. With no numbers, it returns `0`.
- `double mean() const` returns the average: the total divided by the count. With no numbers, it returns `0.0`.

For 4, -6 and 8, `spread()` is 14 and `mean()` is 2.0. With only the number 5, `spread()` is 0 and `mean()` is 5.0. Every getter must work through a `const Stats&`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

class Stats {
public:
    void add(int x) { values_.push_back(x); }
    int count() { return static_cast<int>(values_.size()); }
    int spread() { return values_[values_.size() - 1] - values_[0]; }
    double mean() { return 0.0; }
private:
    std::vector<int> values_;
};
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

class Stats {
public:
    void add(int x) { values_.push_back(x); }
    int count() const { return static_cast<int>(values_.size()); }
    int spread() const {
        if (values_.empty()) return 0;
        return *std::max_element(values_.begin(), values_.end()) - *std::min_element(values_.begin(), values_.end());
    }
    double mean() const {
        if (values_.empty()) return 0.0;
        long long total = 0;
        for (int x : values_) total += x;
        return static_cast<double>(total) / values_.size();
    }
private:
    std::vector<int> values_;
};
--- hint
The starter's `spread` takes the last number minus the first. That is only right when the numbers were added in order. Use `std::min_element` and `std::max_element` from `<algorithm>` instead.
--- hint
Check `values_.empty()` at the top of `spread` and `mean`, before you touch any element. For the mean, add the numbers into a total, then divide with `static_cast<double>(total) / values_.size()`.
--- check test | spread and mean through a const reference
[] { Stats s; s.add(4); s.add(-6); s.add(8); const Stats& v = s; return v.count() == 3 && v.spread() == 14 && std::abs(v.mean() - 2.0) < 1e-9; }()
--- check test | One number: spread 0, mean is that number
[] { Stats s; s.add(5); const Stats& v = s; return v.spread() == 0 && std::abs(v.mean() - 5.0) < 1e-9; }()
--- check test | No numbers: count 0, spread 0, mean 0.0
[] { const Stats s; return s.count() == 0 && s.spread() == 0 && s.mean() == 0.0; }()
--- check test | All negative, not in order
[] { Stats s; s.add(-3); s.add(-9); s.add(-5); const Stats& v = s; return v.spread() == 6 && std::abs(v.mean() + 17.0 / 3) < 1e-9; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the warning that will not compile
--- task
**Bug report:** "`warning` does not compile. clang says: 'this' argument to member function 'name' has type 'const Sensor', but function is not marked const."

A `Sensor` has a name and a reading. The free function `warning(const Sensor& s, int limit)` should return the name followed by `" HIGH"` when the reading is above `limit`, and the name followed by `" ok"` otherwise: `"ALT HIGH"` or `"ALT ok"`. It takes the sensor by `const` reference, which is right: it only reads. Find the cause and fix it there. Keep `warning`'s parameters exactly as they are. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

class Sensor {
public:
    Sensor(const std::string& name, int reading) : name_(name), reading_(reading) {}
    void update(int reading) { reading_ = reading; }
    std::string name() { return name_; }
    int reading() { return reading_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
    int reading_;
};

std::string warning(const Sensor& s, int limit) {
    if (s.reading() > limit) return s.name() + " HIGH";
    return s.name() + " ok";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

class Sensor {
public:
    Sensor(const std::string& name, int reading) : name_(name), reading_(reading) {}
    void update(int reading) { reading_ = reading; }
    std::string name() const { return name_; }
    int reading() const { return reading_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
    int reading_;
};

std::string warning(const Sensor& s, int limit) {
    if (s.reading() > limit) return s.name() + " HIGH";
    return s.name() + " ok";
}
--- hint
The error is about `name`, but read the whole class: which member functions only look at the object?
--- hint
`name()` and `reading()` are getters, so each needs `const` after its parameter list. `update` changes the reading, so it stays as it is.
--- check case | A reading above the limit
warning(Sensor("ALT", 4200), 4000)
=> "ALT HIGH"
--- check case | A reading equal to the limit is ok
warning(Sensor("ALT", 4000), 4000)
=> "ALT ok"
--- check case | warning after an update
[] { Sensor s("TEMP", 90); s.update(-5); return warning(s, 50); }()
=> "TEMP ok"
--- check test | The getters work on a const sensor
[] { const Sensor s("V", 28); return s.name() == "V" && s.reading() == 28; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: an altitude log
--- task
Write a class `AltitudeLog` that records altitude readings in order, and one free function. Every member function that only reads must be `const`. No `main`.

- `void record(int metres)` adds a reading at the end.
- `int count() const` returns how many readings there are.
- `int peak() const` returns the highest reading, or `0` when there are none.
- `int climbs() const` returns how many readings are strictly higher than the reading just before them. For 100, 250, 250, 180, 300 that is 2: 250 is higher than 100, and 300 is higher than 180.
- `int at(int i) const` returns the reading at index `i`. The free function below needs it, because it cannot see the private readings.
- `bool safe_descent(const AltitudeLog& log, int max_drop)`, a free function, returns `true` when no reading is more than `max_drop` metres below the one just before it. For 100, 250, 250, 180, 300 the biggest drop is 70, so `safe_descent(log, 70)` is `true` and `safe_descent(log, 69)` is `false`. A log with fewer than two readings is always safe.
--- starter
#include <vector>

class AltitudeLog {
public:
    void record(int metres) {}
private:
    std::vector<int> readings_;
};

bool safe_descent(const AltitudeLog& log, int max_drop) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

class AltitudeLog {
public:
    void record(int metres) { readings_.push_back(metres); }
    int count() const { return static_cast<int>(readings_.size()); }
    int at(int i) const { return readings_[i]; }
    int peak() const {
        if (readings_.empty()) return 0;
        return *std::max_element(readings_.begin(), readings_.end());
    }
    int climbs() const {
        int n = 0;
        for (int i = 1; i < count(); ++i) {
            if (readings_[i] > readings_[i - 1]) ++n;
        }
        return n;
    }
private:
    std::vector<int> readings_;
};

bool safe_descent(const AltitudeLog& log, int max_drop) {
    for (int i = 1; i < log.count(); ++i) {
        if (log.at(i - 1) - log.at(i) > max_drop) return false;
    }
    return true;
}
--- hint
`climbs` compares each reading with the one before it, so start the loop at index 1, not 0. A log with 0 or 1 readings then never enters the loop at all.
--- hint
A drop is the reading before minus the reading now: `log.at(i - 1) - log.at(i)`. `safe_descent` returns `false` as soon as one drop is bigger than `max_drop`, and `true` after the loop.
--- check test | count, peak and climbs through a const reference
[] { AltitudeLog a; for (int m : {100, 250, 250, 180, 300}) a.record(m); const AltitudeLog& v = a; return v.count() == 5 && v.peak() == 300 && v.climbs() == 2; }()
--- check test | A drop of exactly max_drop is safe, one more is not
[] { AltitudeLog a; for (int m : {100, 250, 250, 180, 300}) a.record(m); return safe_descent(a, 70) && !safe_descent(a, 69); }()
--- check test | An empty log: no peak, no climbs, safe
[] { const AltitudeLog a; return a.count() == 0 && a.peak() == 0 && a.climbs() == 0 && safe_descent(a, 0); }()
--- check test | One reading is safe and has no climbs
[] { AltitudeLog a; a.record(-20); return a.peak() == -20 && a.climbs() == 0 && safe_descent(a, 0); }()

=== cpp2-02 | Debugging: the function that changed a copy
--- teach
Last lesson, `const` stopped a function from changing something it was only meant to look at. This lesson is about the opposite bug: a function that is *meant* to change your data, runs without a single complaint, and changes nothing.

### Bugs that make no noise

Some [[bugs|where-bug-comes-from]] make no noise at all. The program compiles, it runs, and it does nothing: no error, no crash, the numbers are still the old ones. Guessing at a bug like that can waste an afternoon. It calls for a method.

### A method instead of guesswork

An **assumption** is something you believe is true but have not checked. A silent bug always hides behind one. So debug in four steps:

1. **Reproduce** it with the [[smallest input you can|smallest-input]]: one or two items, numbers you can check in your head.
2. **Check your assumptions**, one at a time. "The function runs" — does it? [[Print something inside it|prints-and-debuggers]]. "It changes my data" — print the data inside the function *and* straight after the call.
3. **Narrow down** to the first place where what you see differs from what you expect.
4. **Fix the cause**, not the [[symptom|cause-not-symptom]]. Returning the changed vector and assigning it back would make the output right, but it hides a design mistake that will bite again.

### The classic silent bug: working on a copy

In the references lesson you saw that C++ hands a function a photocopy unless you ask for a reference. In C++ the classic silent bug is exactly that: **working on a copy**. Two places make copies without saying so. "By value" means "as a copy":

```cpp
void reset(std::vector<int> v) {     // 1. a by-value parameter copies the caller's vector
    for (int x : v) {                // 2. a by-value loop variable copies each element
        x = 0;
    }
}
```

Both copies are changed, and then thrown away when the function ends. The caller's vector never sees a thing.

### Following the method

Step 2 says: print inside, and print after. Here is what that looks like:

```cpp
void reset(std::vector<int> v) {
    for (int x : v) {
        x = 0;
        std::cout << "inside: " << x << "\n";   // prints 0, twice
    }
}

std::vector<int> d = {4, 5};
reset(d);
std::cout << "after: " << d[0] << "\n";          // prints 4
```

Inside the loop you see zeros. After the call, the caller's vector is unchanged. That gap, right inside and wrong outside, is [[exactly where the copy is|where-the-copy-is]].

### The fix: a reference at each copy point

Put a reference at *each* copy point: `std::vector<int>& v` for the parameter, and `for (int& x : v)` for the loop.

```cpp
void reset(std::vector<int>& v) {
    for (int& x : v) {
        x = 0;
    }
}
```

Fixing only one of the two still leaves the bug. Fix only the parameter, and the loop still zeroes a copy of each element. That is why step 2 matters: check again after every change you make.

`auto` follows the same rule. `for (auto item : items)` copies each element, the same as `for (int x : v)`. `for (auto& item : items)` makes `item` a reference, so changes stick.

**Watch out:** the compiler rarely warns you. In the tiny `reset` above, clang does print `warning: variable 'x' set but not used`, a clue worth reading. But once the loop changes a member of a struct, as in `item.price_cents = …`, there is no warning at all. Only your method finds it.

::: context where-bug-comes-from Where the word bug comes from
Engineers called faults "bugs" long before computers; Thomas Edison used the word in letters in the 1870s. In September 1947, the team running the Harvard Mark II computer found a real moth stuck in one of its relays. They taped it into their logbook with the note "First actual case of bug being found." Grace Hopper, who worked on that machine and later helped create the language COBOL, loved telling the story. The logbook page is now at the Smithsonian's National Museum of American History.
:::

::: context smallest-input Why a tiny input helps
With two items and round prices, you know the right answer before you run anything: 10% off 1000 is 900. With a thousand items you would need the program to check the program. A small failing case is also quick to run again after every change. Programmers call it a *minimal reproducible example*, and it is what you attach to a bug report so someone else can see the bug for themselves.
:::

::: context prints-and-debuggers Prints, debuggers and telemetry
Adding print lines is the oldest debugging tool, and still one of the most used. Programs called debuggers, such as gdb and lldb, can instead pause a running program at any line and show every variable, without editing the code at all. Either way the idea is the same: stop guessing and look at the real values. Delete your print lines once the bug is fixed. A spacecraft does a version of this all the time: it sends a stream of measurements called *telemetry* back to Earth, so engineers can see inside a program running millions of kilometers away.
:::

::: context cause-not-symptom Symptom or cause
The *symptom* is what you see going wrong; the *cause* is why. Here, making the function return its changed vector, so the caller writes `cart = apply_discount(cart, 10);`, would make the prices right. But the function's name and its `void` return promise to change the caller's vector in place, and the next person who calls it that normal way hits the same bug again. Accident investigators at NASA hunt for the *root cause* of every mishap for the same reason: a symptom that is patched over comes back somewhere else.
:::

::: context where-the-copy-is Three boxes, two copies
The caller's `d` is copied into the parameter `v`, and each element of `v` is copied again into `x`. The zero lands in the last box only. Printing inside the loop looks at `x`; printing after the call looks at `d`.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="60" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">d (caller)</text>
  <text x="180" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">v (parameter)</text>
  <text x="300" y="36" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">x (loop copy)</text>
  <rect x="15" y="45" width="90" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="135" y="45" width="90" height="40" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="255" y="45" width="90" height="40" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="60" y="70" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">{4, 5}</text>
  <text x="180" y="70" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">{4, 5}</text>
  <text x="300" y="70" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">0</text>
  <line x1="107" y1="65" x2="131" y2="65" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="133,65 125,61 125,69" fill="#6c7a93"/>
  <line x1="227" y1="65" x2="251" y2="65" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="253,65 245,61 245,69" fill="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="60" y="108" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">after: 4</text>
  <text x="300" y="108" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">inside: 0</text>
  <text x="180" y="138" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">the gap between the two prints is the copy</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** "After `apply_discount(cart, 10)` every price in `cart` is still the old one. No error, no warning."

An `Item` is a struct with a `name` and a `price_cents`. `apply_discount` should lower each item's `price_cents` by `percent` percent, rounding down, so 10% off 250 is 225. The arithmetic line inside the loop is already right: whole-number division rounds down for you.

Use the method from the explanation to find why the caller's items never change, then fix the cause. Keep the function's name and its `void` return. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Item {
    std::string name;
    int price_cents;
};

// Lowers every price by `percent` percent, rounding down.
void apply_discount(std::vector<Item> items, int percent) {
    for (auto item : items) {
        item.price_cents = item.price_cents * (100 - percent) / 100;
    }
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Item {
    std::string name;
    int price_cents;
};

// Lowers every price by `percent` percent, rounding down.
void apply_discount(std::vector<Item>& items, int percent) {
    for (auto& item : items) {
        item.price_cents = item.price_cents * (100 - percent) / 100;
    }
}
--- hint
Picture step 2 of the method: print `items[0].price_cents` inside the function and `cart[0].price_cents` right after the call. They disagree. Why?
--- hint
There are two copy points, as in `reset`: the parameter `std::vector<Item> items` and the loop variable `auto item`. Each one needs an `&`.
--- hint
The parameter becomes `std::vector<Item>& items` and the loop becomes `for (auto& item : items)`. The line inside the loop stays exactly as it is.
--- check test | 10% off changes the caller's cart
[] { std::vector<Item> cart{{"tea", 1000}, {"jam", 250}}; apply_discount(cart, 10); return cart[0].price_cents == 900 && cart[1].price_cents == 225; }()
--- check test | 0% leaves prices alone
[] { std::vector<Item> cart{{"tea", 1000}}; apply_discount(cart, 0); return cart[0].price_cents == 1000; }()
--- check test | 100% makes everything free
[] { std::vector<Item> cart{{"tea", 1000}, {"jam", 250}}; apply_discount(cart, 100); return cart[0].price_cents == 0 && cart[1].price_cents == 0; }()
--- check test | An empty cart is fine
[] { std::vector<Item> cart; apply_discount(cart, 50); return cart.empty(); }()

+++ practice | Clamp every reading in place
--- task
Write `void clamp_all(std::vector<int>& readings, int low, int high)`. It changes the caller's own vector: every reading below `low` becomes `low`, and every reading above `high` becomes `high`. Readings in between stay as they are.

For `{-5, 12, 40, 99}` with `low` 0 and `high` 50, the vector becomes `{0, 12, 40, 50}`. You may assume `low` is not bigger than `high`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void clamp_all(std::vector<int>& readings, int low, int high) {
    for (int r : readings) {
        if (r < low) r = low;
        if (r > high) r = high;
    }
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void clamp_all(std::vector<int>& readings, int low, int high) {
    for (int& r : readings) {
        if (r < low) r = low;
        if (r > high) r = high;
    }
}
--- hint
The parameter is already a reference. Is there a second copy point?
--- hint
The loop variable `int r` is a copy of each reading. Make it a reference, `int& r`, so the changes land in the vector.
--- check test | Readings outside the range are pulled in
[] { std::vector<int> v{-5, 12, 40, 99}; clamp_all(v, 0, 50); return v == std::vector<int>{0, 12, 40, 50}; }()
--- check test | Readings on the edges stay
[] { std::vector<int> v{0, 50}; clamp_all(v, 0, 50); return v == std::vector<int>{0, 50}; }()
--- check test | A range of one value
[] { std::vector<int> v{3, 7, 5}; clamp_all(v, 5, 5); return v == std::vector<int>{5, 5, 5}; }()
--- check test | An empty vector is fine
[] { std::vector<int> v; clamp_all(v, 0, 10); return v.empty(); }()

+++ practice | The opposite bug: leave the caller's data alone
--- task
This time the caller wants a **new** vector and expects its own data to stay exactly as it was.

Write `std::vector<int> clamped(const std::vector<int>& readings, int low, int high)`. It returns a new vector where every reading below `low` is `low` and every reading above `high` is `high`, in the same order. The caller's `readings` must not change.

`clamped({-5, 12, 99}, 0, 50)` is `{0, 12, 50}`. The starter changes the caller's vector and returns it; the checks catch that. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> clamped(std::vector<int>& readings, int low, int high) {
    for (int& r : readings) {
        if (r < low) r = low;
        if (r > high) r = high;
    }
    return readings;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> clamped(const std::vector<int>& readings, int low, int high) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (int r : readings) {
        if (r < low) r = low;
        if (r > high) r = high;
        out.push_back(r);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
The parameter should be `const std::vector<int>&`: read, never change. Then the compiler stops any line that tries to write into it.
--- hint
Build the answer in a new vector. Here a copy is what you want: `for (int r : readings)` gives you a copy you may change, and `out.push_back(r)` keeps it.
--- check case | clamped gives the new values
clamped({-5, 12, 99}, 0, 50)
=> std::vector<int>{0, 12, 50}
--- check test | The caller's vector is untouched
[] { std::vector<int> v{-5, 12, 99}; clamped(v, 0, 50); return v == std::vector<int>{-5, 12, 99}; }()
--- check test | Works on a const vector
[] { const std::vector<int> v{7, 70}; return clamped(v, 10, 60) == std::vector<int>{10, 60}; }()
--- check case | Nothing in, nothing out
clamped({}, 0, 1)
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | Logging shifts for the crew
--- task
A `Crew` member has a `name` and the `hours` worked so far:

```cpp
struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int hours;
};
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `void log_shift(std::vector<Crew>& crew, const std::string& name, int hours)` adds `hours` to every crew member called `name`. If nobody has that name, nothing changes.
- `int total_hours(const std::vector<Crew>& crew)` returns everyone's hours added up, and `0` for an empty crew.

After `log_shift(crew, "Mae", 6)` on `{{"Mae", 10}, {"Yuri", 4}}`, Mae has 16 hours and `total_hours(crew)` is 20.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int hours;
};

void log_shift(std::vector<Crew> crew, const std::string& name, int hours) {
}

int total_hours(const std::vector<Crew>& crew) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int hours;
};

void log_shift(std::vector<Crew>& crew, const std::string& name, int hours) {
    for (auto& member : crew) {
        if (member.name == name) member.hours += hours;
    }
}

int total_hours(const std::vector<Crew>& crew) {
    int total = 0;
    for (const auto& member : crew) total += member.hours;
    return total;
}
--- hint
`log_shift` changes the caller's crew, so it needs a reference at both copy points: the parameter and the loop variable.
--- hint
`total_hours` only reads, so it loops with `for (const auto& member : crew)` and adds `member.hours` to a running total that starts at 0.
--- check test | log_shift changes the caller's crew
[] { std::vector<Crew> c{{"Mae", 10}, {"Yuri", 4}}; log_shift(c, "Mae", 6); return c[0].hours == 16 && c[1].hours == 4 && total_hours(c) == 20; }()
--- check test | Every member with the name gets the hours
[] { std::vector<Crew> c{{"Al", 1}, {"Bo", 2}, {"Al", 3}}; log_shift(c, "Al", 5); return c[0].hours == 6 && c[1].hours == 2 && c[2].hours == 8; }()
--- check test | An unknown name changes nothing
[] { std::vector<Crew> c{{"Mae", 10}}; log_shift(c, "Zed", 6); return c[0].hours == 10; }()
--- check case | An empty crew has worked 0 hours
total_hours({})
=> 0

+++ practice | Rotate the first reading to the back
--- task
Write `void rotate_left(std::vector<int>& v)`. It moves the first value to the end, and every other value one place to the front, changing the caller's vector. `{1, 2, 3, 4}` becomes `{2, 3, 4, 1}`.

A vector with one value stays the same, and an empty vector stays empty: it has no first value to move. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void rotate_left(std::vector<int> v) {
    int first = v[0];
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i + 1 < v.size(); ++i) {
        v[i] = v[i + 1];
    }
    v[v.size() - 1] = first;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void rotate_left(std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return;
    int first = v[0];
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i + 1 < v.size(); ++i) {
        v[i] = v[i + 1];
    }
    v[v.size() - 1] = first;
}
--- hint
Two things are wrong. One is a copy point: whose vector does the starter change?
--- hint
The other is the empty vector: `v[0]` does not exist there. Return straight away when `v.empty()` is true, before you read anything.
--- check test | Four values rotate
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3, 4}; rotate_left(v); return v == std::vector<int>{2, 3, 4, 1}; }()
--- check test | Two rotations of three values
[] { std::vector<int> v{7, 8, 9}; rotate_left(v); rotate_left(v); return v == std::vector<int>{9, 7, 8}; }()
--- check test | One value stays the same
[] { std::vector<int> v{5}; rotate_left(v); return v == std::vector<int>{5}; }()
--- check test | An empty vector stays empty
[] { std::vector<int> v; rotate_left(v); return v.empty(); }()

+++ practice | Debug: the counters that would not reset
--- task
**Bug report:** "After `reset_all(counts)`, every count is still the old number. No error, no warning."

`reset_all` takes a map from names to counts and should set every count to 0, keeping all the names. The parameter is already a reference. Use the method: find where the copy is, and fix the cause. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>

// Sets every count to 0, keeping the names.
void reset_all(std::map<std::string, int>& counts) {
    for (auto [name, n] : counts) {
        n = 0;
    }
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>

// Sets every count to 0, keeping the names.
void reset_all(std::map<std::string, int>& counts) {
    for (auto& [name, n] : counts) {
        n = 0;
    }
}
--- hint
Print `n` inside the loop and `counts["a"]` after the call. Inside it is 0; after, it is the old number. So `n` is not the map's own count.
--- hint
`auto [name, n]` unpacks a copy of each pair. Add one `&`, as you did for `auto& item`, so `n` names the map's own count.
--- check test | Every count becomes 0
[] { std::map<std::string, int> m{{"a", 3}, {"b", 9}}; reset_all(m); return m.size() == 2 && m["a"] == 0 && m["b"] == 0; }()
--- check test | Negative counts are reset too
[] { std::map<std::string, int> m{{"x", -4}}; reset_all(m); return m["x"] == 0; }()
--- check test | An empty map stays empty
[] { std::map<std::string, int> m; reset_all(m); return m.empty(); }()

+++ practice | Stretch: shipping from the parts store
--- task
A `Part` has a name, how many are in stock, and the stock level at which it must be reordered:

```cpp
struct Part {
    std::string name;
    int stock;
    int reorder_at;
};
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int ship(std::vector<Part>& parts, const std::map<std::string, int>& order)` takes an order, a map from part names to quantities. For each part in the order, it takes that many out of the matching part's stock. If there are not enough, it ships all there are, so stock never goes below 0. Names in the order that are not in `parts` ship nothing. It returns the total number of units shipped.
- `std::vector<std::string> to_reorder(const std::vector<Part>& parts)` returns the names of the parts whose stock is at or below their `reorder_at`, in the order they appear in `parts`.

With parts `{"bolt", 10, 3}` and `{"nut", 4, 5}`, the order `{{"bolt", 8}, {"nut", 6}}` ships 8 bolts and 4 nuts, so `ship` returns 12. Then bolt has 2 and nut has 0, and `to_reorder` is `{"bolt", "nut"}`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Part {
    std::string name;
    int stock;
    int reorder_at;
};

int ship(std::vector<Part>& parts, const std::map<std::string, int>& order) {
    return 0;
}

std::vector<std::string> to_reorder(const std::vector<Part>& parts) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Part {
    std::string name;
    int stock;
    int reorder_at;
};

int ship(std::vector<Part>& parts, const std::map<std::string, int>& order) {
    int shipped = 0;
    for (auto& part : parts) {
        for (const auto& [name, qty] : order) {
            if (part.name != name) continue;
            int n = qty;
            if (n > part.stock) n = part.stock;
            part.stock -= n;
            shipped += n;
        }
    }
    return shipped;
}

std::vector<std::string> to_reorder(const std::vector<Part>& parts) {
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (const auto& part : parts) {
        if (part.stock <= part.reorder_at) names.push_back(part.name);
    }
    return names;
}
--- hint
`ship` changes the caller's parts, so loop over them with `auto& part`. The order is only read: loop over it with `const auto& [name, qty]`, a loop inside the loop.
--- hint
When the names match, the amount to ship is the smaller of `qty` and `part.stock`. Take it off the stock and add it to your running total.
--- hint
`to_reorder` only reads: loop with `const auto&` and `push_back` each name whose `stock <= reorder_at`.
--- check test | Ship what there is, and never go below 0
[] { std::vector<Part> p{{"bolt", 10, 3}, {"nut", 4, 5}}; int n = ship(p, {{"bolt", 8}, {"nut", 6}}); return n == 12 && p[0].stock == 2 && p[1].stock == 0; }()
--- check test | Unknown names ship nothing
[] { std::vector<Part> p{{"gear", 5, 1}}; int n = ship(p, {{"cog", 3}}); return n == 0 && p[0].stock == 5; }()
--- check case | to_reorder after shipping
[] { std::vector<Part> p{{"bolt", 10, 3}, {"nut", 4, 5}, {"gear", 9, 2}}; ship(p, {{"bolt", 8}, {"nut", 6}}); return to_reorder(p); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"bolt", "nut"}
--- check case | Stock exactly at the reorder level counts
to_reorder({{"fuse", 2, 2}, {"wire", 3, 2}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"fuse"}
--- check case | An empty order ships nothing
[] { std::vector<Part> p{{"bolt", 10, 3}}; return ship(p, {}); }()
=> 0

=== cpp2-03 | std::vector and <algorithm>
--- teach
Last lesson you fixed a loop that changed copies instead of the real elements. Most loops you will ever write do one of a handful of jobs: sort, find, count, change every element, add them all up. This lesson hands you ready-made versions of those jobs.

### Loops with names

Think of a toolbox. You could file a piece of wood flat by hand every time, or you could pick up the plane that was made for it. The headers `<algorithm>` and `<numeric>` are that toolbox. They hold tested, named loops called **algorithms**: each one is a recipe of steps for one job.

Using them makes your code say *what* it does ("sort these") instead of *how* ("compare these two, swap them, move on…"). It also removes a whole class of [[off-by-one mistakes|off-by-one]], because you never write the counting yourself.

### A range: begin() and end()

Every algorithm works on a **range**: a `begin()` and an `end()` iterator. You met the pair in the const correctness lesson, with `std::min_element`. `v.begin()` marks the first element and `v.end()` marks the spot one past the last, so together they mean "the whole vector". The next lesson looks at iterators closely.

### Sorting

`std::sort` puts the elements of a range in order, smallest first. It [[changes the vector in place|how-sort-works]]:

```cpp
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> v = {5, 1, 4, 2};
std::sort(v.begin(), v.end());   // v is now {1, 2, 4, 5}
```

To put the largest first, hand `sort` a third thing: the rule for comparing. `std::greater<int>()`, from the header `<functional>`, is a [[ready-made "is a greater than b?"|function-objects]], so sorting with it puts the largest first:

```cpp
#include <functional>

std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), std::greater<int>());   // v is now {5, 4, 2, 1}
```

### Adding them all up

`std::accumulate`, from `<numeric>`, adds every element of a range to a starting value, and gives back the total:

```cpp
#include <numeric>

long long total = std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0LL);   // 12
```

The third argument, `0LL`, is the starting value: zero, written as a `long long`. A **long long** is a whole-number type that is wider than `int`. It takes 8 bytes and reaches about 9.2 quintillion. The letters `LL` on the end of a number make it a `long long`.

**Watch out:** `accumulate` adds in the type of its **starting value**. `0` means `int`, which [[overflows past about 2 billion|overflow]]; `0LL` means `long long`. Add up two readings of 2,000,000,000 starting from `0` and the answer is garbage. Start from `0LL` and it is 4,000,000,000.

### Small rules: lambdas

The next algorithms each take a small rule, like "is this number over 3?". You write the rule as a **lambda**: a small unnamed function written right where you need it. You have been reading lambdas in the check lines since the references lesson.

```cpp
[](int x) { return x > 3; }
```

Read it as "given an int `x`, return whether `x` is greater than 3". The `[]` starts it, `(int x)` is its parameter, and the body in `{ }` works out the answer. Lambdas get their own lesson later in this course; for now read each one as "given x, return this".

### Finding the first match

`std::find_if` walks the range and gives back an iterator to the first element for which the rule says `true`:

```cpp
auto it = std::find_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [](int x) { return x > 3; });
if (it != v.end()) {
    // *it is the first element over 3
}
```

**Watch out:** when nothing matches, `find_if` returns `end()`, the stopping mark, which is not an element. Always compare with `v.end()` before you use `*it`.

### Counting matches

`std::count_if` counts the elements for which the rule says `true`. Here the rule uses `%` (remainder): `x % 2 == 0` means "x is even".

```cpp
auto n = std::count_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [](int x) { return x % 2 == 0; });   // 2
```

In `{5, 4, 2, 1}`, the even ones are 4 and 2, so `n` is 2. The count comes back as a wide signed whole-number type, so to return it from an `int` function, convert it: `static_cast<int>(n)`.

### Using a variable from outside

A lambda can only see its own parameters, unless you name an outside variable in its square brackets. This is called **capturing** the variable:

```cpp
int bar = 2;
auto m = std::count_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [bar](int x) { return x >= bar; });   // 3
```

`[bar]` lets the lambda read `bar`. In `{5, 4, 2, 1}`, three values are at least 2. Leave out the `bar` in the brackets and clang stops with `variable 'bar' cannot be implicitly captured`.

### Changing every element into a new vector

`std::transform` reads each element, runs the rule on it, and writes the result somewhere else:

```cpp
std::vector<int> out(v.size());
std::transform(v.begin(), v.end(), out.begin(), [](int x) { return x * 10; });
// out is {50, 40, 20, 10}
```

The third argument, `out.begin()`, is where the writing starts. `transform` does not make room; it writes over elements that must already exist. So first make `out` the right size: `std::vector<int> out(v.size());` makes a vector holding `v.size()` zeros. [[Round brackets here, not curly ones|round-vs-curly]].

### A short if/else that gives a value

One more piece of syntax that the solutions use from here on is the **conditional operator**: `condition ? a : b`. Read the `?` as "if so" and the `:` as "otherwise". It gives `a` when the condition is true and `b` when it is false:

```cpp
int fuel = 12;
std::string status = fuel < 10 ? "low" : "ok";   // "ok"
```

So `return it == v.end() ? -1 : *it;` is a short if/else that produces a value: `-1` when nothing was found, the element otherwise.

::: context off-by-one Where off-by-one mistakes hide
A hand-written loop has three places to slip: where it starts, where it stops, and how it steps. Use `<=` where you meant `<` and the loop reads one element past the end of the vector. An algorithm takes the whole range as `begin()` and `end()`, so there is no counter to get wrong. The debugging lesson "Debugging: off by one, out of range", later in this course, is all about the ones that still get through.
:::

::: context how-sort-works How fast is std::sort?
Sorting by comparing neighbors over and over is slow on long lists: roughly n × n comparisons for n elements. Since C++11, `std::sort` must finish in about n × log₂(n) comparisons. For a million readings that is about 20 million comparisons instead of a trillion. Library writers usually build it as *introsort*, a mix of quicksort, heapsort and insertion sort. Equal elements may come out in any order; `std::stable_sort` keeps them in their original order when that matters.
:::

::: context function-objects Rules you can hand over
`std::greater<int>` is a type whose objects can be called like a function: `std::greater<int>()(5, 2)` is `true`, because 5 is greater than 2. The `()` after `std::greater<int>` makes one such object, and `sort` calls it every time it needs to compare two elements. An object that acts like a function is called a *function object*. A lambda is a quick way to write one yourself, which is why the two can be used in the same places.
:::

::: context overflow Overflow, and a rocket lost to it
An `int` holds at most 2,147,483,647. Going past that in a signed type is *overflow*, and in C++ it is undefined behavior: the rules say nothing about the result. A `long long` reaches 9,223,372,036,854,775,807. On 4 June 1996, the first Ariane 5 rocket veered off course about 37 seconds after lift-off and destroyed itself. Its guidance software converted a 64-bit floating-point number (a decimal, like a `double`) into a 16-bit whole number, the value was too big to fit, and the error shut the guidance system down. The code had come from Ariane 4, whose slower flight never produced such a big number.
:::

::: context round-vs-curly Round brackets or curly braces
For a vector of ints, the kind of bracket changes the meaning. `std::vector<int> out(4)` means "make 4 elements", all zero. `std::vector<int> out{4}` means "make a vector holding the one value 4". `transform` needs the first kind: slots that already exist, ready to be written over.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 130" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="15" y="32" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">out(4)</text>
  <rect x="90" y="15" width="40" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="130" y="15" width="40" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="170" y="15" width="40" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="210" y="15" width="40" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <g font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="110" y="34">0</text><text x="150" y="34">0</text><text x="190" y="34">0</text><text x="230" y="34">0</text>
  </g>
  <text x="262" y="34" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">4 elements</text>
  <text x="15" y="87" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">out{4}</text>
  <rect x="90" y="70" width="40" height="28" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="110" y="89" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4</text>
  <text x="142" y="89" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">1 element, holding 4</text>
  <text x="15" y="122" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">transform writes over existing slots, so use round brackets</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write these five functions **without writing any loops**: use the algorithms from the explanation. The checks look for `for (` and `while (` in your code, so a hand-written loop will not pass. No `main`.

- `std::vector<int> sorted_descending(std::vector<int> v)` — the values, largest first.
- `int count_at_least(const std::vector<int>& v, int bar)` — how many values are `>= bar` (at least `bar`).
- `long long sum_of(const std::vector<int>& v)` — the total, without overflowing.
- `std::vector<int> doubled(const std::vector<int>& v)` — each value times two, in order.
- `int first_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit)` — the first value `> limit`, or `-1` if there is none.

The starter includes `<algorithm>` and `<numeric>`. Add `#include <functional>` for `std::greater`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> sorted_descending(std::vector<int> v) {
    return v;
}

int count_at_least(const std::vector<int>& v, int bar) {
    return 0;
}

long long sum_of(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return 0;
}

std::vector<int> doubled(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return v;
}

int first_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> sorted_descending(std::vector<int> v) {
    std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), std::greater<int>());
    return v;
}

int count_at_least(const std::vector<int>& v, int bar) {
    return static_cast<int>(std::count_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [bar](int x) { return x >= bar; }));
}

long long sum_of(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0LL);
}

std::vector<int> doubled(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::vector<int> out(v.size());
    std::transform(v.begin(), v.end(), out.begin(), [](int x) { return x * 2; });
    return out;
}

int first_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    auto it = std::find_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [limit](int x) { return x > limit; });
    return it == v.end() ? -1 : *it;
}
--- hint
Each function is one algorithm: `std::sort` with `std::greater<int>()`, `std::count_if`, `std::accumulate`, `std::transform` and `std::find_if`. `sorted_descending` takes its vector by value on purpose: it gets its own copy, which it may sort and return.
--- hint
A lambda can use a parameter if you name it in the brackets: `[bar](int x) { return x >= bar; }`. Wrap the count in `static_cast<int>( … )`. For the sum, start from `0LL`.
--- hint
`transform` needs somewhere to write: create `std::vector<int> out(v.size());` first, then return `out`. For `first_over`, keep what `find_if` gives back in `auto it`, then `return it == v.end() ? -1 : *it;`.
--- check case | sorted_descending({3, 1, 2})
sorted_descending({3, 1, 2})
=> std::vector<int>{3, 2, 1}
--- check case | sorted_descending of nothing is nothing
sorted_descending({})
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | count_at_least counts the boundary too
count_at_least({50, 70, 69, 90, 70}, 70)
=> 3
--- check case | sum_of does not overflow
sum_of({2000000000, 2000000000, 5})
=> 4000000005LL
--- check case | doubled keeps the order
doubled({4, -1, 0})
=> std::vector<int>{8, -2, 0}
--- check case | first_over finds the first match
first_over({3, 9, 4, 12}, 5)
=> 9
--- check case | first_over with no match is -1
first_over({1, 2, 3}, 10)
=> -1
--- check source absent | No hand-written loops
\b(for|while)\s*\(

+++ practice | Squares and multiples, with no loops
--- task
Write two functions **without any loops**: use the algorithms from the lesson. No `main`.

- `std::vector<int> squares(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns each value times itself, in the same order. `squares({-3, 0, 2})` is `{9, 0, 4}`.
- `int count_multiples(const std::vector<int>& v, int k)` returns how many values divide exactly by `k`, meaning the remainder `x % k` is 0. `count_multiples({3, 6, 7, 9, 10}, 3)` is 3. Zero divides exactly by anything, so it counts. You may assume `k` is not 0.

The checks look for `for (` and `while (` in your code.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> squares(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return v;
}

int count_multiples(const std::vector<int>& v, int k) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> squares(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::vector<int> out(v.size());
    std::transform(v.begin(), v.end(), out.begin(), [](int x) { return x * x; });
    return out;
}

int count_multiples(const std::vector<int>& v, int k) {
    return static_cast<int>(std::count_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [k](int x) { return x % k == 0; }));
}
--- hint
`squares` is a `std::transform` into a vector that already has room: make it with round brackets and `v.size()`.
--- hint
`count_multiples` is a `std::count_if` whose lambda needs `k`, so name `k` in the square brackets. Wrap the count in `static_cast<int>( … )`.
--- check case | squares keeps the order

=> std::vector<int>{9, 0, 4}
--- check case | squares of nothing

=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | count_multiples counts exact multiples
count_multiples({3, 6, 7, 9, 10}, 3)
=> 3
--- check case | Zero and negatives are multiples too

=> 3
