+++ practice | Sorting readings from the input
--- stdin
14 -2 40 7 7 3
--- task
The Input tab holds some whole numbers separated by spaces. Read them all into a vector (a `while (std::cin >> x)` loop is the right tool for that part). Then print exactly three lines:

```
sorted: 40 14 7 7 3 -2
total: 69
over 5: 4
```

- `sorted:` then the values largest first, each after one space.
- `total:` then their sum.
- `over 5:` then how many values are greater than 5.

Use `std::sort`, `std::accumulate` and `std::count_if` for the three jobs. Work the numbers out; do not type them.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <iostream>
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

int main() {
    std::vector<int> v;
    int x;
    while (std::cin >> x) v.push_back(x);
    std::cout << "sorted:\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <functional>
#include <iostream>
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

int main() {
    std::vector<int> v;
    int x;
    while (std::cin >> x) v.push_back(x);

    std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), std::greater<int>());
    std::cout << "sorted:";
    for (int n : v) std::cout << " " << n;
    std::cout << "\n";

    long long total = std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0LL);
    std::cout << "total: " << total << "\n";

    auto over = std::count_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [](int n) { return n > 5; });
    std::cout << "over 5: " << over << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Sort with `std::greater<int>()` as the third argument to put the largest first. Then print `"sorted:"` and, for each value, a space followed by the value.
--- hint
The total is `std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0LL)`, and the count is `std::count_if` with the lambda `[](int n) { return n > 5; }`.
--- check output | Prints the three lines
sorted: 40 14 7 7 3 -2
total: 69
over 5: 4
--- check source | Sorts with std::sort
std::sort\s*\(
--- check source | Counts with std::count_if
std::count_if\s*\(

+++ practice | Engine burns that last long enough
--- task
A `Burn` is one firing of an engine, with a name and how many seconds it lasted:

```cpp
struct Burn {
    std::string name;
    int seconds;
};
```

Write two functions with `std::count_if` and `std::find_if`, and no loops. No `main`.

- `int count_long(const std::vector<Burn>& burns, int min_seconds)` returns how many burns lasted at least `min_seconds`.
- `std::string first_long(const std::vector<Burn>& burns, int min_seconds)` returns the name of the first burn that lasted at least `min_seconds`, or `""` if there is none.

For `{{"trim", 4}, {"orbit", 62}, {"deorbit", 30}}` and 30, `count_long` is 2 and `first_long` is `"orbit"`. A lambda can take a `const Burn& b` just as it takes an `int x`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Burn {
    std::string name;
    int seconds;
};

int count_long(const std::vector<Burn>& burns, int min_seconds) {
    return 0;
}

std::string first_long(const std::vector<Burn>& burns, int min_seconds) {
    return burns.empty() ? "" : burns[0].name;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Burn {
    std::string name;
    int seconds;
};

int count_long(const std::vector<Burn>& burns, int min_seconds) {
    return static_cast<int>(std::count_if(burns.begin(), burns.end(),
                                          [min_seconds](const Burn& b) { return b.seconds >= min_seconds; }));
}

std::string first_long(const std::vector<Burn>& burns, int min_seconds) {
    auto it = std::find_if(burns.begin(), burns.end(),
                           [min_seconds](const Burn& b) { return b.seconds >= min_seconds; });
    return it == burns.end() ? "" : (*it).name;
}
--- hint
Both lambdas are the same rule: `[min_seconds](const Burn& b) { return b.seconds >= min_seconds; }`.
--- hint
`find_if` gives back an iterator. Compare it with `burns.end()` before using it. `(*it)` is the burn it points at, so `(*it).name` is its name.
--- check case | count_long counts the boundary too
count_long({{"trim", 4}, {"orbit", 62}, {"deorbit", 30}}, 30)
=> 2
--- check case | first_long finds the first long burn
first_long({{"trim", 4}, {"orbit", 62}, {"deorbit", 30}}, 30)
=> "orbit"
--- check case | No burn long enough
first_long({{"trim", 4}, {"nudge", 2}}, 10)
=> ""
--- check case | No burns at all
count_long({}, 1)
=> 0
--- check source absent | No hand-written loops
\b(for|while)\s*\(

+++ practice | The median, even when it is awkward
--- task
The **median** of some numbers is the middle one once they are sorted. Write `int median_of(std::vector<int> v)`. No `main`.

- With an odd count, it is the middle value: `median_of({7, -2, 5})` is 5.
- With an even count, there are two middle values. Add them and divide by 2 with whole-number division, as C++ does: `median_of({8, 1, 4, 3})` is (3 + 4) / 2, which is 3. And `median_of({-3, 0})` is -3 / 2, which is -1, because C++ cuts toward zero.
- With no values, return `0`.

The vector is taken by value, so you may sort your own copy.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

int median_of(std::vector<int> v) {
    return v[v.size() / 2];
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

int median_of(std::vector<int> v) {
    if (v.empty()) return 0;
    std::sort(v.begin(), v.end());
    std::size_t mid = v.size() / 2;
    if (v.size() % 2 == 1) return v[mid];
    return (v[mid - 1] + v[mid]) / 2;
}
--- hint
Sort first. Then, for a size `n`, the middle index is `n / 2`. For an odd `n` that is the median. For an even `n` the two middle values sit at `n / 2 - 1` and `n / 2`.
--- hint
Check `v.empty()` before anything else: with no values there is no middle index at all.
--- check case | Odd count, unsorted
median_of({7, -2, 5})
=> 5
--- check case | Even count averages the two middle values
median_of({8, 1, 4, 3})
=> 3
--- check case | Even count with a negative total cuts toward zero
median_of({-3, 0})
=> -1
--- check case | Duplicates
median_of({2, 9, 2, 2})
=> 2
--- check case | One value
median_of({42})
=> 42
--- check case | No values
median_of({})
=> 0

+++ practice | Debug: the average that lost its halves
--- task
**Bug report:** "`mean_of({0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 1.5})` returns 0.25. It should be 0.75."

`mean_of` should return the average of some decimal readings: their sum divided by how many there are, and `0.0` for an empty list. Find the cause and fix it; it is one line. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

double mean_of(const std::vector<double>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return 0.0;
    double total = std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0);
    return total / v.size();
}
--- solution
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

double mean_of(const std::vector<double>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return 0.0;
    double total = std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0.0);
    return total / v.size();
}
--- hint
`accumulate` adds in the type of its starting value. What type is the `0` it starts from?
--- hint
`0` is an `int`, so every step is cut back to a whole number: 0 + 0.5 becomes 0. Start from a decimal zero, `0.0`, which is a `double`.
--- check test | Halves are kept
std::abs(mean_of({0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 1.5}) - 0.75) < 1e-9
--- check test | Negative decimals
std::abs(mean_of({-1.25, 2.5}) - 0.625) < 1e-9
--- check test | One reading is its own mean
std::abs(mean_of({3.7}) - 3.7) < 1e-9
--- check test | No readings give 0.0
mean_of({}) == 0.0

+++ practice | Stretch: curving the scores
--- task
A teacher curves a test: every score goes up (or down) by the same amount, so that the highest score becomes exactly 100. For `{50, 80, 72}` the highest is 80, so everyone gets 20 more: `{70, 100, 92}`. For `{120, 90}` the highest is 120, so everyone gets 20 less: `{100, 70}`.

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::vector<int> curve(const std::vector<int>& scores)` returns the curved scores, in the same order. An empty list gives an empty list.
- `int passing_after_curve(const std::vector<int>& scores, int pass_mark)` returns how many curved scores are at least `pass_mark`.

Use `std::max_element`, `std::transform` and `std::count_if`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> curve(const std::vector<int>& scores) {
    std::vector<int> out(scores.size());
    int shift = 100 - *std::max_element(scores.begin(), scores.end());
    std::transform(scores.begin(), scores.end(), out.begin(), [](int s) { return s + 20; });
    return out;
}

int passing_after_curve(const std::vector<int>& scores, int pass_mark) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> curve(const std::vector<int>& scores) {
    if (scores.empty()) return {};
    int shift = 100 - *std::max_element(scores.begin(), scores.end());
    std::vector<int> out(scores.size());
    std::transform(scores.begin(), scores.end(), out.begin(), [shift](int s) { return s + shift; });
    return out;
}

int passing_after_curve(const std::vector<int>& scores, int pass_mark) {
    std::vector<int> curved = curve(scores);
    return static_cast<int>(std::count_if(curved.begin(), curved.end(),
                                          [pass_mark](int s) { return s >= pass_mark; }));
}
--- hint
Work out the shift once: `100` minus the highest score. The lambda in `transform` then needs `shift` in its square brackets.
--- hint
`*std::max_element(...)` on an empty vector follows `end()`, which is undefined behavior. Return an empty vector first when `scores.empty()`.
--- hint
`passing_after_curve` can call `curve(scores)`, keep the result in a vector, and `count_if` over that with a lambda that captures `pass_mark`.
--- check case | Scores go up to reach 100
curve({50, 80, 72})
=> std::vector<int>{70, 100, 92}
--- check case | Scores go down when the top is over 100
curve({120, 90})
=> std::vector<int>{100, 70}
--- check case | Already curved
curve({100, 40})
=> std::vector<int>{100, 40}
--- check case | No scores
curve({})
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | Passing counts the boundary
passing_after_curve({50, 80, 72, 30}, 70)
=> 3
--- check case | Nobody to pass
passing_after_curve({}, 50)
=> 0

=== cpp2-04 | Iterators and range-for
--- teach
Last lesson every algorithm took `v.begin()` and `v.end()`, and `std::find_if` handed back an `it` that you compared with `v.end()` before using `*it`. This lesson opens those up: what an iterator is, and how the range-for loop you have used since the vector lesson is built out of them.

### A bookmark in a container

Picture a bookmark in a book. It is not a page; it marks one. You can read the page it marks, and you can move it forward one page at a time.

An **iterator** is a bookmark for a container: a [[position in it|why-iterators]]. It behaves like a pointer from the pointers lesson:

- `*it` is the element at that position. Read it "the thing it points at".
- `++it` moves it to the next element. Read it "step it forward", like `i++` in a counting loop.

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {7, 3, 9};
auto it = v.begin();        // it marks the 7
++it;                       // now it marks the 3
std::cout << *it << "\n";   // prints 3
```

`auto` saves you writing the real type, which is `std::vector<int>::iterator`: "the iterator type from vector of int" (`::` is the scope operator, read "from").

### begin() and end()

Every container gives you two iterators:

- `begin()` — the first element.
- `end()` — one **past** the last. It is a stopping mark, never an element: do not `*` it.

### The half-open range

Maths has a short way to write "from here, up to but not including there": `[begin, end)`. The square bracket means "this one is included"; the round bracket means "stop right before this one". A range like this is called a **[[half-open range|half-open]]**.

That shape is why an empty container has `begin() == end()`: with no elements, the first position and the stopping mark are the same place. It is also why loops read "while not at the end":

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {7, 3, 9};
for (auto it = v.begin(); it != v.end(); ++it) {
    std::cout << *it << "\n";
}
```

This is the counting `for` loop from the loops lesson, with an iterator in place of the counting number. It starts at `begin()`, keeps going while `it != v.end()` (`!=` is "is not equal to"), and steps with `++it`. It prints 7, 3 and 9, one per line.

### Range-for is that loop, written for you

The **range-for** loop is exactly that loop, [[written for you by the compiler|range-for-inside]]. Each time around, the loop variable is set from `*it`. So the way you declare the loop variable decides whether you get a copy:

```cpp
for (int x : v)               // x is a copy of each element
for (int& x : v)              // x IS each element: changes stick
for (const auto& s : names)   // no copy, read-only: the default for strings and bigger things
```

Changing the elements through `int& x` is fine. Adding or removing elements while you loop is [[not fine at all|invalidation]].

### From an iterator back to an index

Algorithms give back iterators, but sometimes you want the position as a number, an index. `std::distance(a, b)`, from the header `<iterator>`, counts how many steps it takes to get from `a` to `b`:

```cpp
auto it = std::max_element(v.begin(), v.end());   // points at 9
auto index = std::distance(v.begin(), it);        // 2
```

If the largest value appears more than once, `std::max_element` points at the *first* one. Like `count_if`'s count, the distance comes back as a wide signed whole-number type, so convert it with `static_cast<int>` to return it from an `int` function.

### Walking backwards

`rbegin()` and `rend()` walk backwards (the `r` is for reverse). `rbegin()` marks the last element, `++` moves toward the front, and `rend()` is the stopping mark right before the first. A `std::string` is a container of characters, so it has all four:

```cpp
std::string s = "abc";
for (auto it = s.rbegin(); it != s.rend(); ++it) {
    std::cout << *it;   // prints cba
}
```

### The same both ways?

`std::equal(a, b, c)`, from `<algorithm>`, compares the range from `a` to `b`, element by element, with the range that starts at `c`. It gives `true` only if every pair matches.

That makes "is it the same both ways?" a one-liner. `std::equal(s.begin(), s.end(), s.rbegin())` [[compares the start going forwards with the end going backwards|palindrome-pairs]]. It compares characters exactly, so `'L'` and `'l'` count as different.

### Is it empty?

`v.empty()` is `true` when a container has no elements. It means the same as `v.size() == 0`, but says what you mean. Strings have it too: `w.empty()` is `true` for `""`. Put `!` ("not", from the decisions lesson) in front to ask the opposite:

```cpp
std::string w = "ada";
if (!w.empty()) {
    // w has at least one character, so w[0] is safe
}
```

**Watch out:** `end()` is not an element, so check before you follow an iterator that might be `end()`. On an empty vector, `std::max_element` gives back `end()`, and putting `*` in front of that is undefined behavior. The same goes for `w[0]` on an empty string: there is no first letter there that you may change. Check `v.empty()` or `w.empty()` first.

::: context why-iterators One idea for every container
A vector keeps its elements in one row, but a `std::map` usually keeps its keys in a tree, and other containers link elements together in chains. Each kind of container supplies its own iterators, and they all answer to the same `*`, `++`, `==` and `!=`. So one `std::find_if` or `std::count_if` works on all of them. This design is the heart of the Standard Template Library (STL), created by Alexander Stepanov and Meng Lee and voted into the draft C++ standard in 1994.
:::

::: context half-open The stopping mark sits past the end
For `{7, 3, 9}`, `begin()` marks the 7 and `end()` marks the empty spot after the 9. For a vector, the number of elements is always `end() - begin()`, here 3, with no plus or minus one to remember. For an empty vector, both marks sit at the same spot.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="40" y="30" width="60" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="100" y="30" width="60" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="160" y="30" width="60" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="220" y="30" width="60" height="40" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <g font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="70" y="55">7</text><text x="130" y="55">3</text><text x="190" y="55">9</text>
  </g>
  <text x="250" y="55" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">no element</text>
  <line x1="70" y1="105" x2="70" y2="76" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="70,72 65,82 75,82" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="70" y="122" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">begin()</text>
  <line x1="250" y1="105" x2="250" y2="76" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="250,72 245,82 255,82" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="250" y="122" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">end()</text>
  <text x="160" y="145" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">[begin, end): 7 and 3 and 9, then stop</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context range-for-inside What the compiler writes for you
The range-for loop arrived in C++11. For `for (int x : v) { … }`, the compiler writes roughly this: take `v.begin()` and `v.end()`, then loop `for (auto it = v.begin(); it != v.end(); ++it) { int x = *it; … }`. Seen that way, it is clear why `int x` is a copy (it is a new `int` filled from `*it`) and why `int& x` is not (it is another name for `*it`). Anything with `begin()` and `end()` works in a range-for: vectors, strings, maps and sets.
:::

::: context invalidation Why you must not grow a vector while looping over it
When a vector runs out of room, `push_back` moves every element to a bigger block of memory. Every iterator into the old block, including the hidden one inside a range-for, now marks memory that is no longer the vector's. Using it is undefined behavior. This is called *iterator invalidation*. The safe habit: while looping over a container, change its elements, but do not add or remove any. Build a new vector instead, or collect the changes and make them after the loop.
:::

::: context palindrome-pairs Forwards and backwards at once
`std::equal` moves two iterators together: one from `s.begin()` going forwards, one from `s.rbegin()` going backwards. For `"level"` it compares l with l, e with e, v with v, e with e, and l with l, all equal, so the answer is `true`. For `"ab"` the very first pair, a and b, differ, so it is `false`. An empty string has no pairs to disagree, so it counts as a palindrome.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="15" y="42" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">begin() →</text>
  <text x="15" y="112" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">rbegin() →</text>
  <g font-size="16" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="120" y="42">l</text><text x="160" y="42">e</text><text x="200" y="42">v</text><text x="240" y="42">e</text><text x="280" y="42">l</text>
    <text x="120" y="112">l</text><text x="160" y="112">e</text><text x="200" y="112">v</text><text x="240" y="112">e</text><text x="280" y="112">l</text>
  </g>
  <g stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2">
    <line x1="120" y1="52" x2="120" y2="94"/><line x1="160" y1="52" x2="160" y2="94"/><line x1="200" y1="52" x2="200" y2="94"/>
    <line x1="240" y1="52" x2="240" y2="94"/><line x1="280" y1="52" x2="280" y2="94"/>
  </g>
  <text x="200" y="18" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">s = "level" read forwards</text>
  <text x="200" y="138" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">the same s read backwards: every pair matches</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`. The starter already includes `<algorithm>`, `<cctype>`, `<iterator>`, `<string>` and `<vector>`.

- `int index_of_max(const std::vector<int>& v)` — the position of the largest value (the first one if it appears more than once), or `-1` for an empty vector.
- `bool is_palindrome(const std::string& s)` — `true` if `s` reads the same backwards, as `"level"`, `""` and `"a"` do. Compare characters exactly, so `"Level"` is not a palindrome.
- `void capitalize_all(std::vector<std::string>& words)` — uppercase the first letter of every non-empty word, **in place**, so the caller's own words change. `{"ada", "", "grace"}` becomes `{"Ada", "", "Grace"}`.

The starter's `capitalize_all` already has the right uppercasing line, the one from the string lesson, but its loop has a bug, and it forgets about empty words.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cctype>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int index_of_max(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return 0;
}

bool is_palindrome(const std::string& s) {
    return false;
}

void capitalize_all(std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    for (auto w : words) {
        w[0] = static_cast<char>(std::toupper(static_cast<unsigned char>(w[0])));
    }
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cctype>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int index_of_max(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return -1;
    auto it = std::max_element(v.begin(), v.end());
    return static_cast<int>(std::distance(v.begin(), it));
}

bool is_palindrome(const std::string& s) {
    return std::equal(s.begin(), s.end(), s.rbegin());
}

void capitalize_all(std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    for (auto& w : words) {
        if (!w.empty()) {
            w[0] = static_cast<char>(std::toupper(static_cast<unsigned char>(w[0])));
        }
    }
}
--- hint
`std::max_element` already points at the *first* largest value; `std::distance(v.begin(), it)` turns that into an index. Check `v.empty()` before anything else.
--- hint
`std::equal(s.begin(), s.end(), s.rbegin())` walks forwards through `s` and backwards at the same time, so it is the whole body of `is_palindrome`.
--- hint
In `capitalize_all`, the loop variable must be a reference, `for (auto& w : words)`, and `w[0]` on an empty string is not a letter you may change: wrap the uppercasing line in `if (!w.empty()) { … }` (`!` is "not").
--- check case | index_of_max finds the first largest
index_of_max({4, 9, 2, 9})
=> 1
--- check case | index_of_max of an empty vector is -1
index_of_max({})
=> -1
--- check test | Palindromes and not
is_palindrome("level") && is_palindrome("") && is_palindrome("a") && !is_palindrome("ab") && !is_palindrome("Level")
--- check test | capitalize_all changes the words in place
[] { std::vector<std::string> w{"ada", "", "grace", "Linus"}; capitalize_all(w); return w == std::vector<std::string>{"Ada", "", "Grace", "Linus"}; }()

+++ practice | Backwards text and the first negative
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::string reversed(const std::string& s)` returns `s` backwards: `reversed("orbit")` is `"tibro"`, and `reversed("")` is `""`. Walk `s` with `rbegin()` and `rend()` and add each character to a new string with `+=`.
- `int position_of_first_negative(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns the index of the first value below 0, or `-1` if there is none. `position_of_first_negative({4, 0, -2, -9})` is 2. Use `std::find_if`, then `std::distance`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string reversed(const std::string& s) {
    return s;
}

int position_of_first_negative(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string reversed(const std::string& s) {
    std::string out;
    for (auto it = s.rbegin(); it != s.rend(); ++it) {
        out += *it;
    }
    return out;
}

int position_of_first_negative(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    auto it = std::find_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [](int x) { return x < 0; });
    if (it == v.end()) return -1;
    return static_cast<int>(std::distance(v.begin(), it));
}
--- hint
Start with an empty `std::string out;` and loop an iterator from `s.rbegin()` while it is not `s.rend()`, doing `out += *it;` each time.
--- hint
`find_if` gives back `v.end()` when nothing matches: return `-1` then. Otherwise `std::distance(v.begin(), it)` is the index; convert it with `static_cast<int>`.
--- check case | reversed of a word
reversed("orbit")
=> "tibro"
--- check case | reversed of nothing
reversed("")
=> ""
--- check case | The first negative, not the last
position_of_first_negative({4, 0, -2, -9})
=> 2
--- check case | A negative at index 0
position_of_first_negative({-1, 5})
=> 0
--- check case | No negatives
position_of_first_negative({0, 3})
=> -1

+++ practice | Is the list its own mirror?
--- task
Write two functions about lists that read the same from both ends. No `main`.

- `bool is_mirror(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns `true` when `v` is the same backwards, as `{1, 2, 1}` and `{}` are. Use `std::equal` with `rbegin()`.
- `int mirror_breaks_at(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns the first index `i` where `v[i]` differs from its partner at the other end: the partner of index 0 is the last value, the partner of index 1 is the one before the last, and so on. If `v` is a mirror, it returns `-1`. For `{3, 5, 8, 3}`, index 0 matches (3 and 3), but index 1 does not (5 and 8), so the answer is 1.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

bool is_mirror(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return v.empty();
}

int mirror_breaks_at(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

bool is_mirror(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return std::equal(v.begin(), v.end(), v.rbegin());
}

int mirror_breaks_at(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    int n = static_cast<int>(v.size());
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        if (v[i] != v[n - 1 - i]) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}
--- hint
`std::equal(v.begin(), v.end(), v.rbegin())` compares the list forwards with the same list backwards.
--- hint
The partner of index `i` in a list of size `n` is index `n - 1 - i`: for `i` of 0 that is the last one. Loop `i` from 0 and return the first `i` where the two differ.
--- check test | Mirrors and not
is_mirror({1, 2, 1}) && is_mirror({}) && is_mirror({7}) && is_mirror({4, 4}) && !is_mirror({1, 2})
--- check case | The first mismatch
mirror_breaks_at({3, 5, 8, 3})
=> 1
--- check case | A mismatch right at the ends
mirror_breaks_at({1, 2, 2, 9})
=> 0
--- check case | A mirror has no break
mirror_breaks_at({6, 1, 6})
=> -1
--- check case | An empty list has no break
mirror_breaks_at({})
=> -1

+++ practice | Searching from the back
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int last_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit)` returns the **last** value greater than `limit`, or `-1` if there is none. `last_over({9, 2, 14, 6}, 5)` is 6. Use `std::find_if` on `rbegin()` and `rend()`: it walks from the back, so the first match it finds is the last one.
- `std::vector<int> positions_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit)` returns the index of every value greater than `limit`, in order. `positions_over({9, 2, 14, 6}, 5)` is `{0, 2, 3}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

int last_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    auto it = std::find_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [limit](int x) { return x > limit; });
    return it == v.end() ? -1 : *it;
}

std::vector<int> positions_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

int last_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    auto it = std::find_if(v.rbegin(), v.rend(), [limit](int x) { return x > limit; });
    return it == v.rend() ? -1 : *it;
}

std::vector<int> positions_over(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (int i = 0; i < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++i) {
        if (v[i] > limit) out.push_back(i);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Hand `find_if` the reverse range, `v.rbegin()` and `v.rend()`. Then the "nothing found" mark is `v.rend()`, not `v.end()`.
--- hint
For `positions_over`, loop an `int i` over the indexes and `push_back(i)` whenever `v[i] > limit`.
--- check case | last_over walks from the back
last_over({9, 2, 14, 6}, 5)
=> 6
--- check case | The only match is at the front
last_over({9, 2, 1}, 5)
=> 9
--- check case | No match
last_over({1, 2}, 5)
=> -1
--- check case | positions_over lists every index
positions_over({9, 2, 14, 6}, 5)
=> std::vector<int>{0, 2, 3}
--- check case | positions_over of nothing
positions_over({}, 0)
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | The last of the largest
--- task
Write `int index_of_last_max(const std::vector<int>& v)`. It returns the index of the largest value; if the largest value appears more than once, the index of the **last** one. For an empty vector it returns `-1`. No `main`.

`index_of_last_max({4, 9, 2, 9, 1})` is 3. Watch the lists where the easy answers break: all values the same, all values negative, and a single value.
--- starter
#include <vector>

int index_of_last_max(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    int best = 0;
    int best_index = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++i) {
        if (v[i] > best) {
            best = v[i];
            best_index = i;
        }
    }
    return best_index;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

int index_of_last_max(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return -1;
    int best_index = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++i) {
        if (v[i] >= v[best_index]) best_index = i;
    }
    return best_index;
}
--- hint
Starting the best value at 0 breaks when every value is negative. Start from index 0's own value instead, after checking `v.empty()`.
--- hint
`>` keeps the first of equal values; `>=` moves on to each later equal one, so it ends on the last.
--- check case | The last of two maxima
index_of_last_max({4, 9, 2, 9, 1})
=> 3
--- check case | All the same
index_of_last_max({5, 5, 5})
=> 2
--- check case | All negative
index_of_last_max({-8, -3, -5})
=> 1
--- check case | One value
index_of_last_max({-4})
=> 0
--- check case | Empty
index_of_last_max({})
=> -1

+++ practice | Debug: initials with a strange character
--- task
**Bug report:** "`initials({"ada", "", "lin"})` should be `"al"`, but it comes out three characters long, with an invisible character in the middle."

`initials` should return the first letter of every name, in order, skipping names that are empty. Find the missed edge case and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// The first letter of every non-empty name, in order.
std::string initials(const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& name : names) {
        out += name[0];
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// The first letter of every non-empty name, in order.
std::string initials(const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    std::string out;
    for (const auto& name : names) {
        if (!name.empty()) out += name[0];
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Reproduce it small: what is `name[0]` when `name` is `""`? There is no first letter there.
--- hint
Only add `name[0]` when the name has at least one character: `if (!name.empty())`.
--- check case | An empty name is skipped
initials({"ada", "", "lin"})
=> "al"
--- check case | Every name has a letter
initials({"Grace", "Alan"})
=> "GA"
--- check case | Only empty names
initials({"", ""})
=> ""
--- check case | No names
initials({})
=> ""

+++ practice | Stretch: the longest climb
--- task
A probe logs its altitude once a minute. A **climb** is a run of readings where each one is strictly higher than the one before it. For `{5, 7, 9, 4, 6, 8, 10, 3}` the longest climb is 4, 6, 8, 10: it starts at index 3 and holds 4 readings.

```cpp
struct Run {
    int start;
    int length;
};
```

Write `Run longest_climb(const std::vector<int>& alt)`. It returns where the longest climb starts and how many readings it holds. No `main`.

- A single reading is a climb of length 1 on its own.
- If two climbs tie for longest, return the first.
- For an empty list, return `{-1, 0}`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

struct Run {
    int start;
    int length;
};

Run longest_climb(const std::vector<int>& alt) {
    return {0, 0};
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

struct Run {
    int start;
    int length;
};

Run longest_climb(const std::vector<int>& alt) {
    if (alt.empty()) return {-1, 0};
    Run best{0, 1};
    int start = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i < static_cast<int>(alt.size()); ++i) {
        if (alt[i] <= alt[i - 1]) start = i;
        int length = i - start + 1;
        if (length > best.length) best = {start, length};
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Walk the list once, keeping the index where the current climb started. When a reading is not higher than the one before, a new climb starts right there.
--- hint
At each index `i`, the current climb holds `i - start + 1` readings. Replace the best only when it is strictly longer, so the first of a tie stays.
--- hint
Deal with the empty list first. Otherwise start with `Run best{0, 1};`, because the first reading on its own is already a climb of 1.
--- check test | The longest climb in the middle
[] { Run r = longest_climb({5, 7, 9, 4, 6, 8, 10, 3}); return r.start == 3 && r.length == 4; }()
--- check test | Equal readings break a climb
[] { Run r = longest_climb({1, 2, 2, 3}); return r.start == 0 && r.length == 2; }()
--- check test | Always falling: every reading alone
[] { Run r = longest_climb({9, 7, 4}); return r.start == 0 && r.length == 1; }()
--- check test | A climb that runs to the end
[] { Run r = longest_climb({3, 1, 2, 3, 4}); return r.start == 1 && r.length == 4; }()
--- check test | Empty
[] { Run r = longest_climb({}); return r.start == -1 && r.length == 0; }()

=== cpp2-05 | std::map, std::unordered_map and std::set
--- teach
In the iterators lesson you walked through a container one position at a time, with `begin()`, `end()` and `*it`. In the basics course you met `std::map`, which finds a value by its key instead of by its position. This lesson adds two close relatives of the map, shows how to choose between all three, and uses iterators to look things up.

### Three containers for "look it up"

Think of three everyday lists. A phone's contact list is kept in alphabetical order, so you can read it top to bottom. A cloakroom hands you a ticket and finds your coat straight away, but the coats hang in no particular order. And a guest list at a door only answers one question: is this name on it?

C++ has one container for each:

| Container | Holds | Order | Lookup |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `std::map<K, V>` | key → value | sorted by key | O(log n) |
| `std::unordered_map<K, V>` | key → value | none you can rely on | O(1) on average |
| `std::set<T>` | unique values | sorted | O(log n) |

`K` stands for the key's type, `V` for the value's type and `T` for the type of the things stored. The last column says how the time to find something grows as the container fills up. It is written in [[big-O notation|big-o]]: **O(log n)**, read "order log n", means that doubling the number of items adds only about one more step; **O(1)**, read "order one", means about the same few steps however many items there are.

### std::set: each value once

A **std::set** is a sorted collection in which every value appears at most once. Add a value that is already there and nothing happens. It needs `#include <set>`.

```cpp
std::set<std::string> seen = {"b", "a", "b"};   // {"a", "b"}: the second "b" vanishes
seen.insert("c");                               // now {"a", "b", "c"}
seen.contains("a");                             // true
```

`.insert(x)` adds a value. `.contains(x)` answers true or false and never changes the set; it is [[new in C++20|contains-cpp20]].

### std::unordered_map: fast, in no fixed order

A **std::unordered_map** works like a `std::map` from the outside: keys, values, and `[ ]`. Inside, it is built as a [[hash table|hash-table]] instead of a sorted tree, which makes lookups faster but gives up the order. It needs `#include <unordered_map>`.

Counting works exactly as it did with `std::map`, because a missing key starts at 0. Here `words` is a `std::vector<std::string>`:

```cpp
std::unordered_map<std::string, int> count;
for (const auto& w : words) count[w]++;         // each word's count goes up by one
```

### Finding a key, and the arrow ->

`.find(key)` looks for a key without ever adding one. Like the algorithms in the iterators lesson, it gives back an iterator: a position pointing at the matching element, or `end()` if there is none. So always compare with `end()` before you use it:

```cpp
auto it = count.find("pear");
if (it != count.end()) {
    std::cout << it->first << " " << it->second << "\n";   // pear and its count
}
```

Each element of a map is a **pair**: a key and a value stuck together. `first` is the key and `second` is the value.

`->`, read "arrow", means "follow this, then take the member". [[Written out the long way|arrow-operator]], `it->first` is `(*it).first`: `*it` follows the iterator to the pair, and `.first` takes the key out of it. The arrow works the same way on pointers.

### Building one container from another

Every one of these containers can be made straight from a range, a begin and an end:

```cpp
std::vector<std::string> words = {"pear", "fig", "pear"};
std::set<std::string> s(words.begin(), words.end());      // {"fig", "pear"}
std::vector<std::string> back(s.begin(), s.end());        // {"fig", "pear"}, sorted
```

The middle line makes a set from the vector, which removes the repeats and sorts what is left. The last line makes a new vector from the set, in the set's order.

### Letters: isalpha and tolower

In the strings lesson you used `std::toupper` and `std::isdigit` from `<cctype>`. Two more helpers live there:

- `std::isalpha(c)` is true when `c` is a letter, `a` to `z` or `A` to `Z`.
- `std::tolower(c)` gives the lowercase version: `'A'` becomes `'a'`, and anything that is not a capital letter comes back unchanged.

As with `std::toupper`, hand them an `unsigned char` and turn the answer back into a `char`:

```cpp
char c = 'Q';
unsigned char u = static_cast<unsigned char>(c);
if (std::isalpha(u)) {
    std::cout << static_cast<char>(std::tolower(u));   // prints q
}
```

### Keeping the best so far

To find the biggest value in a map, walk through every pair and remember the best one you have seen. To break a tie, compare the keys too: `<` on two strings asks "does this one come first alphabetically?"

```cpp
std::map<std::string, int> fuel = {{"tank-b", 40}, {"tank-a", 40}, {"tank-c", 12}};
std::string fullest;   // the best name so far
int most = 0;          // its amount
for (const auto& [name, kg] : fuel) {
    if (kg > most || (kg == most && name < fullest)) {
        fullest = name;
        most = kg;
    }
}
// fullest is "tank-a": it ties with tank-b and comes first
```

Read the `if` as: "this one has more, **or** it has the same amount and its name comes first". A `std::map` already hands you its pairs in key order, so here the tie rule changes nothing. It matters with an `unordered_map`, whose pairs come in no fixed order.

### How to choose

- Only need fast lookups? `unordered_map`.
- Also need the keys in order, say for a sorted report or "the smallest key over 10"? `map`.
- Only care whether something is there? `set`.

**Watch out:** two things trip people up. First, `m[key]` **inserts** the key when it is missing, even when you only meant to read it; use `find` or `contains` to only look. Second, an `unordered_map` walks through its pairs in an [[unpredictable order|unordered-order]]. If your answer depends on order, like breaking ties alphabetically, you have to decide that order yourself, with a comparison like `name < fullest`.

::: context big-o Counting steps, not seconds
Big-O notation describes how the work grows as the data grows, ignoring the exact speed of any one computer. O(log n) comes from halving: a sorted tree can throw away about half of what is left at every step, and you can halve a million only about 20 times before one item is left. So a map of a million keys needs about 20 steps, and a map of two million about 21. O(1) means a fixed number of steps whether the container holds ten items or ten million. A plain search through a vector, looking at every element, is O(n): twice the data, twice the work.
:::

::: context contains-cpp20 Versions of C++
C++ is updated every three years, and each version is named after its year: C++11, C++14, C++17, C++20, C++23. `.contains` arrived in C++20, and this app compiles your code as C++20. In older code you will see `s.count(x)` used for the same job: for a set or a map it gives 1 when `x` is there and 0 when it is not, which works as true or false in an `if`.
:::

::: context hash-table How a hash table finds things fast
A hash table is a row of numbered slots called **buckets**. A **hash function** turns each key into a number, and that number picks the bucket. To find `"kiwi"` later, the table hashes `"kiwi"` again and looks straight in that one bucket, instead of searching. Sometimes two keys land in the same bucket; then the table checks the few keys there one by one. That is why lookup is O(1) only on average. The bucket numbers below are made up for the picture.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="50" y="40" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">"fig"</text>
  <text x="50" y="85" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">"kiwi"</text>
  <text x="50" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">"pear"</text>
  <rect x="100" y="60" width="70" height="36" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="135" y="82" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">hash</text>
  <line x1="75" y1="36" x2="100" y2="68" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="80" y1="81" x2="100" y2="78" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="80" y1="126" x2="100" y2="90" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>

  <rect x="230" y="41" width="100" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="230" y="67" width="100" height="26" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

  <text x="222" y="32" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="end">0</text>
  <text x="222" y="58" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="end">1</text>
  <text x="222" y="84" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="end">2</text>
  <text x="222" y="110" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="end">3</text>
  <text x="222" y="136" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="end">4</text>
  <text x="280" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">pear</text>
  <text x="280" y="110" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">fig</text>
  <text x="280" y="136" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">kiwi</text>
  <line x1="170" y1="78" x2="228" y2="78" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="200" y="162" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">buckets: the key's hash picks one</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context arrow-operator Why the arrow exists
The brackets in `(*it).first` are needed, because without them C++ would read `*it.first` as `*(it.first)`, which means something else entirely. Typing the brackets every time is clumsy, so the C language, which C++ grew out of, added `->` as a shorthand for "follow, then take a member". You will see it constantly on pointers to structs and classes, and later in the advanced course on smart pointers, which behave like pointers that clean up after themselves.
:::

::: context unordered-order Why the order is unpredictable
An `unordered_map` walks its buckets in bucket order, and which bucket a key lands in depends on its hash and on how many buckets there are right now. When the table grows, it moves every key into a new, bigger set of buckets, so the order can change after one more insert. It can also differ between compilers. Code that happens to print the right order on your computer can fail on someone else's. Flight software aims to be *deterministic*, meaning the same input always gives the same output, so engineers never let a result depend on an order nobody chose.
:::
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it. The starter already includes `<cctype>`, `<map>`, `<set>`, `<string>`, `<unordered_map>` and `<vector>`.

- `std::vector<std::string> unique_sorted(const std::vector<std::string>& words)` returns each different word once, in sorted order. `unique_sorted({"pear", "apple", "pear", "fig"})` is `{"apple", "fig", "pear"}`.
- `std::map<char, int> letter_counts(const std::string& text)` returns how often each letter appears, counting upper and lower case as the same letter. The keys are lowercase letters. Skip everything that is not a letter. `letter_counts("Aa b!")` has `'a'` → 2 and `'b'` → 1.
- `std::string most_common(const std::vector<std::string>& words)` returns the word that appears most often. If several words tie, return the one that comes first alphabetically. For an empty list, return `""`.
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <set>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> unique_sorted(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    return words;
}

std::map<char, int> letter_counts(const std::string& text) {
    return {};
}

std::string most_common(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <set>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> unique_sorted(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    std::set<std::string> s(words.begin(), words.end());
    return std::vector<std::string>(s.begin(), s.end());
}

std::map<char, int> letter_counts(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<char, int> counts;
    for (char c : text) {
        unsigned char u = static_cast<unsigned char>(c);
        if (std::isalpha(u)) counts[static_cast<char>(std::tolower(u))]++;
    }
    return counts;
}

std::string most_common(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    std::unordered_map<std::string, int> counts;
    for (const auto& w : words) counts[w]++;
    std::string best;
    int best_count = 0;
    for (const auto& [word, n] : counts) {
        if (n > best_count || (n == best_count && word < best)) {
            best = word;
            best_count = n;
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
For `unique_sorted`, a set does both jobs at once: it drops repeats and keeps things sorted. Build a set from the words, then build a vector from the set, as in "Building one container from another".
--- hint
For `letter_counts`, loop over `text` one `char` at a time. Turn each into an `unsigned char` `u`; when `std::isalpha(u)` is true, add one to `counts[static_cast<char>(std::tolower(u))]`.
--- hint
For `most_common`, count first with `counts[w]++` in a loop. Then walk the counts keeping the best so far, exactly like the fuel-tank example: replace it when the count is higher, or equal with a word that is `<` the best one.
--- check case | unique_sorted drops duplicates and sorts
unique_sorted({"pear", "apple", "pear", "fig"})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"apple", "fig", "pear"}
--- check case | letter_counts ignores case and non-letters
letter_counts("Aa b!")
=> std::map<char, int>{{'a', 2}, {'b', 1}}
--- check case | most_common picks the most frequent
most_common({"b", "a", "b", "c", "b", "a"})
=> "b"
--- check case | Ties go to the alphabetically smallest
most_common({"kiwi", "fig", "kiwi", "fig", "plum"})
=> "fig"
--- check case | Nothing in, empty string out
most_common({})
=> ""

+++ practice | How many different values?
--- task
Write two functions with a `std::set`. No `main`.

- `int distinct_count(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns how many different values `v` holds. `distinct_count({4, 1, 4, 4, 7})` is 3.
- `bool has_duplicate(const std::vector<std::string>& names)` returns `true` if any name appears more than once. `has_duplicate({"Ada", "Lin", "Ada"})` is `true`.

Both are `0` or `false` for an empty list.
--- starter
#include <set>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int distinct_count(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return static_cast<int>(v.size());
}

bool has_duplicate(const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <set>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int distinct_count(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::set<int> s(v.begin(), v.end());
    return static_cast<int>(s.size());
}

bool has_duplicate(const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    std::set<std::string> seen;
    for (const auto& n : names) {
        if (seen.contains(n)) return true;
        seen.insert(n);
    }
    return false;
}
--- hint
A set keeps each value once, so a set built from the vector's range has one element per different value.
--- hint
For `has_duplicate`, walk the names with a set of the ones seen so far. If a name is already in it (`contains`), that is a repeat.
--- check case | Three different values
distinct_count({4, 1, 4, 4, 7})
=> 3
--- check case | No values
distinct_count({})
=> 0
--- check test | Duplicates found and not
has_duplicate({"Ada", "Lin", "Ada"}) && !has_duplicate({"Ada", "ada"}) && !has_duplicate({})
--- check test | A repeat at the very end
has_duplicate({"a", "b", "c", "d", "a"})

+++ practice | Look it up without adding it
--- task
Write `std::string callsign_of(const std::map<std::string, std::string>& crew, const std::string& name)`. The map goes from crew names to radio call signs. Return the call sign for `name`, or `"unknown"` if `name` is not a key.

Use `find` and the arrow `->`. The map is a `const` reference, so `[ ]` is not allowed, and the checks make sure nothing is added. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>

std::string callsign_of(const std::map<std::string, std::string>& crew, const std::string& name) {
    return "unknown";
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>

std::string callsign_of(const std::map<std::string, std::string>& crew, const std::string& name) {
    auto it = crew.find(name);
    if (it == crew.end()) return "unknown";
    return it->second;
}
--- hint
`crew.find(name)` gives back an iterator: `crew.end()` when the name is missing.
--- hint
When it is not `end()`, the call sign is the pair's value: `it->second`.
--- check case | A name that is there
callsign_of({{"Mae", "Dragon"}, {"Yuri", "Cedar"}}, "Yuri")
=> "Cedar"
--- check case | A missing name
callsign_of({{"Mae", "Dragon"}}, "Zed")
=> "unknown"
--- check test | Nothing is added to the map
[] { const std::map<std::string, std::string> m{{"Mae", "Dragon"}}; callsign_of(m, "Zed"); return m.size() == 1; }()
--- check case | An empty map
callsign_of({}, "Mae")
=> "unknown"

+++ practice | Words said more than n times
--- task
Write `std::vector<std::string> said_more_than(const std::string& text, int n)`. Split `text` into words at the spaces with a `std::istringstream`, count each word, and return the words that appear **more than** `n` times, in alphabetical order. Words are compared exactly, so `"Go"` and `"go"` are different.

`said_more_than("go no go go no stay", 1)` is `{"go", "no"}`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> said_more_than(const std::string& text, int n) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> said_more_than(const std::string& text, int n) {
    std::map<std::string, int> counts;
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string word;
    while (in >> word) counts[word]++;
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    for (const auto& [w, c] : counts) {
        if (c > n) out.push_back(w);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Count first: `while (in >> word) counts[word]++;` with a `std::map<std::string, int>`.
--- hint
A `std::map` hands its pairs over in key order, which for strings is alphabetical. So walk it and keep each word whose count is greater than `n`.
--- check case | Two words said more than once
said_more_than("go no go go no stay", 1)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"go", "no"}
--- check case | More than 2
said_more_than("go no go go no stay", 2)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"go"}
--- check case | Case matters
said_more_than("Go go", 1)
=> std::vector<std::string>{}
--- check case | n of 0 gives every word once
said_more_than("b  a b", 0)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"a", "b"}
--- check case | Empty text
said_more_than("", 0)
=> std::vector<std::string>{}

+++ practice | The most common letter
--- task
Write `char most_common_letter(const std::string& text)`. It returns the letter that appears most often in `text`, as a lowercase letter. Upper and lower case count as the same letter, and anything that is not a letter is ignored. No `main`.

- `most_common_letter("Apollo")` is `'l'`, and so is `most_common_letter("LOL lol")`.
- If letters tie, return the one that comes first in the alphabet: `most_common_letter("bab a")` is `'a'`.
- If `text` has no letters at all, return `'?'`.
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <string>

char most_common_letter(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<char, int> counts;
    for (char c : text) counts[c]++;
    char best = text[0];
    for (const auto& [letter, n] : counts) {
        if (n > counts[best]) best = letter;
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <string>

char most_common_letter(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<char, int> counts;
    for (char c : text) {
        unsigned char u = static_cast<unsigned char>(c);
        if (std::isalpha(u)) counts[static_cast<char>(std::tolower(u))]++;
    }
    char best = '?';
    int best_count = 0;
    for (const auto& [letter, n] : counts) {
        if (n > best_count) {
            best = letter;
            best_count = n;
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Count only letters, and count each one under its lowercase form: `std::isalpha` and `std::tolower` on the `unsigned char`.
--- hint
Keep the best letter so far, starting at `'?'` with a count of 0. The map gives letters in alphabetical order, so replacing only on a strictly higher count keeps the earliest letter of a tie.
--- check case | Apollo
most_common_letter("Apollo")
=> 'l'
--- check case | Upper and lower case together
most_common_letter("LOL lol")
=> 'l'
--- check case | A tie goes to the earlier letter
most_common_letter("bab a")
=> 'a'
--- check case | No letters at all
most_common_letter("12 + 7 = 19!")
=> '?'
--- check case | Empty text
most_common_letter("")
=> '?'

+++ practice | Debug: the lookup that grew the map
--- task
**Bug report:** "`count_known(ids, names)` gives the wrong count when a crew member's id is 0. And after it runs, the `ids` map has grown: every unknown name has been added to it."

`ids` maps crew names to id numbers, and an id of 0 is a real id. `count_known` should return how many of `names` are keys in `ids`, and leave `ids` exactly as it was. Keep the function's parameters as they are, and fix the line that causes both problems. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// How many of the names have an id. The ids map must not change.
int count_known(std::map<std::string, int>& ids, const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    int count = 0;
    for (const auto& name : names) {
        if (ids[name] != 0) ++count;
    }
    return count;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// How many of the names have an id. The ids map must not change.
int count_known(std::map<std::string, int>& ids, const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    int count = 0;
    for (const auto& name : names) {
        if (ids.contains(name)) ++count;
    }
    return count;
}
--- hint
What does `ids[name]` do when `name` is not a key? And what does it give for a name whose id really is 0?
--- hint
You only want to ask "is this a key?". `ids.contains(name)` answers that and never adds anything.
--- check case | An id of 0 still counts
[] { std::map<std::string, int> ids{{"Mae", 0}, {"Yuri", 7}}; return count_known(ids, {"Mae", "Yuri", "Zed"}); }()
=> 2
--- check test | Unknown names are not added
[] { std::map<std::string, int> ids{{"Mae", 3}}; count_known(ids, {"Zed", "Ann"}); return ids.size() == 1; }()
--- check case | A name asked twice counts twice
[] { std::map<std::string, int> ids{{"Mae", 3}}; return count_known(ids, {"Mae", "Mae"}); }()
=> 2
--- check case | No names
[] { std::map<std::string, int> ids{{"Mae", 3}}; return count_known(ids, {}); }()
=> 0

+++ practice | Stretch: the hottest sensor
--- task
Each line of a sensor log is a sensor name and a whole-number reading, separated by a space, like `"pump 71"`. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::map<std::string, int> max_per_sensor(const std::vector<std::string>& lines)` returns each sensor's highest reading. Read each line with its own `std::istringstream` and `>>`. Skip a line if a name and then a number cannot both be read from it.
- `std::string hottest_sensor(const std::vector<std::string>& lines)` returns the sensor with the highest reading of all. If sensors tie, return the one that comes first alphabetically. With no valid lines, return `""`.

For `{"pump 71", "valve 40", "pump 65", "tank x", "valve 71"}`: `max_per_sensor` is pump → 71 and valve → 71 (the line `"tank x"` is skipped), and `hottest_sensor` is `"pump"`, which ties with valve and comes first.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::map<std::string, int> max_per_sensor(const std::vector<std::string>& lines) {
    std::map<std::string, int> best;
    for (const auto& line : lines) {
        std::istringstream in(line);
        std::string name;
        int value;
        in >> name >> value;
        best[name] = value;
    }
    return best;
}

std::string hottest_sensor(const std::vector<std::string>& lines) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::map<std::string, int> max_per_sensor(const std::vector<std::string>& lines) {
    std::map<std::string, int> best;
    for (const auto& line : lines) {
        std::istringstream in(line);
        std::string name;
        int value;
        if (!(in >> name >> value)) continue;
        auto it = best.find(name);
        if (it == best.end() || value > it->second) best[name] = value;
    }
    return best;
}

std::string hottest_sensor(const std::vector<std::string>& lines) {
    std::string hottest;
    int top = 0;
    bool found = false;
    for (const auto& [name, value] : max_per_sensor(lines)) {
        if (!found || value > top) {
            hottest = name;
            top = value;
            found = true;
        }
    }
    return hottest;
}
--- hint
`in >> name >> value` is true only when both reads worked, so it can sit in an `if` that skips the bad line with `continue`.
--- hint
A reading replaces the stored one only when the sensor is new (`find` gives `end()`) or the reading is higher. Readings can be negative, so do not start anything at 0 and compare.
--- hint
For `hottest_sensor`, walk the map from `max_per_sensor`. It is in alphabetical order, so replacing only on a strictly higher reading keeps the earliest name of a tie. A `bool found` tells you whether you have a best yet.
--- check case | Highest reading per sensor, bad lines skipped
max_per_sensor({"pump 71", "valve 40", "pump 65", "tank x", "valve 71"})
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"pump", 71}, {"valve", 71}}
--- check case | Negative readings
max_per_sensor({"cryo -180", "cryo -175", "cryo -190"})
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"cryo", -175}}
--- check case | A tie goes to the earlier name
hottest_sensor({"pump 71", "valve 40", "pump 65", "tank x", "valve 71"})
=> "pump"
--- check case | All readings negative
hottest_sensor({"cryo -180", "ice -20"})
=> "ice"
--- check case | No valid lines
hottest_sensor({"", "pump", "7 up"})
=> ""

=== cpp2-06 | Classes that keep their own rules
--- teach
Last lesson you chose between ready-made containers. This lesson goes back to a type you design yourself. In the basics course you built an `Account` class with private data, a constructor and member functions. Now you will build a class whose whole job is to keep a rule true, whatever anyone does with it.

### A rule that is always true

Picture a digital clock. It can show 07:05 or 23:59, but never 07:75 and never 25:00. That is a rule about every clock, all the time.

A rule like that is called an **[[invariant|invariant-word]]**: something that is true of every object of a class, always. A class keeps its invariant in three ways:

1. The **constructor** makes the rule true the moment the object is born.
2. Every **member function** that changes the data leaves the rule true when it finishes.
3. The data is **private**, so no code outside the class can reach in and break it.

If the data were public, any line of the program could set the minutes to 75. With a class, the only ways in are the constructor and the member functions, so those are the only places the rule has to be enforced.

### A small example: a countdown timer

Here is a timer whose rule is "the seconds left are never negative":

```cpp
class Timer {
public:
    explicit Timer(int seconds) : seconds_(seconds < 0 ? 0 : seconds) {}
    void tick() { if (seconds_ > 0) --seconds_; }
    bool done() const { return seconds_ == 0; }
private:
    int seconds_;   // rule: never negative
};
```

Go through it one piece at a time:

- The constructor uses the conditional operator from the algorithms lesson: `seconds < 0 ? 0 : seconds` gives `0` when the starting value is negative, and the value itself otherwise. So `Timer t(-5);` starts at 0, and the rule is true from the first moment.
- `tick()` counts down by one, but only while there is something left. `--seconds_`, read "decrement", takes one away; it is the opposite of `++`. The `if` guard means the count can never go below 0.
- `done()` only looks, so it is marked `const`, as in the const correctness lesson.

Two habits from the basics course matter here. The **member initializer list**, `: seconds_(…)`, gives each member its first value before the constructor body runs; prefer it to assigning in the body. And `explicit` on a constructor that takes one value stops surprise conversions like `Timer t = 5;`.

### Store the data in the shape that makes the rule easy

Now the clock. You could store hours and minutes as two numbers, but then every function has to juggle both: adding 70 minutes means carrying into the hours, and the hours then wrap past 23.

A neater trick is to store **one** number: the total minutes since midnight. A day has 24 × 60 = 1440 minutes, so the rule becomes "the total is always in [0, 1440)". That bracket notation is the [[half-open range|half-open-day]] from the iterators lesson: from 0, up to but **not** including 1440.

Hours and minutes then come straight out with whole-number division and remainder:

```cpp
int total = 125;         // 125 minutes after midnight
int h = total / 60;      // 2
int m = total % 60;      // 5, so the time is 02:05
```

### Wrapping any number onto the clock

Every time the total changes, it has to be brought back into [0, 1440). The remainder `%` nearly does it: `1500 % 1440` is `60`, which is 01:00 the next day.

But remember from the basics course that [[% keeps the sign of the left side|remainder-sign]]. So `-1 % 1440` is `-1`, not `1439`. One minute before midnight should be 23:59, and a plain `%` gets it wrong.

The fix is a formula that works for every whole number, negative or not. To wrap `x` into [0, n):

```cpp
((x % n) + n) % n
```

Follow it for `x = -1` and `n = 1440`:

1. `-1 % 1440` is `-1`.
2. Add 1440: `1439`.
3. `1439 % 1440` is `1439`. That is 23:59.

And for `x = 60`, a number already in range:

1. `60 % 1440` is `60`.
2. Add 1440: `1500`.
3. `1500 % 1440` is `60` again. The last `%` undoes the extra 1440 when it was not needed.

### Numbers into text

`std::to_string`, from `<string>`, turns a number into text: `std::to_string(7)` is `"7"`. A clock shows two digits, so add a `"0"` in front when the number is below 10:

```cpp
std::string s = std::to_string(7);   // "7"
if (7 < 10) s = "0" + s;             // "07"
```

Your clock will have a member function called `to_string` too. That is allowed: inside the class, writing `std::to_string` with the `std::` in front makes it clear you mean the library's one.

### static: belongs to the class, not to one object

Some helpers do not need any particular clock. Wrapping a number is the same sum for every clock there is. A member marked **static** belongs to the class itself, not to any one object:

```cpp
class Clock {
    // ...
private:
    static constexpr int kDay = 24 * 60;
    static int wrap(int m) { return ((m % kDay) + kDay) % kDay; }
};
```

- `static int wrap(int m)` is a [[static member function|static-members]]: a helper that works without an object, so it cannot see any one clock's data. It only uses its parameter.
- `static constexpr int kDay = 24 * 60;` is one constant that every `Clock` shares. **constexpr** means its value is [[known while compiling|constexpr-meaning]], before the program ever runs (the advanced course covers it properly). Constants are often named `kLikeThis`, with a small `k` in front.

Both sit in the `private:` part, because they are the class's own tools, not something other code needs.

**Watch out:** the invariant has to be restored in **every** place the data changes. It is easy to wrap the total in the constructor and then forget in `add_minutes`; the clock works until someone adds 90 minutes at 23:00. Put the wrapping in one helper and call it everywhere the total changes.

::: context invariant-word A word from mathematics
"Invariant" means "does not vary". Mathematicians use it for something that stays the same while everything else changes: however you cut up a pizza, the total amount of pizza is invariant. Programmers borrowed it for the rules a class promises to keep. Careful teams write their invariants down and check them. NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory, in its "Power of Ten" rules for safety-critical code, asks for at least two assertions per function on average: checks that stop the program loudly if a rule that should always be true turns out to be false.
:::

::: context half-open-day Why 1440 itself is left out
Minute 0 is midnight, 00:00. Minute 1439 is 23:59. Minute 1440 would be midnight again, the start of the next day, so it must wrap back to 0. A half-open range makes that automatic: the day has exactly 1440 allowed values, 0 to 1439, and the range [0, 1440) names them by writing the first one and the first one that is *not* allowed.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="30" y1="60" x2="310" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="30" y="50" width="250" height="20" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="30" cy="60" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="310" cy="60" r="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="30" y="35" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="30" y="92" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">00:00</text>
  <text x="280" y="35" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1439</text>
  <text x="280" y="92" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">23:59</text>
  <text x="318" y="35" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">1440</text>
  <text x="318" y="92" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">not allowed</text>
  <text x="155" y="112" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">[0, 1440): 1440 allowed values</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context remainder-sign Why C++ keeps the sign
C++ division of whole numbers cuts toward zero, so `-1 / 1440` is `0`. The remainder is then defined so that division and remainder always fit back together: `(a / b) * b + a % b` equals `a`. With `a = -1` that forces `a % b` to be `-1`. Other languages choose differently: in Python, `-1 % 1440` is `1439`. The same formula wraps compass headings too: `((h % 360) + 360) % 360` turns a heading of -90 degrees into 270.
:::

::: context static-members One copy for the whole class
Every `Clock` object has its own `total_`: two clocks, two totals. A static member has exactly one copy, shared by the whole class, however many clocks exist, even none at all. That is why a static member function has no object to work on, and cannot use `total_` or call `hours()`. From outside the class you would call a public one with the scope operator, read "from": `Clock::wrap(90)`, "wrap from Clock".
:::

::: context constexpr-meaning Worked out by the compiler
When the compiler sees `constexpr int kDay = 24 * 60;`, it works out 1440 itself while it builds your program, and writes that number wherever `kDay` is used. Nothing is multiplied while the program runs, and nothing can change `kDay` afterwards. Flight software leans on values like this: a fixed table size or a sensor's limit that is known for certain before launch should be a constant the compiler checks, not a variable that something might overwrite.
:::
--- task
Write `class Clock`, a 24-hour clock that always shows a real time. No `main`: the checker supplies it. The starter includes `<string>`.

- `Clock(int hours, int minutes)` accepts any values, and wraps them onto the 24-hour clock: `Clock(25, 70)` is 02:10, and `Clock(0, -1)` is 23:59.
- `void add_minutes(int m)` moves the time forward, or back if `m` is negative, wrapping around midnight.
- `int hours() const` and `int minutes() const` return the hour (0 to 23) and the minute (0 to 59).
- `std::string to_string() const` returns the time as `HH:MM`, always two digits each, like `"07:05"`.

The starter stores hours and minutes separately and never wraps them. You may change the private part however you like.
--- starter
#include <string>

class Clock {
public:
    Clock(int hours, int minutes) : hours_(hours), minutes_(minutes) {}
    void add_minutes(int m) { minutes_ += m; }
    int hours() const { return hours_; }
    int minutes() const { return minutes_; }
    std::string to_string() const { return std::to_string(hours_) + ":" + std::to_string(minutes_); }
private:
    int hours_;
    int minutes_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>

class Clock {
public:
    Clock(int hours, int minutes) : total_(wrap(hours * 60 + minutes)) {}
    void add_minutes(int m) { total_ = wrap(total_ + m); }
    int hours() const { return total_ / 60; }
    int minutes() const { return total_ % 60; }
    std::string to_string() const { return two(hours()) + ":" + two(minutes()); }
private:
    static constexpr int kDay = 24 * 60;
    static int wrap(int m) { return ((m % kDay) + kDay) % kDay; }
    static std::string two(int n) { return (n < 10 ? "0" : "") + std::to_string(n); }
    int total_;   // minutes since midnight, always in [0, kDay)
};
--- hint
Store one number, the minutes since midnight, as a private `int total_;`. The constructor turns its two values into `hours * 60 + minutes` and wraps that.
--- hint
Write a private helper `static int wrap(int m)` that returns `((m % 1440) + 1440) % 1440`, and call it in both places the total changes: the constructor (`: total_(wrap(hours * 60 + minutes))`) and `add_minutes`. Then `hours()` is `total_ / 60` and `minutes()` is `total_ % 60`.
--- hint
For `to_string`, turn each part into text with `std::to_string`, put a `"0"` in front when it is below 10, and join the two parts with `":"` between them.
--- check case | Clock(8, 5) shows 08:05
Clock(8, 5).to_string()
=> "08:05"
--- check case | Clock(25, 70) wraps to 02:10
Clock(25, 70).to_string()
=> "02:10"
--- check case | Clock(0, -1) is 23:59
Clock(0, -1).to_string()
=> "23:59"
--- check test | add_minutes wraps forwards and backwards
[] { Clock c(23, 30); c.add_minutes(45); bool fwd = c.to_string() == "00:15"; c.add_minutes(-1500); return fwd && c.to_string() == "23:15" && c.hours() == 23 && c.minutes() == 15; }()
--- check case | A negative start many days back
Clock(-49, 0).to_string()
=> "23:00"

+++ practice | A thermostat that stays in range
--- task
Write `class Thermostat`. Its rule: the target temperature is always between 10 and 30 degrees, both included. No `main`.

- `explicit Thermostat(int target)` starts at `target`, pulled into the range: `Thermostat(5)` starts at 10 and `Thermostat(99)` at 30.
- `void raise(int by)` and `void lower(int by)` move the target up or down by `by`, and the result is pulled back into the range too.
- `int target() const` returns the current target.

After `Thermostat t(22); t.raise(15);` the target is 30. Then `t.lower(25)` makes it 10.
--- starter
class Thermostat {
public:
    explicit Thermostat(int target) : target_(target) {}
    void raise(int by) { target_ += by; }
    void lower(int by) { target_ -= by; }
    int target() const { return target_; }
private:
    int target_;
};
--- solution
class Thermostat {
public:
    explicit Thermostat(int target) : target_(clamp(target)) {}
    void raise(int by) { target_ = clamp(target_ + by); }
    void lower(int by) { target_ = clamp(target_ - by); }
    int target() const { return target_; }
private:
    static constexpr int kLow = 10;
    static constexpr int kHigh = 30;
    static int clamp(int t) { return t < kLow ? kLow : (t > kHigh ? kHigh : t); }
    int target_;   // always in [10, 30]
};
--- hint
The rule has to be made true in the constructor and restored in both `raise` and `lower`. Put the "pull into range" step in one private static helper and call it in all three places.
--- hint
The helper gives 10 for anything below 10, 30 for anything above 30, and the value itself otherwise.
--- check test | The constructor pulls the start into range
Thermostat(5).target() == 10 && Thermostat(99).target() == 30 && Thermostat(21).target() == 21
--- check test | raise and lower stay in range
[] { Thermostat t(22); t.raise(15); bool top = t.target() == 30; t.lower(25); return top && t.target() == 10; }()
--- check test | The edges themselves are allowed
[] { Thermostat t(10); t.raise(20); bool high = t.target() == 30; t.lower(20); return Thermostat(30).target() == 30 && high && t.target() == 10; }()
--- check test | A negative raise lowers the target, still in range
[] { Thermostat t(12); t.raise(-50); return t.target() == 10; }()

+++ practice | A compass heading
--- task
Write `class Compass`. Its rule: the heading is always a whole number of degrees in [0, 360), from 0 up to but not including 360. No `main`.

- `explicit Compass(int degrees)` accepts any value and wraps it: `Compass(370)` points at 10 and `Compass(-90)` at 270.
- `void turn(int degrees)` turns right by `degrees`, or left if it is negative, wrapping past north.
- `int heading() const` returns the heading.
- `std::string to_string() const` returns the heading as three digits, with zeros in front when needed: `"045"`, `"270"`, `"000"`.
--- starter
#include <string>

class Compass {
public:
    explicit Compass(int degrees) : deg_(degrees % 360) {}
    void turn(int degrees) { deg_ += degrees; }
    int heading() const { return deg_; }
    std::string to_string() const { return std::to_string(deg_); }
private:
    int deg_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>

class Compass {
public:
    explicit Compass(int degrees) : deg_(wrap(degrees)) {}
    void turn(int degrees) { deg_ = wrap(deg_ + degrees); }
    int heading() const { return deg_; }
    std::string to_string() const {
        std::string s = std::to_string(deg_);
        while (s.size() < 3) s = "0" + s;
        return s;
    }
private:
    static constexpr int kCircle = 360;
    static int wrap(int d) { return ((d % kCircle) + kCircle) % kCircle; }
    int deg_;   // always in [0, 360)
};
--- hint
Wrap with the formula from the lesson, `((d % 360) + 360) % 360`, in a private static helper. Call it in the constructor and in `turn`.
--- hint
For `to_string`, start from `std::to_string(deg_)` and put `"0"` in front while the text is shorter than 3 characters.
--- check test | The constructor wraps both ways
Compass(370).heading() == 10 && Compass(-90).heading() == 270 && Compass(360).heading() == 0
--- check test | Turning past north, both ways
[] { Compass c(350); c.turn(20); bool right = c.heading() == 10; c.turn(-30); return right && c.heading() == 340; }()
--- check test | Several full turns
[] { Compass c(0); c.turn(-1085); return c.heading() == 355; }()
--- check test | Three digits
Compass(45).to_string() == "045" && Compass(270).to_string() == "270" && Compass(0).to_string() == "000" && Compass(-351).to_string() == "009"

+++ practice | A fuel tank that never overflows
--- task
Write `class FuelTank` and one free function. The rule: the level is always between 0 and the capacity, both included. No `main`.

- `explicit FuelTank(int capacity)` makes an empty tank. A negative capacity is treated as 0.
- `int fill(int litres)` adds fuel, but never past the capacity. It returns how much actually went in.
- `int draw(int litres)` takes fuel out, but never below 0. It returns how much actually came out.
- `int level() const` and `int capacity() const` return the level and the capacity.
- `int total_level(const std::vector<FuelTank>& tanks)` returns the levels of all the tanks added up.

For a tank of capacity 100: `fill(70)` returns 70, then `fill(50)` returns 30 and the tank is full. Then `draw(120)` returns 100 and the tank is empty. You may assume `fill` and `draw` are never given a negative amount.
--- starter
#include <vector>

class FuelTank {
public:
    explicit FuelTank(int capacity) : capacity_(capacity), level_(0) {}
    int fill(int litres) { level_ += litres; return litres; }
    int draw(int litres) { level_ -= litres; return litres; }
    int level() const { return level_; }
    int capacity() const { return capacity_; }
private:
    int capacity_;
    int level_;
};

int total_level(const std::vector<FuelTank>& tanks) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

class FuelTank {
public:
    explicit FuelTank(int capacity) : capacity_(capacity < 0 ? 0 : capacity), level_(0) {}
    int fill(int litres) {
        int room = capacity_ - level_;
        int added = litres < room ? litres : room;
        level_ += added;
        return added;
    }
    int draw(int litres) {
        int taken = litres < level_ ? litres : level_;
        level_ -= taken;
        return taken;
    }
    int level() const { return level_; }
    int capacity() const { return capacity_; }
private:
    int capacity_;
    int level_;   // always in [0, capacity_]
};

int total_level(const std::vector<FuelTank>& tanks) {
    int total = 0;
    for (const auto& t : tanks) total += t.level();
    return total;
}
--- hint
In `fill`, the room left is `capacity_ - level_`. What goes in is the smaller of `litres` and that room. `draw` is the same idea with `level_` in place of the room.
--- hint
The constructor makes the rule true from the start: use the conditional operator so a negative capacity becomes 0.
--- check test | fill stops at the capacity and says how much went in
[] { FuelTank t(100); int a = t.fill(70); int b = t.fill(50); return a == 70 && b == 30 && t.level() == 100; }()
--- check test | draw stops at empty and says how much came out
