[] { FuelTank t(100); t.fill(60); int d = t.draw(120); return d == 60 && t.level() == 0; }()
--- check test | A negative capacity is an empty, zero-sized tank
[] { FuelTank t(-5); int a = t.fill(10); return t.capacity() == 0 && a == 0 && t.level() == 0; }()
--- check case | total_level adds the levels
[] { std::vector<FuelTank> v{FuelTank(50), FuelTank(80)}; v[0].fill(20); v[1].fill(100); return total_level(v); }()
=> 100

+++ practice | A fraction that is always in lowest terms
--- task
Write `class Fraction`. Its rules: the denominator (the bottom number) is always greater than 0, and the fraction is always in **lowest terms**, meaning the top and bottom share no whole-number factor bigger than 1. So 2/4 is stored as 1/2, 3/-6 as -1/2, and 0/5 as 0/1. No `main`.

- `Fraction(int num, int den)` builds `num / den` and makes the rules true. If `den` is 0, the fraction becomes 0/1.
- `int num() const` and `int den() const` return the top and the bottom.
- `Fraction plus(const Fraction& other) const` returns the sum as a new fraction: a/b + c/d is (a × d + c × b) / (b × d), which the constructor then reduces.
- `std::string to_string() const` returns `"num/den"`, like `"-1/2"`.

To reduce, divide top and bottom by their **greatest common divisor**: the biggest whole number that divides both. You can find it with this loop, on two numbers `a` and `b` that are not negative: while `b` is not 0, the new `a` is the old `b`, and the new `b` is the old `a % b`. When `b` reaches 0, `a` is the answer. For 12 and 18 the pairs go (12, 18), (18, 12), (12, 6), (6, 0), so the divisor is 6.
--- starter
#include <string>

class Fraction {
public:
    Fraction(int num, int den) : num_(num), den_(den) {}
    int num() const { return num_; }
    int den() const { return den_; }
    Fraction plus(const Fraction& other) const { return Fraction(num_ + other.num_, den_ + other.den_); }
    std::string to_string() const { return std::to_string(num_) + "/" + std::to_string(den_); }
private:
    int num_;
    int den_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <string>

class Fraction {
public:
    Fraction(int num, int den) : num_(0), den_(1) {
        if (den == 0) return;
        if (den < 0) {
            num = -num;
            den = -den;
        }
        int g = gcd(std::abs(num), den);
        num_ = num / g;
        den_ = den / g;
    }
    int num() const { return num_; }
    int den() const { return den_; }
    Fraction plus(const Fraction& other) const {
        return Fraction(num_ * other.den_ + other.num_ * den_, den_ * other.den_);
    }
    std::string to_string() const { return std::to_string(num_) + "/" + std::to_string(den_); }
private:
    static int gcd(int a, int b) {
        while (b != 0) {
            int r = a % b;
            a = b;
            b = r;
        }
        return a;
    }
    int num_;
    int den_;   // always > 0, and gcd(|num_|, den_) == 1
};
--- hint
Deal with the special cases first. A denominator of 0 gives 0/1. A negative denominator flips the sign of both numbers, so the bottom becomes positive and the top carries the sign.
--- hint
Then divide both by the greatest common divisor of `std::abs(num)` and `den`. For a top of 0 that divisor is `den` itself, so 0/5 becomes 0/1 with no special code.
--- hint
The divisor loop needs a spare variable, as in the swapping example of the references lesson: `int r = a % b; a = b; b = r;`.
--- check test | Reduced, with the sign on top
Fraction(2, 4).to_string() == "1/2" && Fraction(3, -6).to_string() == "-1/2" && Fraction(-4, -8).to_string() == "1/2"
--- check test | Zero is 0/1, and so is a zero denominator
Fraction(0, 5).to_string() == "0/1" && Fraction(7, 0).to_string() == "0/1"
--- check test | plus reduces its answer
Fraction(1, 6).plus(Fraction(1, 3)).to_string() == "1/2" && Fraction(1, 2).plus(Fraction(-1, 2)).to_string() == "0/1"
--- check test | A whole number
[] { Fraction f(12, 4); return f.num() == 3 && f.den() == 1; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the angle that went out of range
--- task
**Bug report:** "`Angle(-90).degrees()` is -90, but it should be 270. And an `Angle(350)` after `add(20)` says 370 instead of 10."

`Angle` promises that `degrees()` is always in [0, 360). Find every place the rule is broken and restore it, with one wrapping helper used everywhere the value changes. No `main`.
--- starter
class Angle {
public:
    explicit Angle(int degrees) : deg_(degrees % 360) {}
    void add(int degrees) { deg_ += degrees; }
    int degrees() const { return deg_; }
private:
    int deg_;   // rule: always in [0, 360)
};
--- solution
class Angle {
public:
    explicit Angle(int degrees) : deg_(wrap(degrees)) {}
    void add(int degrees) { deg_ = wrap(deg_ + degrees); }
    int degrees() const { return deg_; }
private:
    static int wrap(int d) { return ((d % 360) + 360) % 360; }
    int deg_;   // rule: always in [0, 360)
};
--- hint
There are two bugs. `%` keeps the sign of the left side, so `-90 % 360` is still -90. And `add` never wraps at all.
--- hint
Write `static int wrap(int d)` with the formula `((d % 360) + 360) % 360`, and call it in the constructor and in `add`.
--- check test | Negative angles wrap
Angle(-90).degrees() == 270 && Angle(-720).degrees() == 0
--- check test | add wraps past 360
[] { Angle a(350); a.add(20); return a.degrees() == 10; }()
--- check test | add wraps below 0
[] { Angle a(10); a.add(-30); return a.degrees() == 340; }()
--- check test | Big values
[] { Angle a(1000); a.add(-1440); return a.degrees() == 280; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: keep only the recent readings
--- task
A display shows only the most recent readings. Write `class RecentReadings`. Its rule: it never holds more than `capacity` readings; when a new one arrives and it is full, the oldest one is dropped. No `main`.

- `explicit RecentReadings(int capacity)`. A capacity below 1 is treated as 1.
- `void add(int reading)` adds a reading, dropping the oldest if it is already full.
- `int count() const` returns how many readings it holds now.
- `int oldest() const` and `int newest() const` return the oldest and newest readings it holds, or `0` when it holds none.
- `double average() const` returns the average of the readings it holds, or `0.0` when it holds none.

With capacity 3, after adding 5, 8, 2 and 9, it holds 8, 2 and 9: `oldest()` is 8, `newest()` is 9, and `average()` is about 6.33.
--- starter
#include <vector>

class RecentReadings {
public:
    explicit RecentReadings(int capacity) : capacity_(capacity) {}
    void add(int reading) { items_.push_back(reading); }
    int count() const { return static_cast<int>(items_.size()); }
    int oldest() const { return items_[0]; }
    int newest() const { return items_[items_.size() - 1]; }
    double average() const { return 0.0; }
private:
    int capacity_;
    std::vector<int> items_;
};
--- solution
#include <vector>

class RecentReadings {
public:
    explicit RecentReadings(int capacity) : capacity_(capacity < 1 ? 1 : capacity) {}
    void add(int reading) {
        if (count() < capacity_) {
            items_.push_back(reading);
            return;
        }
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i + 1 < items_.size(); ++i) items_[i] = items_[i + 1];
        items_[items_.size() - 1] = reading;
    }
    int count() const { return static_cast<int>(items_.size()); }
    int oldest() const { return items_.empty() ? 0 : items_[0]; }
    int newest() const { return items_.empty() ? 0 : items_[items_.size() - 1]; }
    double average() const {
        if (items_.empty()) return 0.0;
        long long total = 0;
        for (int x : items_) total += x;
        return static_cast<double>(total) / items_.size();
    }
private:
    int capacity_;
    std::vector<int> items_;   // oldest first, never more than capacity_
};
--- hint
Keep the readings in a vector, oldest first. While it has room, `push_back`. When it is full, move every reading one place toward the front, then put the new one in the last place.
--- hint
The rule also covers the capacity: make it at least 1 in the constructor. And every getter must cope with an empty vector.
--- check test | Only the last three are kept
[] { RecentReadings r(3); for (int x : {5, 8, 2, 9}) r.add(x); return r.count() == 3 && r.oldest() == 8 && r.newest() == 9 && std::abs(r.average() - 19.0 / 3) < 1e-9; }()
--- check test | Before it is full, nothing is dropped
[] { RecentReadings r(5); r.add(4); r.add(-6); return r.count() == 2 && r.oldest() == 4 && r.newest() == -6 && std::abs(r.average() + 1.0) < 1e-9; }()
--- check test | A capacity of 0 is treated as 1
[] { RecentReadings r(0); r.add(1); r.add(2); return r.count() == 1 && r.oldest() == 2 && r.newest() == 2; }()
--- check test | Empty
[] { const RecentReadings r(4); return r.count() == 0 && r.oldest() == 0 && r.newest() == 0 && r.average() == 0.0; }()

=== cpp2-07 | Overloading and default arguments
--- teach
Last lesson your `Clock` turned numbers into text with `std::to_string`. You may have noticed that `std::to_string(7)` and `std::to_string(2.5)` both work, although one gets an `int` and the other a `double`. That is because `std::to_string` is not one function but several with the same name. This lesson shows how to write functions like that yourself, and how to let a caller leave out an argument.

### One name, several functions

In everyday speech one word can mean different things, and you tell which from what follows it: "play the piano" and "play football". C++ does the same with function names.

**Overloading** means giving several functions the same name, as long as their parameter lists differ: a different number of parameters, or different types.

```cpp
std::string area(int side)           { return "square"; }
std::string area(int w, int h)       { return "rectangle"; }
std::string area(double radius)      { return "circle"; }
```

Each version is a separate function. The return type alone does not count: two functions that differ only in what they return cannot share a name, because the call looks the same for both.

### How the compiler picks one

When you call `area`, the compiler looks at the arguments and picks the version that fits them best. This choosing is called **overload resolution**, and it happens while [[compiling|name-mangling]], not while the program runs.

```cpp
area(3);       // "square"    — 3 is an int: exact match
area(3, 4);    // "rectangle" — the only one with two parameters
area(1.5);     // "circle"    — 1.5 is a double: exact match
```

When no version is an exact match, the compiler ranks how much each argument would have to change. Roughly, from best to worst:

1. an **exact match**: the argument already has the parameter's type;
2. a **[[promotion|promotion]]**: a small, safe widening, like `char` to `int` or `float` to `double`;
3. a **standard conversion**: a change built into the language, like `int` to `double`, or *anything pointer-like* to `bool`;
4. a **user-defined conversion**: a change some class provides, like making a `std::string` out of a `const char*`.

If two versions tie for best, the call is **ambiguous**, read "could mean either", and it does not compile:

```cpp
long big = 3;
area(big);     // compile error: call to 'area' is ambiguous
```

A `long` is a bigger kind of whole number. Turning it into an `int` and turning it into a `double` are both standard conversions, so neither version wins.

### The string literal trap

That ranking hides a famous trap. A [[string literal|string-literal]] such as `"hi"`, text in double quotes, is not a `std::string`. C++ stores it as a row of `char`s and hands it over as a `const char*`: a pointer to its first character.

Now suppose there are two overloads, one for `bool` and one for `const std::string&`:

```cpp
std::string f(bool b)               { return "bool"; }
std::string f(const std::string& s) { return "string"; }

f("hi");       // "bool"!
```

Turning a pointer into a `bool` is a standard conversion (rank 3). Building a `std::string` from it is a user-defined conversion (rank 4). The standard conversion wins, so `f("hi")` quietly calls the `bool` version: any pointer that is not `nullptr` counts as `true`. No error and no warning.

The cure is an overload that takes `const char*` exactly. It wins because it is an exact match, and it can hand the work on to the string version:

```cpp
std::string f(const char* s) { return f(std::string(s)); }   // now f("hi") is "string"
```

`std::string(s)` builds a real `std::string` from the pointer, so the call inside picks the `const std::string&` version.

### Default arguments

Sometimes the difference between two versions is only a value the caller usually does not care about. A **default argument** is a value written into the parameter list, used whenever the caller leaves that argument out:

```cpp
std::string greet(const std::string& name, const std::string& greeting = "Hello") {
    return greeting + ", " + name;
}
greet("Ada");            // "Hello, Ada"
greet("Ada", "Hi");      // "Hi, Ada"
```

Two rules:

- Defaults go on the **rightmost** parameters only. The caller leaves arguments off the end, so C++ fills them in from the right.
- A default is written **once**. If the function has a separate [[declaration|declaration-once]] (a line announcing it, without its body), the default goes there and not on the body.

Use defaults for the common case. Use overloads when the *work* differs by type.

### Two tools for padding text

The task pads text on the left, which needs two more things.

`std::string(n, c)` makes a string of `n` copies of the character `c`:

```cpp
std::string stars(3, '*');    // "***"
```

And `s.size()` is a `std::size_t`, which is unsigned: it can never be negative. So `width - s.size()` goes badly wrong when `s` is already longer than `width`: instead of a negative number, it wraps round to a gigantic positive one. Check `s.size() >= width` first, and only subtract when the answer cannot be negative.

One last thing you will see in the task's starter: `describe(double)` builds its text with a [[string stream|to-string-double]] rather than `std::to_string`. It already works, so leave it as it is.

**Watch out:** the literal trap bites whenever a function has a `bool` overload (or any other overload a pointer converts to) next to a `std::string` one. Test the overloads with a plain `"text"` argument, not only with a `std::string` variable, because that is how people will call them.

::: context name-mangling How one name becomes several
The computer that runs your program never sees the name `area` on its own. The compiler gives each version a longer hidden name that spells out its parameter types. With g++ on Linux, `int area(int)` becomes `_Z4areai`, `int area(int, int)` becomes `_Z4areaii`, and `int area(double)` becomes `_Z4aread`: the `4` is the length of "area", and the letters after it are the parameter types. This is called **name mangling**. It is how the parts of a large program, compiled separately, still find the right version of each other's functions.
:::

::: context promotion Widening without losing anything
A promotion moves a value into a bigger type that can hold every value of the smaller one, so nothing is ever lost. A `char` is one byte and an `int` is usually four, so every `char` fits in an `int`. A `float` keeps about 7 significant digits and a `double` about 15, so every `float` fits in a `double`. A conversion like `double` to `int` is different: it can throw away the fraction or overflow, which is why C++ ranks it lower than a promotion.
:::

::: context string-literal What "hi" really is
In memory, `"hi"` is three bytes in a row: `'h'`, `'i'`, and a hidden zero byte that marks the end (the basics course mentioned it in the strings lesson). Its type is "array of 3 const chars". When you pass an array to a function, C++ passes a pointer to its first element instead, a rule called **decay**. That is how `"hi"` arrives as a `const char*`, and why it can turn into a `bool`.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="150" y="30" width="50" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="200" y="30" width="50" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="250" y="30" width="50" height="40" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="175" y="55" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">'h'</text>
  <text x="225" y="55" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">'i'</text>
  <text x="275" y="55" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">'\0'</text>
  <text x="275" y="90" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">end marker</text>
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="60" y="55" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">const char*</text>
  <line x1="100" y1="50" x2="144" y2="50" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="144,45 150,50 144,55" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="118" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">the pointer holds the address of the first char</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context declaration-once Declarations and definitions
A **declaration** tells the compiler a function exists: `std::string greet(const std::string& name, const std::string& greeting = "Hello");`, ending in a semicolon. The **definition** is the version with the body in braces. In bigger programs the declarations live in header files, so every part of the program can see them, and that is exactly where callers need to see the defaults. Writing the default again on the definition is a compile error, "redefinition of default argument". When a function has no separate declaration, as in the task, the default goes on the definition itself.
:::

::: context to-string-double Why describe uses a string stream
The starter's `describe(double)` builds its text with a `std::ostringstream`, an output string stream: `<<` writes into it the same way it writes to `std::cout`, and `.str()` hands back the text. It uses one because `std::to_string(2.5)` gives `"2.500000"`, always six digits after the point, while `<<` prints `2.5`, the way you would write it. The course comes back to string streams in "Processing text with string streams".
:::
--- task
The starter has four overloads of `describe`, for `int`, `double`, `bool` and `const std::string&`. Each returns the type's name, a space, and the value: `"int 42"`, `"double 2.5"`, `"bool true"`, `"string hi"`.

But `describe("hi")` returns `"bool true"`, because of the string literal trap. Add the overload that makes `describe("hi")` return `"string hi"`, and keep the other four working.

Then finish `std::string pad_left(const std::string& s, std::size_t width, char fill = ' ')`. It returns `s` with `fill` characters added in front until it is `width` characters long, so `pad_left("42", 5, '0')` is `"00042"`. If `s` is already `width` long or longer, return it unchanged. When no `fill` is given, it is a space: `pad_left("7", 3)` is `"  7"`. The starter's version has no default yet.

No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string describe(int n) { return "int " + std::to_string(n); }
std::string describe(double d) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << "double " << d;
    return out.str();
}
std::string describe(bool b) { return std::string("bool ") + (b ? "true" : "false"); }
std::string describe(const std::string& s) { return "string " + s; }

std::string pad_left(const std::string& s, std::size_t width, char fill) {
    return s;
}
--- solution
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string describe(int n) { return "int " + std::to_string(n); }
std::string describe(double d) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << "double " << d;
    return out.str();
}
std::string describe(bool b) { return std::string("bool ") + (b ? "true" : "false"); }
std::string describe(const std::string& s) { return "string " + s; }
std::string describe(const char* s) { return describe(std::string(s)); }

std::string pad_left(const std::string& s, std::size_t width, char fill = ' ') {
    if (s.size() >= width) return s;
    return std::string(width - s.size(), fill) + s;
}
--- hint
A string literal arrives as a `const char*`. Add a fifth `describe` that takes exactly a `const char* s`, so it is an exact match.
--- hint
Inside it, hand the work to the string version: `return describe(std::string(s));`.
--- hint
For `pad_left`, write the default into the parameter list: `char fill = ' '`. Return `s` when `s.size() >= width`; otherwise return `std::string(width - s.size(), fill) + s`.
--- check case | describe("hi") is a string
describe("hi")
=> "string hi"
--- check test | The other overloads still work
describe(42) == "int 42" && describe(2.5) == "double 2.5" && describe(false) == "bool false" && describe(std::string("yo")) == "string yo"
--- check case | pad_left uses spaces by default
pad_left("7", 3)
=> "  7"
--- check case | pad_left with a fill character
pad_left("42", 5, '0')
=> "00042"
--- check case | pad_left never cuts
pad_left("toolong", 3)
=> "toolong"

+++ practice | Repeat a character or a word
--- task
Write two overloads of `repeat`. No `main`.

- `std::string repeat(char c, int n)` returns `n` copies of the character `c`: `repeat('-', 4)` is `"----"`.
- `std::string repeat(const std::string& s, int n)` returns `n` copies of `s` joined together: `repeat("ab", 3)` is `"ababab"`.

Both return `""` when `n` is 0 or negative. A call with a string literal, like `repeat("ab", 3)`, must pick the string version.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string repeat(const std::string& s, int n) {
    return s;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string repeat(char c, int n) {
    if (n <= 0) return "";
    return std::string(n, c);
}

std::string repeat(const std::string& s, int n) {
    std::string out;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) out += s;
    return out;
}
--- hint
Two functions with the same name and different parameter types are two overloads. The compiler picks by the type of the first argument: `'-'` is a `char`, `"ab"` is not.
--- hint
For the `char` version, `std::string(n, c)` makes `n` copies, but check `n <= 0` first. For the string version, add `s` to an empty string `n` times in a loop; a loop that never runs gives `""`.
--- check test | repeat with a char
repeat('-', 4) == "----" && repeat('x', 1) == "x"
--- check test | repeat with a word, even from a literal
repeat("ab", 3) == "ababab" && repeat(std::string("go"), 2) == "gogo"
--- check test | Zero and negative counts give nothing
repeat('*', 0) == "" && repeat('*', -3) == "" && repeat("ab", 0) == "" && repeat("ab", -1) == ""

+++ practice | Centre the text, with a default fill
--- task
Write `std::string center(const std::string& s, std::size_t width, char fill = ' ')`. It returns `s` with `fill` characters added on both sides until it is `width` long. When the padding cannot be split evenly, the extra character goes on the **right**. If `s` is already `width` long or longer, return it unchanged. No `main`.

- `center("go", 6, '*')` is `"**go**"`.
- `center("go", 5, '*')` is `"*go**"`.
- `center("abc", 7)` is `"  abc  "`, with spaces, because `fill` defaults to a space.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string center(const std::string& s, std::size_t width, char fill) {
    std::size_t pad = width - s.size();
    return std::string(pad / 2, fill) + s + std::string(pad / 2, fill);
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string center(const std::string& s, std::size_t width, char fill = ' ') {
    if (s.size() >= width) return s;
    std::size_t pad = width - s.size();
    std::size_t left = pad / 2;
    return std::string(left, fill) + s + std::string(pad - left, fill);
}
--- hint
Write the default right in the parameter list: `char fill = ' '`. And check `s.size() >= width` before you subtract anything, because `width - s.size()` cannot go below zero.
--- hint
The left side gets `pad / 2` characters and the right side gets the rest, `pad - pad / 2`, which is one more when `pad` is odd.
--- check case | Even padding
center("go", 6, '*')
=> "**go**"
--- check case | The extra character goes on the right
center("go", 5, '*')
=> "*go**"
--- check case | Spaces by default
center("abc", 7)
=> "  abc  "
--- check case | Already wide enough
center("toolong", 3)
=> "toolong"
--- check case | Empty text
center("", 3, '.')
=> "..."

+++ practice | One name for three kinds of total
--- task
Write three overloads of `total`, all taking their containers by `const` reference. No `main`.

- `int total(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns the values added up.
- `int total(const std::map<std::string, int>& m)` returns the map's **values** added up.
- `int total(const std::vector<int>& v, int cap)` adds up the values, but any value above `cap` counts as `cap`.

`total({4, 9, 2})` is 15, `total(std::map<std::string, int>{{"a", 5}, {"b", 7}})` is 12, and `total({4, 9, 2}, 5)` is 4 + 5 + 2 = 11. All three give 0 for an empty container.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <numeric>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int total(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <numeric>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int total(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0);
}

int total(const std::map<std::string, int>& m) {
    int sum = 0;
    for (const auto& [name, value] : m) sum += value;
    return sum;
}

int total(const std::vector<int>& v, int cap) {
    int sum = 0;
    for (int x : v) sum += x > cap ? cap : x;
    return sum;
}
--- hint
Each overload is its own function with its own body. The compiler tells them apart by the parameter types and by how many there are.
--- hint
For the map, walk its pairs with `for (const auto& [name, value] : m)` and add up `value`. For the capped one, add `x > cap ? cap : x` for each value.
--- check case | A vector
total({4, 9, 2})
=> 15
--- check case | A map's values
total(std::map<std::string, int>{{"a", 5}, {"b", 7}})
=> 12
--- check case | Capped values
total({4, 9, 2}, 5)
=> 11
--- check test | Empty containers total 0
total(std::vector<int>{}) == 0 && total(std::map<std::string, int>{}) == 0 && total(std::vector<int>{}, 3) == 0

+++ practice | Cut long text with an ellipsis
--- task
Write `std::string shorten(const std::string& s, std::size_t max_len, const std::string& ellipsis = "...")`. It makes sure the result is at most `max_len` characters. No `main`.

- If `s` fits (its size is at most `max_len`), return `s` unchanged.
- Otherwise, cut `s` and add `ellipsis`, so that the whole result is exactly `max_len` long: `shorten("telemetry", 7)` is `"tele..."`.
- If `max_len` is not even bigger than the ellipsis, there is no room for any of `s`: return just the first `max_len` characters of `ellipsis`. So `shorten("telemetry", 2)` is `".."` and `shorten("telemetry", 0)` is `""`.

`shorten("telemetry", 6, "~")` is `"telem~"`. Watch the unsigned subtraction: `max_len - ellipsis.size()` wraps round when `max_len` is the smaller one.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string shorten(const std::string& s, std::size_t max_len, const std::string& ellipsis = "...") {
    return s.substr(0, max_len - ellipsis.size()) + ellipsis;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string shorten(const std::string& s, std::size_t max_len, const std::string& ellipsis = "...") {
    if (s.size() <= max_len) return s;
    if (max_len <= ellipsis.size()) return ellipsis.substr(0, max_len);
    return s.substr(0, max_len - ellipsis.size()) + ellipsis;
}
--- hint
Three cases, in this order: `s` fits; there is no room for any of `s`; the normal cut.
--- hint
Only subtract `max_len - ellipsis.size()` once you know `max_len` is bigger than `ellipsis.size()`, so the answer cannot wrap round.
--- check case | Short text is untouched
shorten("fuel", 7)
=> "fuel"
--- check case | Exactly the maximum is untouched
shorten("oxidizer", 8)
=> "oxidizer"
--- check case | Cut with the default ellipsis
shorten("telemetry", 7)
=> "tele..."
--- check case | A custom ellipsis
shorten("telemetry", 6, "~")
=> "telem~"
--- check case | No room for any text
shorten("telemetry", 2)
=> ".."
--- check case | A maximum of 0
shorten("telemetry", 0)
=> ""

+++ practice | Debug: right-aligning crashes on long names
--- task
**Bug report:** "`right_align` works for short names, but the program crashes when a name is longer than the width."

`right_align(s, width)` should put spaces in front of `s` until it is `width` long, and return `s` unchanged when it is already `width` long or longer. Find the cause and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

// Spaces in front of s until it is width characters long.
std::string right_align(const std::string& s, std::size_t width) {
    return std::string(width - s.size(), ' ') + s;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

// Spaces in front of s until it is width characters long.
std::string right_align(const std::string& s, std::size_t width) {
    if (s.size() >= width) return s;
    return std::string(width - s.size(), ' ') + s;
}
--- hint
Trace a small failing case: `s` is `"Kathryn"` (7 characters) and `width` is 4. What is `width - s.size()` for an unsigned type?
--- hint
It wraps round to a gigantic number, and a string that long cannot be made. Return `s` before subtracting whenever `s.size() >= width`.
--- check case | A short name
right_align("Mae", 6)
=> "   Mae"
--- check case | Exactly the width
right_align("Yuri", 4)
=> "Yuri"
--- check case | Longer than the width
right_align("Kathryn", 4)
=> "Kathryn"
--- check case | Width 0
right_align("", 0)
=> ""

+++ practice | Stretch: a launch countdown clock
--- task
Write a small countdown display with overloads and a default argument. No `main`.

- `std::string countdown(int seconds, const std::string& prefix = "T")` shows the time to launch as the prefix, a sign, then minutes and seconds with two digits each. Before launch (a positive number) the sign is `-`; at launch and after (0 or negative) it is `+`, and the time shown is how long ago. So `countdown(125)` is `"T-02:05"`, `countdown(0)` is `"T+00:00"`, `countdown(-7)` is `"T+00:07"`, and `countdown(90, "L")` is `"L-01:30"`.
- `std::string countdown(int minutes, int seconds)` is the same display for `minutes * 60 + seconds` seconds, with the default prefix: `countdown(2, 5)` is `"T-02:05"`.

Minutes can go past 99: `countdown(6000)` is `"T-100:00"`. Only numbers below 10 get a `0` in front.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string countdown(int seconds, const std::string& prefix = "T") {
    return prefix + "-" + std::to_string(seconds / 60) + ":" + std::to_string(seconds % 60);
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string two_digits(int n) {
    return (n < 10 ? "0" : "") + std::to_string(n);
}

std::string countdown(int seconds, const std::string& prefix = "T") {
    std::string sign = seconds > 0 ? "-" : "+";
    int left = seconds > 0 ? seconds : -seconds;
    return prefix + sign + two_digits(left / 60) + ":" + two_digits(left % 60);
}

std::string countdown(int minutes, int seconds) {
    return countdown(minutes * 60 + seconds);
}
--- hint
Work with the size of the number and remember the sign separately: the sign is `"-"` when `seconds > 0`, and the time shown is `seconds` without its minus sign.
--- hint
A small helper that turns a number into text with a `"0"` in front when it is below 10 keeps both parts tidy. Minutes are `left / 60` and seconds `left % 60`.
--- hint
The second overload only works out the total and calls the first: `return countdown(minutes * 60 + seconds);`. The call `countdown(90, "L")` still picks the first one, because a string literal cannot become an `int`.
--- check test | Before launch
countdown(125) == "T-02:05" && countdown(2, 5) == "T-02:05" && countdown(59) == "T-00:59"
--- check test | At launch and after
countdown(0) == "T+00:00" && countdown(-7) == "T+00:07" && countdown(-3600) == "T+60:00"
--- check test | A custom prefix from a literal
countdown(90, "L") == "L-01:30" && countdown(-61, std::string("E")) == "E+01:01"
--- check case | Minutes past 99
countdown(6000)
=> "T-100:00"

=== cpp2-08 | enum class
--- teach
Last lesson you gave several functions one name and let the compiler pick by type. This lesson makes a new kind of type: one whose values are a short list of names you choose.

### A value that can only be one of a few things

A traffic light is red, amber or green. Nothing else. A compass direction is north, east, south or west. A rocket is on the pad, climbing, coasting or landed.

You could store these as numbers, 0 for red, 1 for amber, 2 for green, but then nothing stops a `7` sneaking in, and nobody reading `if (light == 2)` knows what 2 means. Better to give the values names and let the compiler check them.

An **enumeration** is a type whose values are a fixed list of names. Each name in the list is an **enumerator**. Modern C++ writes one with `enum class`:

```cpp
enum class Light { Red, Amber, Green };

Light now = Light::Red;
```

The first line makes a new type, `Light`, with exactly three values, and ends with `};` like a struct. The second makes a `Light` variable. `Light::Red` uses the scope operator `::`, read "from", as in `std::cout`: "Red from Light".

### Scoped names

The names live *inside* the enum, which is what "class" in `enum class` gives you: they are **scoped**. You must write `Light::Red`, never plain `Red`, so two enums can both have a `Red` without clashing:

```cpp
enum class Light { Red, Amber, Green };
enum class Alarm { Green, Red };      // fine: Light::Red and Alarm::Red are different
```

The [[older plain enum|plain-enum]] does not do this, which is one reason modern code prefers `enum class`.

### A number underneath, but not an int

Underneath, each enumerator is a whole number, counting from 0 in the order you listed them: `Red` is 0, `Amber` is 1, `Green` is 2.

But an `enum class` does **not** silently turn into an `int`. This does not compile:

```cpp
if (now == 2) { }    // compile error: a Light is not an int
```

That error catches real mistakes. When you do want the number, ask for it with `static_cast`, and turn a number back into the enum the same way:

```cpp
int n = static_cast<int>(Light::Amber);     // 1
Light l = static_cast<Light>(2);            // Light::Green
```

### switch: one branch for each value

To act on each value, use a **switch**. It compares one value with a list of `case` labels and jumps straight to the one that matches:

```cpp
std::string meaning(Light l) {
    switch (l) {
        case Light::Red:   return "stop";
        case Light::Amber: return "wait";
        case Light::Green: return "go";
    }
    return "";
}
```

Read it as: "look at `l`; in the case it is `Light::Red`, return `"stop"`", and so on. Each label ends with a colon `:`.

A case that does not `return` must end with **break**, which jumps out of the switch. Without it, the code **falls through**: it carries on running the next case's code too.

```cpp
switch (l) {
    case Light::Red:   std::cout << "stop\n"; break;
    case Light::Amber: std::cout << "wait\n"; break;
    case Light::Green: std::cout << "go\n";   break;
}
```

A `default:` label catches any value that no case lists.

The `return "";` after the first switch is never reached, because every value has a case. It is there because a `Light` can be forced to hold a number that is not on the list, such as `static_cast<Light>(7)`, and some compilers (g++, for one) warn that the function could end without returning anything.

### A free check from the compiler

Leave out the `default:` label and the compiler checks your cases for you. If you forget one, clang and g++ both warn, with a warning called `-Wswitch`:

```
warning: enumeration value 'Amber' not handled in switch [-Wswitch]
```

That is a [[free check every time|wswitch-check]] someone adds a new value to the enum later: every switch that forgot it lights up.

### Turning through the list with arithmetic

Sometimes the order of the list means something. For compass directions listed as North, East, South, West, [[turning right is moving one step along the list|compass-numbers]], and turning left is one step back. From West, turning right wraps round to North.

That is the wrapping you did on the clock, with 4 in place of 1440. Turn the enum into its number, add, wrap with `% 4`, and turn it back:

```cpp
enum class Direction { North, East, South, West };

Direction right_of_west = static_cast<Direction>((static_cast<int>(Direction::West) + 1) % 4);
// West is 3; 3 + 1 is 4; 4 % 4 is 0: North
```

To step back one, add 3 instead of subtracting 1. Three steps forward round a circle of four is the same as one step back, and it never goes negative. From North: 0 + 3 is 3, and 3 % 4 is 3, which is West. Subtracting would give 0 - 1, which is -1, and `-1 % 4` is `-1` in C++, which is not a direction at all.

### An enum inside a struct

An enum is a type like any other, so a struct can have one as a member, the way a spacecraft's record of its state might hold its [[current mode|flight-modes]] next to its position:

```cpp
struct Position {
    int x;
    int y;
    Direction facing;
};

Position p{0, 0, Direction::North};   // at (0, 0), facing North
p.y++;                                // one step north: now at (0, 1)
p.facing = Direction::East;
```

On a grid, a step North is `y + 1`, South is `y - 1`, East is `x + 1` and West is `x - 1`.

**Watch out:** forgetting `break` at the end of a case. The code compiles, and the program runs the next case as well. If the North case in a robot's switch forgot its `break`, an `'F'` while facing North would move the robot north *and then* east. Every case ends in `break` or `return`.

::: context plain-enum The older plain enum
Before C++11, C++ only had the plain `enum`, which it inherited from C: `enum Light { Red, Amber, Green };`. Its names spill out into the surrounding code, so you write plain `Red`, and a second enum with a `Red` in it will not compile. Its values also turn into `int` without asking, so `if (light == 2)` compiles happily. `enum class`, added in C++11, fixed both. You will still meet plain enums in older code and in C libraries.
:::

::: context wswitch-check Why leaving out default helps
A `default:` label means "anything else", so it also catches a value you forgot, silently. Without one, the compiler knows the full list of enumerators and can tell you which ones have no case. This matters most in big programs: add `Flashing` to `Light` a year from now, and every switch on a `Light`, in every file, is flagged at the next build. The app compiles with `-Wall`, which turns this warning on. Flight-software teams often go further and treat every warning as an error, so the program will not build until each new value is handled everywhere.
:::

::: context compass-numbers Four directions, four numbers
Put the numbers round a circle and turning becomes counting: right is +1, left is +3, and `% 4` brings 4 back to 0. The same idea works for any list that goes round in a loop, like the days of the week (`% 7`) or the clock's minutes (`% 1440`).

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <circle cx="180" cy="100" r="60" fill="none" stroke="#8fb8f0" stroke-width="3"/>
  <circle cx="180" cy="40" r="16" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="240" cy="100" r="16" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="180" cy="160" r="16" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="120" cy="100" r="16" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="45" font-size="13" fill="#ffffff" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="240" y="105" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="165" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <text x="120" y="105" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3</text>
  <text x="180" y="16" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">North</text>
  <text x="264" y="104" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">East</text>
  <text x="180" y="194" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">South</text>
  <text x="96" y="104" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="end">West</text>
  <text x="300" y="40" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">right: +1</text>
  <text x="60" y="40" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">left: +3</text>
  <text x="300" y="170" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">then % 4</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context flight-modes Modes on a spacecraft
Spacecraft software is often organized around a mode: a value from a short list such as launch, cruise, science and safe mode, with a switch that decides what the craft does in each one. **Safe mode** is the famous one: when something unexpected happens, the craft stops its normal work, turns its solar panels towards the Sun, keeps in touch with Earth, and waits for instructions. An `enum class` with a switch that the compiler checks is exactly how such a list of modes is written in modern C++.
:::
--- task
The starter declares:

```cpp
enum class Direction { North, East, South, West };
struct Position { int x; int y; Direction facing; };
```

Write these functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `Direction turn_right(Direction d)`: the next direction clockwise. `turn_right(Direction::West)` is `Direction::North`.
- `Direction turn_left(Direction d)`: the next direction the other way. `turn_left(Direction::North)` is `Direction::West`.
- `std::string direction_name(Direction d)`: the name as text, `"North"`, `"East"`, `"South"` or `"West"`.
- `Position follow(const std::string& commands)`: a robot starts at (0, 0) facing North and obeys each character of `commands` in turn. `'L'` turns it left and `'R'` turns it right. `'F'` moves it one step forward: North is `y + 1`, East is `x + 1`, South is `y - 1` and West is `x - 1`. Ignore any other character. Return where it ends up, and which way it faces. `follow("FFRFF")` ends at (2, 2) facing East.
--- starter
#include <string>

enum class Direction { North, East, South, West };

struct Position {
    int x;
    int y;
    Direction facing;
};

Direction turn_right(Direction d) { return d; }
Direction turn_left(Direction d) { return d; }
std::string direction_name(Direction d) { return ""; }

Position follow(const std::string& commands) {
    return Position{0, 0, Direction::North};
}
--- solution
#include <string>

enum class Direction { North, East, South, West };

struct Position {
    int x;
    int y;
    Direction facing;
};

Direction turn_right(Direction d) { return static_cast<Direction>((static_cast<int>(d) + 1) % 4); }
Direction turn_left(Direction d) { return static_cast<Direction>((static_cast<int>(d) + 3) % 4); }

std::string direction_name(Direction d) {
    switch (d) {
        case Direction::North: return "North";
        case Direction::East:  return "East";
        case Direction::South: return "South";
        case Direction::West:  return "West";
    }
    return "";
}

Position follow(const std::string& commands) {
    Position p{0, 0, Direction::North};
    for (char c : commands) {
        if (c == 'L') {
            p.facing = turn_left(p.facing);
        } else if (c == 'R') {
            p.facing = turn_right(p.facing);
        } else if (c == 'F') {
            switch (p.facing) {
                case Direction::North: p.y++; break;
                case Direction::East:  p.x++; break;
                case Direction::South: p.y--; break;
                case Direction::West:  p.x--; break;
            }
        }
    }
    return p;
}
--- hint
Turning right is "one step along the list": turn `d` into its number, add 1, wrap with `% 4`, and turn it back. Turning left adds 3 instead of 1.
--- hint
`direction_name` is a switch on `d` with one `case` per direction, each returning its name, and a `return "";` after the switch.
--- hint
In `follow`, make `Position p{0, 0, Direction::North};` and loop over the characters. For `'L'` and `'R'`, set `p.facing` with your turn functions. For `'F'`, a switch on `p.facing` changes `p.x` or `p.y`, with `break` after each case. Return `p` at the end.
--- check test | Turning right goes round the compass
turn_right(Direction::North) == Direction::East && turn_right(Direction::West) == Direction::North
--- check test | Turning left goes the other way
turn_left(Direction::North) == Direction::West && turn_left(Direction::East) == Direction::North
--- check case | direction_name
direction_name(turn_left(Direction::South))
=> "East"
--- check test | FFRFF ends at (2, 2) facing East
[] { Position p = follow("FFRFF"); return p.x == 2 && p.y == 2 && p.facing == Direction::East; }()
--- check test | A full square comes home
[] { Position p = follow("FRFRFRFR"); return p.x == 0 && p.y == 0 && p.facing == Direction::North; }()
--- check test | Other characters are ignored
[] { Position p = follow("L?F F"); return p.x == -2 && p.y == 0 && p.facing == Direction::West; }()

+++ practice | The phases of a flight
--- task
The starter declares the phases of a rocket flight, in order:

```cpp
enum class Phase { Pad, Ascent, Coast, Descent, Landed };
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::string phase_name(Phase p)` returns the name as text: `"Pad"`, `"Ascent"`, `"Coast"`, `"Descent"` or `"Landed"`. Use a `switch`.
- `Phase next_phase(Phase p)` returns the phase that comes after `p`. `Landed` is the last one, so the phase after `Landed` is `Landed` again: it does **not** wrap round to `Pad`.
--- starter
#include <string>

enum class Phase { Pad, Ascent, Coast, Descent, Landed };

std::string phase_name(Phase p) {
    return "";
}

Phase next_phase(Phase p) {
    return static_cast<Phase>((static_cast<int>(p) + 1) % 5);
}
--- solution
#include <string>

enum class Phase { Pad, Ascent, Coast, Descent, Landed };

std::string phase_name(Phase p) {
    switch (p) {
        case Phase::Pad:     return "Pad";
        case Phase::Ascent:  return "Ascent";
        case Phase::Coast:   return "Coast";
        case Phase::Descent: return "Descent";
        case Phase::Landed:  return "Landed";
    }
    return "";
}

Phase next_phase(Phase p) {
    if (p == Phase::Landed) return Phase::Landed;
    return static_cast<Phase>(static_cast<int>(p) + 1);
}
--- hint
`phase_name` is a `switch` with one `case` per phase, each returning its name, and a `return "";` after the switch.
--- hint
For `next_phase`, deal with `Phase::Landed` first. Every other phase turns into its number, adds 1, and turns back with `static_cast<Phase>`.
--- check test | Every name
phase_name(Phase::Pad) == "Pad" && phase_name(Phase::Ascent) == "Ascent" && phase_name(Phase::Coast) == "Coast" && phase_name(Phase::Descent) == "Descent" && phase_name(Phase::Landed) == "Landed"
--- check test | Phases step forward
next_phase(Phase::Pad) == Phase::Ascent && next_phase(Phase::Descent) == Phase::Landed
--- check test | Landed stays Landed
next_phase(Phase::Landed) == Phase::Landed
--- check case | Three steps from the pad
phase_name(next_phase(next_phase(next_phase(Phase::Pad))))
=> "Descent"

+++ practice | Commands from single letters
--- task
A lift for a launch tower takes one-letter commands. The starter declares:

```cpp
enum class Command { Up, Down, Hold, Unknown };
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `Command parse_command(char c)` turns a letter into a command: `'U'` is `Up`, `'D'` is `Down` and `'H'` is `Hold`, in upper or lower case. Any other character is `Unknown`.
- `int apply(int floor, Command c)` returns the floor after the command: `Up` adds 1, `Down` takes 1 away, but never below floor 0, and `Hold` and `Unknown` leave it alone. Use a `switch`.

`apply(3, parse_command('d'))` is 2, and `apply(0, Command::Down)` is 0.
--- starter
#include <cctype>

enum class Command { Up, Down, Hold, Unknown };

Command parse_command(char c) {
    return Command::Unknown;
}

int apply(int floor, Command c) {
    return floor;
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>

enum class Command { Up, Down, Hold, Unknown };

Command parse_command(char c) {
    char u = static_cast<char>(std::toupper(static_cast<unsigned char>(c)));
    if (u == 'U') return Command::Up;
    if (u == 'D') return Command::Down;
    if (u == 'H') return Command::Hold;
    return Command::Unknown;
}

int apply(int floor, Command c) {
    switch (c) {
        case Command::Up:
            return floor + 1;
        case Command::Down:
            return floor > 0 ? floor - 1 : 0;
        case Command::Hold:
        case Command::Unknown:
            return floor;
    }
    return floor;
}
--- hint
Turn the letter into upper case first, with `std::toupper` on the `unsigned char`, as in the strings lesson. Then compare it with `'U'`, `'D'` and `'H'`.
--- hint
In `apply`, each case can `return` straight away. Two labels in a row, `case Command::Hold:` then `case Command::Unknown:`, share the code that follows them.
--- check test | Letters in both cases
parse_command('U') == Command::Up && parse_command('d') == Command::Down && parse_command('h') == Command::Hold
--- check test | Anything else is Unknown
parse_command('x') == Command::Unknown && parse_command(' ') == Command::Unknown && parse_command('7') == Command::Unknown
--- check test | apply moves the floor
apply(3, parse_command('d')) == 2 && apply(3, Command::Up) == 4 && apply(3, Command::Hold) == 3 && apply(3, Command::Unknown) == 3
--- check case | Never below floor 0
apply(0, Command::Down)
=> 0

+++ practice | The worst sensor on the board
--- task
The starter declares:

```cpp
enum class Status { Ok, Warning, Fault };
struct Sensor {
    std::string name;
    Status status;
};
```

The statuses are listed from best to worst. Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int count_status(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors, Status s)` returns how many sensors have status `s`. Use `std::count_if` with a lambda that captures `s`.
- `Status worst(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors)` returns the worst status on the board, and `Status::Ok` when there are no sensors. Compare statuses by their numbers, with `static_cast<int>`.
- `std::vector<std::string> names_with(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors, Status s)` returns the names of the sensors with status `s`, in order.

For `{{"pump", Ok}, {"valve", Fault}, {"tank", Warning}, {"fan", Fault}}`: `count_status(..., Status::Fault)` is 2, `worst` is `Fault`, and `names_with(..., Status::Fault)` is `{"valve", "fan"}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

enum class Status { Ok, Warning, Fault };

struct Sensor {
    std::string name;
    Status status;
};

int count_status(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors, Status s) {
    return 0;
}

Status worst(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors) {
    return sensors.empty() ? Status::Ok : sensors[0].status;
}

std::vector<std::string> names_with(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors, Status s) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

enum class Status { Ok, Warning, Fault };

struct Sensor {
    std::string name;
    Status status;
};

int count_status(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors, Status s) {
    return static_cast<int>(std::count_if(sensors.begin(), sensors.end(),
                                          [s](const Sensor& x) { return x.status == s; }));
}

Status worst(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors) {
    Status result = Status::Ok;
    for (const auto& x : sensors) {
        if (static_cast<int>(x.status) > static_cast<int>(result)) result = x.status;
    }
    return result;
}

std::vector<std::string> names_with(const std::vector<Sensor>& sensors, Status s) {
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (const auto& x : sensors) {
        if (x.status == s) names.push_back(x.name);
    }
    return names;
}
--- hint
Two statuses can be compared with `==` directly. To ask which is worse, compare their numbers: `static_cast<int>(a) > static_cast<int>(b)`.
--- hint
For `worst`, start from `Status::Ok`, the best there is, and replace it whenever a sensor's status has a bigger number. That also gives `Ok` for an empty board.
--- check case | Two faults
count_status({{"pump", Status::Ok}, {"valve", Status::Fault}, {"tank", Status::Warning}, {"fan", Status::Fault}}, Status::Fault)
=> 2
--- check test | The worst status
worst({{"pump", Status::Ok}, {"tank", Status::Warning}, {"valve", Status::Fault}}) == Status::Fault && worst({{"pump", Status::Warning}, {"fan", Status::Ok}}) == Status::Warning
--- check test | No sensors: all is Ok
worst({}) == Status::Ok && count_status({}, Status::Ok) == 0
--- check case | Names in order
names_with({{"pump", Status::Ok}, {"valve", Status::Fault}, {"tank", Status::Warning}, {"fan", Status::Fault}}, Status::Fault)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"valve", "fan"}
--- check case | No sensor with that status
names_with({{"pump", Status::Ok}}, Status::Warning)
=> std::vector<std::string>{}

+++ practice | Days of the week, forwards and back
--- task
The starter declares:

```cpp
enum class Weekday { Mon, Tue, Wed, Thu, Fri, Sat, Sun };
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `Weekday add_days(Weekday d, int n)` returns the day `n` days after `d`. `n` can be 0, negative (days before), or very large. `add_days(Weekday::Fri, 3)` is `Mon`, `add_days(Weekday::Mon, -1)` is `Sun`, and `add_days(Weekday::Wed, -15)` is `Tue`.
- `bool is_weekend(Weekday d)` returns `true` for `Sat` and `Sun` only.

The trick of adding 3 instead of taking 1 away works for one step back, but not for any `n`. Use the wrapping formula from the classes lesson instead.
--- starter
enum class Weekday { Mon, Tue, Wed, Thu, Fri, Sat, Sun };

Weekday add_days(Weekday d, int n) {
    return static_cast<Weekday>((static_cast<int>(d) + n) % 7);
}

bool is_weekend(Weekday d) {
    return d == Weekday::Sun;
}
--- solution
enum class Weekday { Mon, Tue, Wed, Thu, Fri, Sat, Sun };

Weekday add_days(Weekday d, int n) {
    int x = static_cast<int>(d) + n % 7;
    return static_cast<Weekday>(((x % 7) + 7) % 7);
}

bool is_weekend(Weekday d) {
    return d == Weekday::Sat || d == Weekday::Sun;
}
--- hint
`% 7` keeps the sign of the left side, so `(0 - 1) % 7` is -1, which is not a day. Wrap with `((x % 7) + 7) % 7`.
--- hint
Taking `n % 7` first, before adding it to the day's number, keeps every number small even when `n` is huge.
--- check test | Forwards, past the end of the week
add_days(Weekday::Fri, 3) == Weekday::Mon && add_days(Weekday::Sun, 1) == Weekday::Mon
--- check test | Backwards
add_days(Weekday::Mon, -1) == Weekday::Sun && add_days(Weekday::Wed, -15) == Weekday::Tue
--- check test | Zero, whole weeks, and huge numbers
add_days(Weekday::Thu, 0) == Weekday::Thu && add_days(Weekday::Thu, 70) == Weekday::Thu && add_days(Weekday::Mon, 2000000000) == Weekday::Sat && add_days(Weekday::Mon, -2000000000) == Weekday::Wed
--- check test | Weekends
is_weekend(Weekday::Sat) && is_weekend(Weekday::Sun) && !is_weekend(Weekday::Fri) && !is_weekend(Weekday::Mon)

+++ practice | Debug: the nudge that burned too long
--- task
**Bug report:** "`burn_seconds(Maneuver::Nudge)` gives 10, but a nudge is a 2-second burn. The other two are right."

The starter declares `enum class Maneuver { Nudge, Turn, Boost };`. `burn_seconds` should give 2 for `Nudge`, 10 for `Turn` and 30 for `Boost`, and `total_burn` adds up the seconds for a whole list of maneuvers. Find the cause and fix it; it is one line. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

enum class Maneuver { Nudge, Turn, Boost };

int burn_seconds(Maneuver m) {
    int s = 0;
    switch (m) {
        case Maneuver::Nudge: s = 2;
        case Maneuver::Turn:  s = 10; break;
        case Maneuver::Boost: s = 30; break;
    }
    return s;
}

int total_burn(const std::vector<Maneuver>& plan) {
    int total = 0;
    for (Maneuver m : plan) total += burn_seconds(m);
    return total;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

enum class Maneuver { Nudge, Turn, Boost };

int burn_seconds(Maneuver m) {
    int s = 0;
    switch (m) {
        case Maneuver::Nudge: s = 2;  break;
        case Maneuver::Turn:  s = 10; break;
        case Maneuver::Boost: s = 30; break;
    }
    return s;
}

int total_burn(const std::vector<Maneuver>& plan) {
    int total = 0;
    for (Maneuver m : plan) total += burn_seconds(m);
    return total;
}
--- hint
Trace `Maneuver::Nudge` through the switch line by line. After `s = 2;`, what runs next?
--- hint
Without `break`, the code falls through into the `Turn` case and sets `s` to 10. Every case must end in `break` or `return`.
--- check case | A nudge is 2 seconds
burn_seconds(Maneuver::Nudge)
=> 2
--- check test | Turn and Boost are still right
burn_seconds(Maneuver::Turn) == 10 && burn_seconds(Maneuver::Boost) == 30
--- check case | A whole plan
total_burn({Maneuver::Nudge, Maneuver::Boost, Maneuver::Nudge, Maneuver::Turn})
=> 44
--- check case | An empty plan
total_burn({})
=> 0

+++ practice | Stretch: an airlock that follows the rules
--- task
An airlock door is always in one of four states. The starter declares:

```cpp
enum class Door { Closed, Opening, Open, Closing };
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::string door_name(Door d)` returns `"Closed"`, `"Opening"`, `"Open"` or `"Closing"`.
- `Door run(const std::string& events)` starts with the door `Closed` and handles each character of `events` in turn:
  - `'o'` (the open button): a `Closed` or `Closing` door starts `Opening`. Any other state stays as it is.
  - `'c'` (the close button): an `Open` or `Opening` door starts `Closing`. Any other state stays as it is.
  - `'t'` (a tick of time): an `Opening` door becomes `Open`, and a `Closing` door becomes `Closed`. `Open` and `Closed` stay as they are.
  - Any other character is ignored.

It returns the state at the end. `run("ot")` is `Open`, `run("otc")` is `Closing`, and `run("oc")` is `Closing`.
--- starter
#include <string>

enum class Door { Closed, Opening, Open, Closing };

std::string door_name(Door d) {
    return "";
}

Door run(const std::string& events) {
    return Door::Closed;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

enum class Door { Closed, Opening, Open, Closing };

std::string door_name(Door d) {
    switch (d) {
        case Door::Closed:  return "Closed";
        case Door::Opening: return "Opening";
        case Door::Open:    return "Open";
        case Door::Closing: return "Closing";
    }
    return "";
}

Door step(Door d, char event) {
    if (event == 'o' && (d == Door::Closed || d == Door::Closing)) return Door::Opening;
    if (event == 'c' && (d == Door::Open || d == Door::Opening)) return Door::Closing;
    if (event == 't') {
        switch (d) {
            case Door::Opening: return Door::Open;
            case Door::Closing: return Door::Closed;
            case Door::Open:
            case Door::Closed:  return d;
        }
    }
    return d;
}

Door run(const std::string& events) {
    Door d = Door::Closed;
    for (char e : events) d = step(d, e);
    return d;
}
--- hint
Write a helper `Door step(Door d, char event)` that handles one event and returns the new state. Then `run` loops over the characters and keeps calling it.
--- hint
Each rule is a condition on the event and the current state, like `event == 'o' && (d == Door::Closed || d == Door::Closing)`. When no rule matches, return `d` unchanged.
--- check test | Opening, then open
run("o") == Door::Opening && run("ot") == Door::Open && run("ott") == Door::Open
--- check test | Closing from open, and from halfway
run("otc") == Door::Closing && run("otct") == Door::Closed && run("oc") == Door::Closing
--- check test | Buttons that do nothing
run("c") == Door::Closed && run("oto") == Door::Open && run("t") == Door::Closed
--- check test | Reopening while it closes, and noise ignored
run("otcox t") == Door::Open
--- check case | door_name of the end state
door_name(run("otcot c"))
=> "Closing"

=== cpp2-09 | std::optional: maybe a value
--- teach
Last lesson, `enum class` let a value be exactly one of a few named things. This lesson handles a different kind of answer: one that might not exist at all.

### When a function has no answer

Picture asking a librarian for a book. Usually they hand it to you. Sometimes the honest answer is "we do not have it". A function can be in the same spot. "Read this text as a number" has no answer for `"abc"`. "Find this name in the list" has no answer when the name is not there.

Older C++ code handles that with a **[[magic value|magic-values]]**: an ordinary-looking value, like `-1`, `0` or `""`, that everyone agrees to read as "no answer".

```cpp
int find_age(const std::string& name) {
    if (name == "Ada") return 36;
    return -1;   // magic value: "not found"
}
```

That works until `-1` is a real answer. A temperature of −1 degrees is a real reading, not "missing".

### A box that may be empty

Now picture a parcel with a window in it. You can see at a glance whether something is inside or whether it is empty. **`std::optional<T>`** is that parcel: a value that either holds one `T` or holds nothing, where `T` stands for any type you choose. Read `std::optional<int>` as "maybe an int". The type inside goes in angle brackets, the way it does in `std::vector<int>`, and it needs `#include <optional>`. Optional is one of the [[newer parts of C++|optional-history]].

```cpp
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> find_age(const std::string& name) {
    if (name == "Ada") return 36;    // a box holding 36
    return std::nullopt;             // an empty box
}
```

Returning `36` puts 36 in the box for you. **`std::nullopt`**, read "null optional", means the empty box: "no value".

### Looking inside

Before you use the value, ask whether there is one. An optional used as a condition is true when it holds a value:

```cpp
auto age = find_age("Bob");
if (age) {                  // true only when there is a value
    std::cout << *age;      // the star takes the value out
}
```

The star `*` in front, read "the value in", takes the value out of the box. It is the same symbol you used to [[follow a pointer|star-again]] in the basics course.

### A fallback value

Often you want "the value, or else this". **`value_or`** gives exactly that: the value if there is one, otherwise the fallback you hand it.

```cpp
int years = find_age("Bob").value_or(0);   // 0: Bob has no age
int ada = find_age("Ada").value_or(0);     // 36
```

### Comparing an optional

An optional compares directly with a value, and with `std::nullopt`:

```cpp
find_age("Ada") == 36             // true
find_age("Bob") == std::nullopt   // true
find_age("Bob") == 0              // false: an empty box equals no number
```

**Watch out:** never put `*` in front of an optional you have not checked. With nothing inside, `*age` is **undefined behavior**: C++ makes no promise about what happens, and [[no error message will tell you|empty-star]]. Check with `if (age)` first, or use `value_or`.

### Decide the edge cases first

Before you write a parser (a function that reads text and turns it into a value), decide which texts count. Is `""` a number? Is a lone `"-"`? Is `" 7"`, with a space in front? Write your answers down first. That is half the work, and each answer becomes a test.

The parser in this lesson accepts an optional minus sign, then one to nine digits, and nothing else. Nine is the limit because [[ten digits can overflow an int|nine-digits]].

### From digit characters to a number

The text `"42"` is two characters, `'4'` and `'2'`, not the number 42. You already know `std::isdigit`, from `<cctype>`, which is true for a digit character; convert to `unsigned char` first, as in the basics strings lesson.

To get a digit's value, subtract the character `'0'`. Because the digit characters are [[stored as numbers in order|digit-codes]], `'7' - '0'` is 7.

Then build the number from left to right: multiply what you have so far by 10 and add the next digit.

```cpp
std::string s = "472";
int value = 0;
for (char c : s) {
    value = value * 10 + (c - '0');
}
// value went 0, 4, 47, 472
```

For a leading minus, remember that you saw one, read the digits, and flip the sign at the end with the conditional operator from the algorithms lesson: `negative ? -value : value`.

An optional can hold any type. A function that returns `std::optional<std::size_t>` puts an index in the box with an ordinary `return i;`, and returns `std::nullopt` when there is no index to give.

::: context magic-values The trouble with magic values
A value that secretly means "no answer" is also called a *sentinel*. You have met one already: `std::string::npos`, the huge number `find` returns for "not found". That one is safe, because no real string is that long. Most sentinels are not safe. If `-1` means "missing", a real −1 gets mistaken for missing. Worse, a caller who forgets to check does arithmetic with the −1 as if it were real, and the compiler cannot help, because −1 is an ordinary `int`. An `std::optional<int>` cannot be added to an `int` at all: you must take the value out first, which forces you to think about the empty case.
:::

::: context optional-history Where optional came from
`std::optional` joined the standard library in C++17, the version of C++ published in 2017. It grew out of `boost::optional`, from the Boost libraries, a large free collection where many ideas are tried before they enter the standard. Other languages have the same idea under other names: Rust has `Option`, and Swift writes a maybe-int as `Int?`. The idea is always the same: the type itself says the value might be missing, so the missing case cannot be forgotten.
:::

::: context star-again The same star, a similar job
In the pointers lesson, `*p` followed a pointer to the value it points at. Optional borrows that symbol on purpose, because the job is similar: `*age` means "the thing inside". An optional and a pointer can both be empty, and using `*` on an empty one is a mistake in both cases. The difference is where the value lives. An optional keeps its value inside itself, so there is no separate memory, no `new` and no `delete`.
:::

::: context empty-star Why the star does not check
`*age` does no checking, for the same reason `v[i]` does no checking: checking costs a little time on every use, and C++ lets you skip it when you have already checked. There is a checked way, `age.value()`, which stops the program with an error when the box is empty, much like `v.at(i)` for vectors. Stopping loudly is far better than carrying on with a garbage number, which is why careful code checks first and uses `*` only inside the `if`.
:::

::: context nine-digits Why stop at nine digits
The largest `int` is 2,147,483,647, which has ten digits. Every nine-digit number, up to 999,999,999, fits easily, but many ten-digit numbers, such as 9,999,999,999, do not. Building a number past the largest `int` is overflow, which is undefined behavior. Allowing at most nine digits is a simple rule that makes overflow impossible, at the cost of turning away a few numbers that would fit. Overflow has cost rockets: in 1996, Ariane 5 Flight 501 was destroyed about 40 seconds after launch when a 64-bit floating-point value was converted to a 16-bit integer that could not hold it. A debugging lesson later in this course comes back to overflow.
:::

::: context digit-codes Characters are small numbers
Inside the computer, every `char` is stored as a number, its *character code*. In ASCII, the code used for plain English text, `'0'` is 48, `'1'` is 49, and so on up to `'9'`, which is 57. So `'7' - '0'` is 55 − 48, which is 7. The C++ standard promises that the ten digit characters always sit next to each other in order, on every computer, so subtracting `'0'` is always correct. It makes no such promise for letters.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 130" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <rect x="15" y="30" width="330" height="32" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
    <rect x="246" y="30" width="33" height="32" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
    <rect x="15" y="30" width="33" height="32" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
    <text x="31" y="51">'0'</text><text x="64" y="51">'1'</text><text x="97" y="51">'2'</text><text x="130" y="51">'3'</text><text x="163" y="51">'4'</text>
    <text x="196" y="51">'5'</text><text x="229" y="51">'6'</text><text x="262" y="51">'7'</text><text x="295" y="51">'8'</text><text x="328" y="51">'9'</text>
  </g>
  <g font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">
    <text x="31" y="78">48</text><text x="64" y="78">49</text><text x="97" y="78">50</text><text x="130" y="78">51</text><text x="163" y="78">52</text>
    <text x="196" y="78">53</text><text x="229" y="78">54</text><text x="262" y="78">55</text><text x="295" y="78">56</text><text x="328" y="78">57</text>
  </g>
  <text x="15" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">character codes</text>
  <text x="180" y="112" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">'7' - '0'  =  55 - 48  =  7</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `std::optional<int> parse_int(const std::string& s)` reads `s` as a whole number. It accepts an optional `-` followed by one to nine digits, and nothing else. `parse_int("42")` is 42 and `parse_int("-7")` is −7. Anything else is `std::nullopt`: the empty string, a lone `"-"`, spaces (`" 5"`), letters (`"4x2"`), or ten or more digits.
- `std::optional<std::size_t> index_of(const std::vector<std::string>& v, const std::string& target)` gives the position of the **first** `target` in `v`, or `std::nullopt` if it is not there. `index_of({"a", "b", "a"}, "a")` is 0.
- `int sum_valid(const std::vector<std::string>& items)` adds up the items that `parse_int` accepts and skips the rest. `sum_valid({"3", "x", "-1", "", "10"})` is 12. Use `value_or(0)`.
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> parse_int(const std::string& s) {
    return 0;
}

std::optional<std::size_t> index_of(const std::vector<std::string>& v, const std::string& target) {
    return 0;
}

int sum_valid(const std::vector<std::string>& items) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> parse_int(const std::string& s) {
    std::size_t i = 0;
    bool negative = false;
    if (i < s.size() && s[i] == '-') {
        negative = true;
        ++i;
    }
    std::size_t digits = s.size() - i;
    if (digits == 0 || digits > 9) return std::nullopt;
    int value = 0;
    for (; i < s.size(); ++i) {
        if (!std::isdigit(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[i]))) return std::nullopt;
        value = value * 10 + (s[i] - '0');
    }
    return negative ? -value : value;
}

std::optional<std::size_t> index_of(const std::vector<std::string>& v, const std::string& target) {
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        if (v[i] == target) return i;
    }
    return std::nullopt;
}

int sum_valid(const std::vector<std::string>& items) {
    int total = 0;
    for (const auto& item : items) {
        total += parse_int(item).value_or(0);
    }
    return total;
}
--- hint
In `parse_int`, deal with the minus sign first: if the first character is `'-'`, remember that in a `bool` and move your index past it. Then count how many characters are left. If that is 0 or more than 9, return `std::nullopt` before looking at them.
--- hint
Go through the remaining characters. If one is not a digit (`std::isdigit` on the `unsigned char`), return `std::nullopt` at once. Otherwise build the number with `value = value * 10 + (s[i] - '0');`, and at the end return `negative ? -value : value`. `index_of` is a plain loop over the indexes that does `return i;` on the first match and `return std::nullopt;` after the loop.
--- hint
`sum_valid` keeps a running `total` and, for each item, adds `parse_int(item).value_or(0)`: a string that does not parse adds 0.
--- check test | Plain and negative numbers parse
parse_int("42") == 42 && parse_int("-7") == -7 && parse_int("0") == 0
--- check test | Empty, a lone minus and letters do not
parse_int("") == std::nullopt && parse_int("-") == std::nullopt && parse_int("4x2") == std::nullopt && parse_int(" 5") == std::nullopt
--- check test | Nine digits parse, ten do not
parse_int("999999999") == 999999999 && parse_int("1234567890") == std::nullopt
--- check test | index_of finds the first match, or nothing
index_of({"a", "b", "a"}, "a") == std::size_t{0} && index_of({"a", "b"}, "b") == std::size_t{1} && index_of({"a"}, "z") == std::nullopt
--- check case | sum_valid skips what does not parse
sum_valid({"3", "x", "-1", "", "10"})
=> 12

+++ practice | Maybe a negative, maybe a digit
--- task
Write two functions that may have no answer. No `main`.

- `std::optional<int> first_negative(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns the first value below 0, or `std::nullopt` if there is none. `first_negative({4, -2, -9})` is -2.
- `std::optional<char> first_digit(const std::string& s)` returns the first digit character in `s`, or `std::nullopt` if there is none. `first_digit("T-minus 10")` is `'1'`.

Returning a value puts it in the box; `return std::nullopt;` means "no value".
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> first_negative(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return -1;
}

std::optional<char> first_digit(const std::string& s) {
    return '0';
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> first_negative(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < 0) return x;
    }
    return std::nullopt;
}

std::optional<char> first_digit(const std::string& s) {
    for (char c : s) {
        if (std::isdigit(static_cast<unsigned char>(c))) return c;
    }
    return std::nullopt;
}
--- hint
Loop over the values and `return` the first one that matches. If the loop ends without finding one, `return std::nullopt;`.
--- hint
`std::isdigit` wants an `unsigned char`, as in the strings lesson: `std::isdigit(static_cast<unsigned char>(c))`.
--- check test | The first negative
first_negative({4, -2, -9}) == -2 && first_negative({-1}) == -1
--- check test | No negative: no value, not -1 and not 0
first_negative({0, 3}) == std::nullopt && first_negative({}) == std::nullopt
--- check test | The first digit
first_digit("T-minus 10") == '1' && first_digit("9 lives") == '9'
--- check test | No digit
first_digit("no digits here") == std::nullopt && first_digit("") == std::nullopt

+++ practice | Optionals going in and out
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::optional<int> safe_divide(int a, int b)` returns `a / b` (whole-number division), or `std::nullopt` when `b` is 0. `safe_divide(17, 5)` is 3.
- `std::string describe(std::optional<int> reading)` takes a maybe-value and returns `"reading 42"` when it holds 42, or `"no reading"` when it is empty. A reading of 0 is a real reading: `"reading 0"`.

`describe(safe_divide(9, 0))` is `"no reading"`.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> safe_divide(int a, int b) {
    if (b == 0) return 0;
    return a / b;
}

std::string describe(std::optional<int> reading) {
    return "reading " + std::to_string(reading.value_or(0));
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> safe_divide(int a, int b) {
    if (b == 0) return std::nullopt;
    return a / b;
}

std::string describe(std::optional<int> reading) {
    if (!reading) return "no reading";
    return "reading " + std::to_string(*reading);
}
--- hint
Dividing by 0 has no answer, so `safe_divide` should return the empty box there, not a magic 0.
--- hint
In `describe`, test the optional itself: `if (!reading)` is true when it is empty. Only after that, take the value out with `*reading`.
--- check test | safe_divide divides, and refuses 0
safe_divide(17, 5) == 3 && safe_divide(-9, 3) == -3 && safe_divide(9, 0) == std::nullopt
--- check test | 0 divided by something is a real 0
safe_divide(0, 4) == 0 && safe_divide(0, 4) != std::nullopt
--- check case | describe a reading
describe(42)
=> "reading 42"
--- check case | describe a reading of 0
describe(0)
=> "reading 0"
--- check case | describe nothing
describe(safe_divide(9, 0))
=> "no reading"

+++ practice | Looking up scores, maybe
--- task
Write two functions over a map of names to scores. No `main`.

- `std::optional<int> score_of(const std::map<std::string, int>& scores, const std::string& name)` returns the score for `name`, or `std::nullopt` if `name` is not in the map. Use `find`, so nothing is ever added.
- `std::optional<std::string> top_scorer(const std::map<std::string, int>& scores)` returns the name with the highest score. If names tie, return the one that comes first alphabetically. An empty map has no top scorer: `std::nullopt`.

For `{{"ada", 90}, {"lin", 97}, {"sam", 97}}`, `score_of(..., "ada")` is 90, `score_of(..., "zed")` is empty, and `top_scorer` is `"lin"`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> score_of(const std::map<std::string, int>& scores, const std::string& name) {
    return std::nullopt;
}

std::optional<std::string> top_scorer(const std::map<std::string, int>& scores) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> score_of(const std::map<std::string, int>& scores, const std::string& name) {
    auto it = scores.find(name);
    if (it == scores.end()) return std::nullopt;
    return it->second;
}

std::optional<std::string> top_scorer(const std::map<std::string, int>& scores) {
    std::optional<std::string> best = std::nullopt;
    int best_score = 0;
    for (const auto& [name, score] : scores) {
        if (!best || score > best_score) {
            best = name;
            best_score = score;
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
`score_of` is a `find`: return `std::nullopt` when it gives `end()`, and `it->second` otherwise.
--- hint
In `top_scorer`, the best name so far can itself be an optional that starts empty. Replace it when it is still empty or when a score is strictly higher. The map walks names alphabetically, so the first of a tie stays.
--- check test | score_of finds a score, even a score of 0
score_of({{"ada", 90}, {"lin", 97}}, "ada") == 90 && score_of({{"kim", 0}}, "kim") == 0
--- check test | score_of of a missing name is empty
score_of({{"ada", 90}}, "zed") == std::nullopt
--- check test | top_scorer breaks ties alphabetically
top_scorer({{"ada", 90}, {"lin", 97}, {"sam", 97}}) == std::string("lin")
--- check test | Negative scores still have a top scorer
top_scorer({{"bo", -5}, {"al", -9}}) == std::string("bo")
--- check test | An empty map has no top scorer
top_scorer({}) == std::nullopt

+++ practice | Read a time like 09:45
--- task
Write `std::optional<int> parse_time(const std::string& s)`. It reads a 24-hour time written as exactly five characters, `HH:MM`, and returns the minutes since midnight. No `main`.

- `parse_time("09:45")` is 9 × 60 + 45 = 585, and `parse_time("00:00")` is 0, a real answer.
- The hours must be 00 to 23 and the minutes 00 to 59.
- Anything else is `std::nullopt`: `""`, `"9:45"` (one hour digit), `"24:00"`, `"12:60"`, `"12-30"`, `"1a:00"`, `"12:345"`.
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> parse_time(const std::string& s) {
    int h = (s[0] - '0') * 10 + (s[1] - '0');
    int m = (s[3] - '0') * 10 + (s[4] - '0');
    return h * 60 + m;
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

bool is_digit(char c) {
