    return std::isdigit(static_cast<unsigned char>(c));
}

std::optional<int> parse_time(const std::string& s) {
    if (s.size() != 5 || s[2] != ':') return std::nullopt;
    if (!is_digit(s[0]) || !is_digit(s[1]) || !is_digit(s[3]) || !is_digit(s[4])) return std::nullopt;
    int h = (s[0] - '0') * 10 + (s[1] - '0');
    int m = (s[3] - '0') * 10 + (s[4] - '0');
    if (h > 23 || m > 59) return std::nullopt;
    return h * 60 + m;
}
--- hint
Decide the rules before the arithmetic: the size must be exactly 5, the middle character must be `':'`, and the other four must all be digits. Check all of that before reading any character as a number.
--- hint
Only then build the hours and minutes (`'7' - '0'` is 7), check `h <= 23` and `m <= 59`, and return `h * 60 + m`.
--- check test | Good times
parse_time("09:45") == 585 && parse_time("23:59") == 1439 && parse_time("12:00") == 720
--- check test | Midnight is 0, not missing
parse_time("00:00") == 0 && parse_time("00:00") != std::nullopt
--- check test | Out of range
parse_time("24:00") == std::nullopt && parse_time("12:60") == std::nullopt && parse_time("99:99") == std::nullopt
--- check test | Wrong shape
parse_time("") == std::nullopt && parse_time("9:45") == std::nullopt && parse_time("12-30") == std::nullopt && parse_time("1a:00") == std::nullopt && parse_time("12:345") == std::nullopt

+++ practice | Debug: a reading of zero went missing
--- task
**Bug report:** "`count_present({3, std::nullopt, 0})` gives 1, but two readings are present: 3 and 0."

Each item in the list is a reading that may be missing. `count_present` should return how many readings are present, whatever their value. Find the cause and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

// How many readings are present (not missing), whatever their value.
int count_present(const std::vector<std::optional<int>>& readings) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const auto& r : readings) {
        if (r.value_or(0) != 0) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

// How many readings are present (not missing), whatever their value.
int count_present(const std::vector<std::optional<int>>& readings) {
    int n = 0;
    for (const auto& r : readings) {
        if (r) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
What does `value_or(0)` give for a present reading of 0? And for a missing one?
--- hint
Both give 0, so the test cannot tell them apart. Ask the optional itself whether it holds a value: `if (r)`.
--- check case | A reading of 0 is present
count_present({3, std::nullopt, 0})
=> 2
--- check case | All missing
count_present({std::nullopt, std::nullopt})
=> 0
--- check case | Negative readings count
count_present({-4, -1})
=> 2
--- check case | No readings
count_present({})
=> 0

+++ practice | Stretch: read a duration like 1h30m
--- task
Mission planners write durations like `1h30m`, `45s` or `2h5s`. Write `std::optional<int> parse_duration(const std::string& s)` that returns the total number of seconds. No `main`.

The rules:

- The text is one or more **pieces**. Each piece is 1 to 5 digits followed by a unit letter: `h` (hours), `m` (minutes) or `s` (seconds).
- Each unit appears at most once, and the pieces come in the order `h`, then `m`, then `s`. Any of them may be left out.
- Nothing else is allowed. Anything that breaks a rule gives `std::nullopt`.

So `"1h30m"` is 5400, `"45s"` is 45, `"2h5s"` is 7205 and `"0m"` is 0. These are all `std::nullopt`: `""`, `"5"` (no unit), `"m5"` (no digits before the unit), `"5m1h"` (wrong order), `"1h1h"` (a unit twice), `"1d"` (not a unit), `"123456s"` (six digits).
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> parse_duration(const std::string& s) {
    int total = 0;
    int value = 0;
    for (char c : s) {
        if (c == 'h') { total += value * 3600; value = 0; }
        else if (c == 'm') { total += value * 60; value = 0; }
        else if (c == 's') { total += value; value = 0; }
        else value = value * 10 + (c - '0');
    }
    return total;
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <optional>
#include <string>

std::optional<int> parse_duration(const std::string& s) {
    int total = 0;
    int value = 0;
    int digits = 0;
    int last_unit = -1;   // 0 for h, 1 for m, 2 for s: each piece must come after the last one
    for (char c : s) {
        if (std::isdigit(static_cast<unsigned char>(c))) {
            ++digits;
            if (digits > 5) return std::nullopt;
            value = value * 10 + (c - '0');
            continue;
        }
        int unit = 0;
        int seconds = 0;
        if (c == 'h') { unit = 0; seconds = 3600; }
        else if (c == 'm') { unit = 1; seconds = 60; }
        else if (c == 's') { unit = 2; seconds = 1; }
        else return std::nullopt;
        if (digits == 0 || unit <= last_unit) return std::nullopt;
        total += value * seconds;
        last_unit = unit;
        value = 0;
        digits = 0;
    }
    if (digits != 0 || last_unit == -1) return std::nullopt;
    return total;
}
--- hint
Walk the characters once. Build the current number from digits, and count how many digits it has. A unit letter ends a piece; any other character breaks the rules at once.
--- hint
Give each unit a number, h 0, m 1, s 2, and remember the last one you used. A new unit must have a bigger number than the last, which rules out both a wrong order and a unit twice.
--- hint
At the end, digits left over with no unit are an error, and so is a text with no pieces at all. Five digits of hours is at most 99999 × 3600, which still fits in an `int`.
--- check test | Good durations
parse_duration("1h30m") == 5400 && parse_duration("45s") == 45 && parse_duration("2h5s") == 7205 && parse_duration("1h1m1s") == 3661
--- check test | Zero is a real duration
parse_duration("0m") == 0 && parse_duration("0m") != std::nullopt
--- check test | Missing pieces or units
parse_duration("") == std::nullopt && parse_duration("5") == std::nullopt && parse_duration("m5") == std::nullopt && parse_duration("1h5") == std::nullopt
--- check test | Wrong order, repeats and strangers
parse_duration("5m1h") == std::nullopt && parse_duration("1h1h") == std::nullopt && parse_duration("1d") == std::nullopt && parse_duration("1h 5m") == std::nullopt
--- check test | Five digits allowed, six not
parse_duration("99999s") == 99999 && parse_duration("123456s") == std::nullopt

=== cpp2-10 | Lambdas and captures
--- teach
Last lesson, `std::optional` gave functions a way to say "no answer". You have been reading small unnamed functions since the basics course, in the checks, and you wrote a few for `count_if` and `find_if` in the algorithms lesson. This lesson covers them properly, including how they reach the variables around them.

### A function written on the spot

Picture a sticky note with one instruction on it, stuck right where you need it, instead of a full recipe filed away in a book. A **lambda** is that: a small function with no name, written right where it is used.

```cpp
auto square = [](int x) { return x * x; };
int s = square(5);   // 25
```

It has three parts. The square brackets `[]` are the **capture list** (more on that in a moment). The round brackets `(int x)` are the parameters, as in any function. The curly braces `{ … }` are the body. You can keep a lambda in an `auto` variable and call it like any function.

### Capturing a copy

A lambda's body cannot see the local variables around it unless you list them in the brackets. That list is the capture list: it says which outside variables the lambda may use. A plain name, like `[limit]`, **captures by copy**: the lambda keeps its own copy, taken at the moment the lambda is made.

```cpp
int limit = 10;
auto over = [limit](int x) { return x > limit; };
limit = 0;
bool b = over(5);   // false: the lambda kept its own 10
```

Changing `limit` afterwards does not reach the lambda. Each lambda you make is an [[object that carries its captures|closure]] around with it.

### Handing a lambda to an algorithm

The everyday use is handing a lambda to an algorithm. **`std::copy_if`**, from `<algorithm>`, copies every element that passes a test. It takes the range (`begin()` and `end()`), a place to write, and the test:

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {4, 15, 8, 23};
int limit = 10;
std::vector<int> out;
std::copy_if(v.begin(), v.end(), std::back_inserter(out),
             [limit](int x) { return x > limit; });
// out is {15, 23}, in their original order
```

**`std::back_inserter(out)`**, from `<iterator>`, is the place to write: [[it calls push_back for you|back-inserter]], so `out` grows by one for each value that passes.

### Capturing by reference

Sometimes the lambda should change a variable outside it. Put `&` in front of the name, read "reference to", exactly as in the references lesson. Now the lambda uses the caller's own variable, not a copy:

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {3, 1, 2};
int calls = 0;
std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), [&calls](int a, int b) { ++calls; return a < b; });
// calls now holds how many times sort compared two values
```

Here is every kind of capture in one table:

| Capture | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `[]` | nothing from outside |
| `[limit]` | a copy of `limit`, taken when the lambda is made |
| `[&total]` | a reference to `total`: the lambda can change it |
| `[=]` / `[&]` | everything it uses, by copy / by reference |
| `[n = 0]` | a new variable living inside the lambda |

The two short forms `[=]` and `[&]` save typing, but [[naming each capture|capture-all]] shows the reader exactly what the lambda touches.

### A lambda with its own memory

The last row is an **init capture**: `[n = 0]` makes a brand-new variable `n` that belongs to the lambda, starting at 0. The copies a lambda holds are read-only unless you mark the lambda **`mutable`**, which means "allowed to change its own copies". Together they give a lambda **state**: a value it remembers between calls.

```cpp
auto next_id = [id = 100]() mutable { return id++; };
next_id();   // 100
next_id();   // 101
```

Without `mutable`, clang stops with "cannot assign to a variable captured by copy in a non-mutable lambda". Two lambdas made separately each get their own `id`, so they count separately.

A function can hand back a lambda. The lambda's type has no name you could write, so declare the function's return type as `auto` and let the compiler fill it in:

```cpp
auto make_greeter() {
    return [](const std::string& name) { return "Hello, " + name; };
}
```

**Watch out:** do not capture a local variable **by reference** in a lambda that outlives it. When the function that made the lambda ends, its local variables are gone, and the reference [[dangles|dangling]]: it names memory that no longer holds that variable. Using it is undefined behavior, and it compiles without complaint.

```cpp
auto make_bad() {
    int count = 0;
    return [&count]() { return ++count; };   // count dies when make_bad ends
}
```

When a lambda leaves the function that made it, for instance when you return it, capture by value or with an init capture.

### Sorting with a lambda

`std::sort` can take a third argument, a **comparator**: a lambda that gets two values `a` and `b` and returns true when `a` should come **before** `b`. With `a < b` you get the usual smallest-first order. Change the test, and you change the order.

To sort by how close each value is to some target, compare distances. **`std::abs`**, from `<cstdlib>`, gives the size of a number without its sign: `std::abs(3 - 8)` is 5. And when two distances are equal, [[say which comes first|strict-order]], or the order of those two is up to `sort`. Here, sorting by closeness to 0 (the size of each value), with the smaller value first on a tie:

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {3, -1, -3, 2};
std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), [](int a, int b) {
    int da = std::abs(a);
    int db = std::abs(b);
    if (da != db) return da < db;   // closer to 0 comes first
    return a < b;                   // same distance: smaller value first
});
// v is {-1, 2, -3, 3}
```

::: context closure What the compiler builds
A lambda is shorthand. For each lambda, the compiler writes a small class with no name you can use: every captured variable becomes a member of it, and the body becomes the function that runs when you call it. The object made from that class is called a *closure*. That is why each counter made by the same code has its own count: each is its own object with its own member.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="180" y="20" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">two lambdas made from [n = 0]() mutable { return ++n; }</text>
  <rect x="30" y="40" width="130" height="70" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="95" y="60" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">lambda a</text>
  <rect x="70" y="70" width="50" height="28" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="95" y="89" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">n = 2</text>
  <rect x="200" y="40" width="130" height="70" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="265" y="60" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">lambda b</text>
  <rect x="240" y="70" width="50" height="28" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="265" y="89" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">n = 0</text>
  <text x="95" y="132" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">called twice</text>
  <text x="265" y="132" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">not called yet</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context back-inserter Writing past the end, safely
Algorithms like `copy_if` write through an iterator: "put the value here, then move one along". If you passed `out.begin()` of an empty vector, there would be no room, and writing there would be undefined behavior. The algorithms lesson avoided that by making `out` the right size first. When you do not know how many values will pass the test, that is impossible, so `std::back_inserter` gives a different kind of iterator: writing through it calls `out.push_back(value)`, and the vector grows exactly as needed.
:::

::: context capture-all Name what you capture
`[=]` and `[&]` capture whatever the body happens to use, which hides from the reader what the lambda depends on. The C++ Core Guidelines, a widely used rule book, suggest capturing by reference for lambdas used right there, such as one handed straight to `std::sort`, and avoiding reference captures for lambdas that are stored or returned. Naming each capture makes that choice visible, one variable at a time.
:::

::: context dangling Why the reference dangles
A function's local variables live in a piece of memory set aside for that call, and the piece is handed back the moment the function returns. A reference captured from inside still points at that spot, but the spot now belongs to whatever runs next. Reading it may give the old value, a stranger's number, or a crash; writing it can damage another function's data. Compilers often cannot see this mistake. Sanitizer tools, which add checks while the program runs, catch many cases, but the dependable fix is to capture by value.
:::

::: context strict-order The rules a comparator must follow
`std::sort` trusts its comparator to behave like "less than". In particular, it must return false when `a` and `b` are equal. So write `<`, never `<=`: with `<=`, two equal values would each claim to come before the other, and the C++ rules call sorting with such a comparator undefined behavior. Breaking ties with a second test, as here, keeps the comparator a proper "less than" and makes the result the same on every computer.
:::
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `std::vector<int> keep_above(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit)` returns the values greater than `limit`, in their original order. Use `std::copy_if` with `std::back_inserter` and a lambda that captures `limit`. `keep_above({5, 12, 3, 40, 10}, 10)` is `{12, 40}`.
- `void sort_by_distance(std::vector<int>& v, int target)` sorts `v` in place so that values closest to `target` come first. On a tie, the smaller value comes first. Use `std::sort` with a lambda comparator that captures `target` and uses `std::abs`.
- `auto make_counter()` returns a lambda. Calling that lambda returns 1 the first time, then 2, then 3, and so on. Each counter you make counts on its own.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <iterator>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> keep_above(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    return v;
}

void sort_by_distance(std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
}

auto make_counter() {
    return [] { return 0; };
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <iterator>
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> keep_above(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    std::copy_if(v.begin(), v.end(), std::back_inserter(out), [limit](int x) { return x > limit; });
    return out;
}

void sort_by_distance(std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), [target](int a, int b) {
        int da = std::abs(a - target);
        int db = std::abs(b - target);
        if (da != db) return da < db;
        return a < b;
    });
}

auto make_counter() {
    return [n = 0]() mutable { return ++n; };
}
--- hint
For `keep_above`, make an empty `std::vector<int> out;`, then call `std::copy_if(v.begin(), v.end(), std::back_inserter(out), …)` with a lambda `[limit](int x) { return x > limit; }` in place of the dots, and return `out`.
--- hint
`sort_by_distance` is the closeness-to-0 example from the explanation, with the distances measured from `target` instead: `std::abs(a - target)`. The lambda needs `[target]` in its brackets to see it.
--- hint
The counter needs its own variable that it may change: an init capture plus `mutable`, as in `next_id`. Start it at 0 and return `++n`, so the first call gives 1.
--- check case | keep_above keeps order
keep_above({5, 12, 3, 40, 10}, 10)
=> std::vector<int>{12, 40}
--- check test | sort_by_distance puts the closest first, ties smaller first
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 9, 6, 4, 5}; sort_by_distance(v, 5); return v == std::vector<int>{5, 4, 6, 1, 9}; }()
--- check test | A counter counts
[] { auto c = make_counter(); c(); c(); return c() == 3; }()
--- check test | Two counters are independent
[] { auto a = make_counter(); auto b = make_counter(); a(); a(); return b() == 1 && a() == 3; }()

+++ practice | Long words, shortest first
--- task
Write two functions with lambdas. No `main`.

- `std::vector<std::string> longer_than(const std::vector<std::string>& words, std::size_t n)` returns the words with more than `n` characters, in their original order. Use `std::copy_if` with `std::back_inserter` and a lambda that captures `n`. `longer_than({"go", "orbit", "sun", "apogee"}, 3)` is `{"orbit", "apogee"}`.
- `void sort_by_length(std::vector<std::string>& words)` sorts the words in place, shortest first. Words of the same length go in alphabetical order. Use `std::sort` with a lambda comparator. `{"moon", "sun", "mars", "io"}` becomes `{"io", "sun", "mars", "moon"}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> longer_than(const std::vector<std::string>& words, std::size_t n) {
    return words;
}

void sort_by_length(std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    std::sort(words.begin(), words.end());
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> longer_than(const std::vector<std::string>& words, std::size_t n) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    std::copy_if(words.begin(), words.end(), std::back_inserter(out),
                 [n](const std::string& w) { return w.size() > n; });
    return out;
}

void sort_by_length(std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    std::sort(words.begin(), words.end(), [](const std::string& a, const std::string& b) {
        if (a.size() != b.size()) return a.size() < b.size();
        return a < b;
    });
}
--- hint
The lambda for `copy_if` takes one word, `const std::string& w`, and needs `n` in its square brackets.
--- hint
The comparator returns true when `a` should come before `b`: first compare the sizes, and only when they are equal compare the words themselves with `<`.
--- check case | Words longer than 3
longer_than({"go", "orbit", "sun", "apogee"}, 3)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"orbit", "apogee"}
--- check case | Exactly n is not longer
longer_than({"sun", "star"}, 4)
=> std::vector<std::string>{}
--- check test | Shortest first, ties alphabetical
[] { std::vector<std::string> w{"moon", "sun", "mars", "io"}; sort_by_length(w); return w == std::vector<std::string>{"io", "sun", "mars", "moon"}; }()
--- check test | Sorting nothing is fine
[] { std::vector<std::string> w; sort_by_length(w); return w.empty(); }()

+++ practice | Functions that build functions
--- task
Write two functions that each return a lambda. Declare their return type as `auto`. No `main`.

- `auto make_scaler(int factor)` returns a lambda that takes an `int` and returns it times `factor`. `make_scaler(3)(7)` is 21.
- `auto make_between(int low, int high)` returns a lambda that takes an `int x` and returns `true` when `low <= x` and `x <= high`. `make_between(10, 20)(15)` is `true`.

The lambdas outlive the functions that made them, so capture by copy.
--- starter
auto make_scaler(int factor) {
    return [](int x) { return x; };
}

auto make_between(int low, int high) {
    return [](int x) { return false; };
}
--- solution
auto make_scaler(int factor) {
    return [factor](int x) { return x * factor; };
}

auto make_between(int low, int high) {
    return [low, high](int x) { return low <= x && x <= high; };
}
--- hint
A lambda can only use `factor` if it is named in the square brackets. A plain name there captures a copy, which is what a returned lambda needs.
--- hint
You can capture several variables: list them in the brackets with commas, as in `[low, high]`.
--- check test | A scaler scales
[] { auto triple = make_scaler(3); return triple(7) == 21 && triple(-2) == -6 && triple(0) == 0; }()
--- check test | Two scalers keep their own factors
[] { auto a = make_scaler(2); auto b = make_scaler(10); return a(5) == 10 && b(5) == 50; }()
--- check test | Between includes both ends
[] { auto ok = make_between(10, 20); return ok(10) && ok(15) && ok(20) && !ok(9) && !ok(21); }()
--- check test | Negative ranges
[] { auto cold = make_between(-40, -10); return cold(-25) && !cold(0); }()

+++ practice | The veterans, most missions first
--- task
A `Crew` member has a name and a number of missions flown:

```cpp
struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int missions;
};
```

Write `std::vector<std::string> veterans(const std::vector<Crew>& crew, int min_missions)`. It returns the names of the crew who have flown at least `min_missions` missions, most missions first. Crew with the same number go in alphabetical order. No `main`.

For `{{"Yuri", 3}, {"Mae", 6}, {"Al", 1}, {"Kay", 6}}` and 3, the answer is `{"Kay", "Mae", "Yuri"}`. Use `std::copy_if` to pick the crew, and `std::sort` with a lambda to order them.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int missions;
};

std::vector<std::string> veterans(const std::vector<Crew>& crew, int min_missions) {
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (const auto& c : crew) names.push_back(c.name);
    return names;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int missions;
};

std::vector<std::string> veterans(const std::vector<Crew>& crew, int min_missions) {
    std::vector<Crew> picked;
    std::copy_if(crew.begin(), crew.end(), std::back_inserter(picked),
                 [min_missions](const Crew& c) { return c.missions >= min_missions; });
    std::sort(picked.begin(), picked.end(), [](const Crew& a, const Crew& b) {
        if (a.missions != b.missions) return a.missions > b.missions;
        return a.name < b.name;
    });
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (const auto& c : picked) names.push_back(c.name);
    return names;
}
--- hint
Copy the crew who qualify into a new `std::vector<Crew>`, sort that, and only then pull out the names.
--- hint
For "most first", the comparator returns `a.missions > b.missions` when the missions differ. When they are equal, it falls back to `a.name < b.name`.
--- check case | Most missions first, ties alphabetical
veterans({{"Yuri", 3}, {"Mae", 6}, {"Al", 1}, {"Kay", 6}}, 3)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"Kay", "Mae", "Yuri"}
--- check case | Everyone qualifies with a minimum of 0
veterans({{"Bo", 0}, {"Al", 2}}, 0)
=> std::vector<std::string>{"Al", "Bo"}
--- check case | Nobody qualifies
veterans({{"Bo", 1}}, 5)
=> std::vector<std::string>{}
--- check case | No crew
veterans({}, 1)
=> std::vector<std::string>{}

+++ practice | Lambdas that remember
--- task
Write two functions that each return a lambda with its own memory. No `main`.

- `auto make_limiter(int max_calls)` returns a lambda that takes nothing and returns `true` for its first `max_calls` calls, then `false` for ever after. If `max_calls` is 0 or negative, it returns `false` from the very first call.
- `auto make_running_min()` returns a lambda that takes an `int` and returns the smallest value it has been given so far, counting this one. Given 5, then 8, then 2, it returns 5, 5, 2.

Watch the edge cases: a running minimum that starts at 0 is wrong as soon as every value is positive.
--- starter
auto make_limiter(int max_calls) {
    return [left = max_calls]() mutable { --left; return left > 0; };
}

auto make_running_min() {
    return [best = 0](int x) mutable {
        if (x < best) best = x;
        return best;
    };
}
--- solution
auto make_limiter(int max_calls) {
    return [left = max_calls]() mutable {
        if (left <= 0) return false;
        --left;
        return true;
    };
}

auto make_running_min() {
    return [best = 0, seen = false](int x) mutable {
        if (!seen || x < best) best = x;
        seen = true;
        return best;
    };
}
--- hint
For the limiter, check whether any calls are left **before** using one up: return `false` when `left <= 0`; otherwise take one away and return `true`.
--- hint
The running minimum needs to know whether it has seen anything yet. Give it a second init capture, a `bool` that starts `false`, separated from the first by a comma: `[best = 0, seen = false]`. The first value always becomes the best.
--- check test | A limiter of 2
[] { auto go = make_limiter(2); bool a = go(); bool b = go(); bool c = go(); bool d = go(); return a && b && !c && !d; }()
--- check test | A limiter of 0 or less never says yes
[] { auto none = make_limiter(0); auto neg = make_limiter(-3); return !none() && !none() && !neg(); }()
--- check test | Running minimum of positive values
[] { auto m = make_running_min(); int a = m(5); int b = m(8); int c = m(2); return a == 5 && b == 5 && c == 2; }()
--- check test | Running minimum with negatives, and two independent ones
[] { auto m = make_running_min(); auto n = make_running_min(); m(-1); m(-7); return m(3) == -7 && n(3) == 3; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the dropped count that stayed at zero
--- task
**Bug report:** "`keep_at_most({5, 12, 3, 40}, 10)` keeps `{5, 3}`, which is right, but it says 0 values were dropped instead of 2."

`keep_at_most` returns a `Split`: the values at most `limit`, in order, and how many values were dropped because they were bigger. Find why the count never leaves 0 and fix the cause. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <vector>

struct Split {
    std::vector<int> kept;
    int dropped;
};

Split keep_at_most(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    std::vector<int> kept;
    int dropped = 0;
    std::copy_if(v.begin(), v.end(), std::back_inserter(kept), [limit, dropped](int x) mutable {
        if (x <= limit) return true;
        ++dropped;
        return false;
    });
    return {kept, dropped};
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <iterator>
#include <vector>

struct Split {
    std::vector<int> kept;
    int dropped;
};

Split keep_at_most(const std::vector<int>& v, int limit) {
    std::vector<int> kept;
    int dropped = 0;
    std::copy_if(v.begin(), v.end(), std::back_inserter(kept), [limit, &dropped](int x) {
        if (x <= limit) return true;
        ++dropped;
        return false;
    });
    return {kept, dropped};
}
--- hint
Which `dropped` does `++dropped` change: the function's own variable, or something else?
--- hint
`[dropped]` captures a copy, and `mutable` only lets the lambda change its own copy. To change the function's variable, capture it by reference: `&dropped`.
--- check test | Kept and dropped
[] { Split s = keep_at_most({5, 12, 3, 40}, 10); return s.kept == std::vector<int>{5, 3} && s.dropped == 2; }()
--- check test | A value equal to the limit is kept
[] { Split s = keep_at_most({10, 11}, 10); return s.kept == std::vector<int>{10} && s.dropped == 1; }()
--- check test | Everything dropped
[] { Split s = keep_at_most({7, 8}, 0); return s.kept.empty() && s.dropped == 2; }()
--- check test | Nothing to split
[] { Split s = keep_at_most({}, 5); return s.kept.empty() && s.dropped == 0; }()

+++ practice | Stretch: the race podium
--- task
Pilots fly timed laps in a simulator, and some laps are thrown out. Each lap is an `Entry`:

```cpp
struct Entry {
    std::string pilot;
    int time_ms;
    bool valid;
};
```

Write `std::vector<std::string> podium(const std::vector<Entry>& entries)`. It returns up to three pilot names, fastest first. No `main`.

- Only valid laps count.
- Each pilot counts once, with their best (smallest) valid time.
- Pilots with the same best time go in alphabetical order.
- If fewer than three pilots have a valid lap, return as many as there are.

For laps Ann 5200, Bo 4900, Ann 4800, Cy 4900 (not valid), Cy 5100 and Di 4900, the best times are Ann 4800, Bo 4900, Di 4900 and Cy 5100, so the podium is `{"Ann", "Bo", "Di"}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Entry {
    std::string pilot;
    int time_ms;
    bool valid;
};

std::vector<std::string> podium(const std::vector<Entry>& entries) {
    std::vector<Entry> laps = entries;
    std::sort(laps.begin(), laps.end(), [](const Entry& a, const Entry& b) { return a.time_ms < b.time_ms; });
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < 3; ++i) names.push_back(laps[i].pilot);
    return names;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Entry {
    std::string pilot;
    int time_ms;
    bool valid;
};

std::vector<std::string> podium(const std::vector<Entry>& entries) {
    std::map<std::string, int> best;
    for (const auto& e : entries) {
        if (!e.valid) continue;
        auto it = best.find(e.pilot);
        if (it == best.end() || e.time_ms < it->second) best[e.pilot] = e.time_ms;
    }
    std::vector<Entry> ranked;
    for (const auto& [pilot, time] : best) ranked.push_back({pilot, time, true});
    std::sort(ranked.begin(), ranked.end(), [](const Entry& a, const Entry& b) {
        if (a.time_ms != b.time_ms) return a.time_ms < b.time_ms;
        return a.pilot < b.pilot;
    });
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < ranked.size() && i < 3; ++i) names.push_back(ranked[i].pilot);
    return names;
}
--- hint
First keep each pilot's best valid time in a `std::map<std::string, int>`: skip laps that are not valid, and store a time when the pilot is new or the time is smaller.
--- hint
Then put one `Entry` per pilot into a vector and sort it with a comparator: time first, and name when the times are equal.
--- hint
Take names while `i < ranked.size() && i < 3`, so a short list never reads past its end.
--- check case | Best valid time per pilot, ties alphabetical
podium({{"Ann", 5200, true}, {"Bo", 4900, true}, {"Ann", 4800, true}, {"Cy", 4900, false}, {"Cy", 5100, true}, {"Di", 4900, true}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"Ann", "Bo", "Di"}
--- check case | A pilot with only invalid laps is left out
podium({{"Ann", 4000, false}, {"Bo", 6000, true}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"Bo"}
--- check case | The same pilot is never on the podium twice
podium({{"Ann", 3000, true}, {"Ann", 3100, true}, {"Ann", 3200, true}, {"Bo", 9000, true}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"Ann", "Bo"}
--- check case | No laps at all
podium({})
=> std::vector<std::string>{}

=== cpp2-11 | Processing text with string streams
--- teach
Last lesson you handed small lambdas to algorithms. This lesson turns to text. Real input is messy: extra spaces, blank lines, fields separated by commas, a number with junk stuck to it. Here you learn to cut text into pieces, clean each piece, and check it.

### Reading from a string

In the basics map lesson you read words out of a string with a **`std::istringstream`** (input string stream, from `<sstream>`). It reads from a string exactly as `std::cin` reads from the input, and `>>`, read "get from", reads any type:

```cpp
std::istringstream in("3 apples 4.5");
int n;
std::string what;
double w;
in >> n >> what >> w;   // n is 3, what is "apples", w is 4.5
```

Each `>>` skips spaces, then reads the next piece as the type of its variable.

### When a read fails

If the next text is not a number, `in >> n` **fails**. A stream that has failed counts as false in a condition, so you can test a read directly. After a failed read, `n` is set to 0, and [[the stream stops reading|failed-read]].

```cpp
std::istringstream bad("abc");
int n;
if (!(bad >> n)) {
    // "abc" is not a number: we get here
}
```

The `!` means "not", so the condition reads "if the read did not work".

### One line at a time

`std::getline(stream, line)`, from the basics course, reads up to the end of a line. In a `while` loop it reads every line of a block of text, and stops when the lines run out:

```cpp
std::istringstream text("first\nsecond\n");
std::string line;
while (std::getline(text, line)) {
    // runs twice: line is "first", then "second"
}
```

The `\n` is the [[newline character|escapes]], written inside quotes as a backslash and `n`.

### Up to any character

Give `getline` a third argument, a `char`, and it reads up to that character instead of the end of the line. The separator itself is thrown away:

```cpp
std::istringstream row("ALT,4200,OK");
std::string field;
while (std::getline(row, field, ',')) {
    // field is "ALT", then "4200", then "OK"
}
```

**Watch out:** this way of splitting quietly [[drops a trailing empty field|trailing-field]]. Splitting `"a,b,"` gives two fields, `"a"` and `"b"`, not three, and splitting `""` gives none at all. When empty fields matter, cut the string yourself with `find` and `substr`.

### Cutting by hand with find and substr

You know `find` and `substr` from the basics course. Find the separator, then cut on either side of it:

```cpp
std::string s = "key=value";
auto eq = s.find('=');                 // 3, or std::string::npos if there is none
std::string key = s.substr(0, eq);     // "key"
std::string val = s.substr(eq + 1);    // "value"
```

`find` can also take a second number: the index to start looking from. That lets you find the next separator after the one you already found:

```cpp
std::string t = "a,b,c";
t.find(',');      // 1
t.find(',', 2);   // 3: starts looking at index 2
```

To split a whole string, keep a `start` index. Find the next separator from `start`, take the piece between them, and move `start` to just after the separator. When `find` gives `npos`, there are no more separators, and everything from `start` to the end is the last field, even if it is empty.

### Trimming whitespace

**Whitespace** means the invisible characters: spaces, tabs (`\t`) and newlines (`\n`). **Trimming** a string means removing whitespace from both ends and keeping everything between. `std::isspace` from `<cctype>` recognizes [[all kinds of whitespace|whitespace-kinds]]; convert to `unsigned char` first, as always.

The idea is two indexes that walk inward. One starts at the front and steps forward past whitespace:

```cpp
std::string s = "  hi";
std::size_t a = 0;
while (a < s.size() && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[a]))) ++a;
// a is 2, so s.substr(a) is "hi"
```

The other starts at the end and steps backward the same way. What is left between them is the trimmed string.

### Is the whole field a number?

`>>` stops reading a number at the first character that is not part of it. So `"12abc"` reads as 12, and the read **succeeds**. To be sure the whole field was a number, check two things: the read worked, **and** nothing but spaces is left afterwards.

```cpp
std::istringstream num(" 42 ");
int n;
if (num >> n) {
    num >> std::ws;      // skip any spaces after the number
    if (num.eof()) {
        // the whole field was a number
    }
}
```

`std::ws`, "skip white space", which you met in the basics course, eats spaces. Then **`eof()`**, short for [["end of file"|eof]], is true when the stream has reached the end of its text. For `"12abc"`, the `abc` is still there, so `eof()` is false and you know to reject it.

::: context failed-read What a failed read does
When `>>` cannot read what you asked for, the stream sets a "failed" flag inside itself. Since C++11, the variable you were reading into is set to 0. While the flag is set, every later `>>` on that stream also fails and reads nothing, until you call `in.clear()`. That is why `while (in >> w)` stops cleanly at the first bad piece or the end of the text. It is also why the check in this lesson gives each score its own small stream: one bad line cannot jam the reading of the next.
:::

::: context escapes Characters you cannot type in quotes
Some characters cannot be typed straight into a string, so C++ writes them with a backslash and a letter, called an *escape sequence*. `\n` is the newline, `\t` a tab, `\\` a real backslash and `\"` a double quote inside a string. Each one is a single character, even though you type two: `"a\nb"` has three characters, and its `size()` is 3.
:::

::: context trailing-field The field that goes missing
`getline` stops when there is nothing left to read. After `"a,b,"` it has read `a` and `b`, and the last comma leaves nothing after it, so the loop ends: there is no empty third field. In a spreadsheet file, though, `a,b,` means three columns with the third empty. Dropping it quietly shifts meaning. If a telemetry row lists readings in fixed columns and the last reading is missing, a program that loses the empty field may read the wrong number of columns and misplace everything after.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 150" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="20" y="22" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">text: a,b,</text>
  <text x="20" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">split by hand</text>
  <rect x="140" y="42" width="50" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="200" y="42" width="50" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="260" y="42" width="50" height="26" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="165" y="60" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a</text>
  <text x="225" y="60" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">b</text>
  <text x="285" y="60" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">""</text>
  <text x="20" y="108" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">getline with ','</text>
  <rect x="140" y="92" width="50" height="26" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="200" y="92" width="50" height="26" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="165" y="110" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a</text>
  <text x="225" y="110" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">b</text>
  <text x="285" y="110" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">missing</text>
  <text x="180" y="140" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">three fields by hand, two with getline</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context whitespace-kinds More whitespace than you think
`std::isspace` is true for six characters: space, tab `\t`, newline `\n`, carriage return `\r`, and two rarely seen ones, vertical tab `\v` and form feed `\f`. The carriage return matters in practice. Text files saved on Windows end each line with `\r\n`, so `getline`, which stops at `\n`, leaves a stray `\r` at the end of every line. It is invisible when printed, but `"ALT\r"` is not equal to `"ALT"`. Trimming each field removes it.
:::

::: context eof Why "end of file"
Streams were first used mostly for reading files, so reaching the end of the text is called *end of file*, even when the stream reads from a string. `eof()` becomes true only once a read has actually bumped into the end. That is why the check reads with `>> std::ws` first: skipping the trailing spaces runs into the end, and only then does `eof()` report it.
:::
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `std::vector<std::string> split(const std::string& s, char sep)` returns every field between the separators, **including empty ones**. `split("a,,b", ',')` is `{"a", "", "b"}`, `split("a,", ',')` is `{"a", ""}`, and `split("", ',')` is `{""}`. The starter's version uses `getline`, which drops empty fields at the end; rewrite it with `find` and `substr`.
- `std::string trim(const std::string& s)` returns `s` without whitespace (spaces, tabs, newlines) at either end. `trim(" \t hello world \n")` is `"hello world"`, and `trim("   ")` is `""`.
- `std::map<std::string, int> parse_scores(const std::string& text)` reads text with one entry per line, like `name: score`, with any amount of whitespace around the name and the score. Store `name → score` in the map, with the name trimmed. Skip any line that is blank, has no `:`, has an empty name, or whose score is not a whole number (such as `7x`).
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> split(const std::string& s, char sep) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    std::istringstream in(s);
    std::string field;
    while (std::getline(in, field, sep)) out.push_back(field);
    return out;
}

std::string trim(const std::string& s) {
    return s;
}

std::map<std::string, int> parse_scores(const std::string& text) {
    return {};
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> split(const std::string& s, char sep) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    std::size_t start = 0;
    while (true) {
        std::size_t at = s.find(sep, start);
        if (at == std::string::npos) {
            out.push_back(s.substr(start));
            return out;
        }
        out.push_back(s.substr(start, at - start));
        start = at + 1;
    }
}

std::string trim(const std::string& s) {
    std::size_t a = 0;
    std::size_t b = s.size();
    while (a < b && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[a]))) ++a;
    while (b > a && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[b - 1]))) --b;
    return s.substr(a, b - a);
}

std::map<std::string, int> parse_scores(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<std::string, int> scores;
    std::istringstream lines(text);
    std::string line;
    while (std::getline(lines, line)) {
        std::size_t colon = line.find(':');
        if (colon == std::string::npos) continue;
        std::string name = trim(line.substr(0, colon));
        if (name.empty()) continue;
        std::istringstream num(line.substr(colon + 1));
        int score;
        if (!(num >> score)) continue;
        num >> std::ws;
        if (!num.eof()) continue;
        scores[name] = score;
    }
    return scores;
}
--- hint
For `split`, keep a `start` index at 0 and loop: `s.find(sep, start)` finds the next separator. If there is one at `at`, push `s.substr(start, at - start)` and set `start = at + 1`. If `find` gives `std::string::npos`, push `s.substr(start)`, the rest of the string, even if it is empty, and return.
--- hint
For `trim`, keep one index `a` at the front and one index `b` at `s.size()`. Move `a` forward while `s[a]` is whitespace, move `b` backward while `s[b - 1]` is whitespace (never letting them cross), then return `s.substr(a, b - a)`.
--- hint
In `parse_scores`, read each line with `getline`. Find the `:`, and `continue` if it is missing. Trim the part before it and `continue` if that is empty. Read the part after it from its own `std::istringstream num(...)`: `continue` if `num >> score` fails, then do `num >> std::ws` and `continue` if `!num.eof()`. Only then store `scores[name] = score`.
--- check case | split keeps empty fields in the middle
split("a,,b", ',')
=> std::vector<std::string>{"a", "", "b"}
--- check case | split keeps a trailing empty field
split("a,", ',')
=> std::vector<std::string>{"a", ""}
--- check case | split of an empty string is one empty field
split("", ',')
=> std::vector<std::string>{""}
--- check case | trim removes spaces, tabs and newlines at both ends
trim(" \t hello world \n")
=> "hello world"
--- check case | trim of only spaces is empty
trim("   ")
=> ""
--- check case | parse_scores reads good lines and skips bad ones
parse_scores("  ada : 90\n\nlin:75 \nno colon here\n: 5\nsam: 7x\n")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"ada", 90}, {"lin", 75}}

+++ practice | Words and lines
--- task
Write two functions with string streams. No `main`.

- `int count_words(const std::string& text)` returns how many words `text` holds, where words are separated by any amount of whitespace. `count_words("  ignition   sequence\tstart\n")` is 3.
- `std::vector<std::string> lines_of(const std::string& text)` returns each line of `text`, in order, using `std::getline`. `lines_of("go\nno go\n")` is `{"go", "no go"}`. Blank lines count: `lines_of("a\n\nb")` is `{"a", "", "b"}`.
--- starter
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int count_words(const std::string& text) {
    return 0;
}

std::vector<std::string> lines_of(const std::string& text) {
    return {text};
}
--- solution
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int count_words(const std::string& text) {
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string word;
    int n = 0;
    while (in >> word) ++n;
    return n;
}

std::vector<std::string> lines_of(const std::string& text) {
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::vector<std::string> lines;
    std::string line;
    while (std::getline(in, line)) lines.push_back(line);
    return lines;
}
--- hint
`>>` into a `std::string` skips any whitespace, then reads one word. In a `while` loop it stops when the words run out.
--- hint
`std::getline(in, line)` reads up to the next newline and throws the newline away, so a blank line comes back as `""`.
--- check case | Words with messy spacing
count_words("  ignition   sequence\tstart\n")
=> 3
--- check case | No words
count_words("   \n ")
=> 0
--- check case | Lines in order
lines_of("go\nno go\n")
=> std::vector<std::string>{"go", "no go"}
--- check case | A blank line in the middle counts
lines_of("a\n\nb")
=> std::vector<std::string>{"a", "", "b"}
--- check case | Empty text has no lines
lines_of("")
=> std::vector<std::string>{}

+++ practice | Read a settings file
--- task
Write `std::map<std::string, std::string> parse_config(const std::string& text)`. The text has one setting per line, written `key = value`. No `main`.

- Split each line at its **first** `=`. Trim whitespace from the key and from the value.
- Skip a line that is blank (only whitespace), a line whose first non-space character is `#` (a comment), a line with no `=`, and a line whose key is empty after trimming.
- A value may be empty. If a key appears twice, the later line wins.

For `"# engine\n mode = burn \nthrust=90\n\nlabel =\nbad line\nmode=coast"` the result is label → `""`, mode → `"coast"` and thrust → `"90"`.

Write your own `trim` helper, as in the lesson.
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::map<std::string, std::string> parse_config(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<std::string, std::string> out;
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string line;
    while (std::getline(in, line)) {
        std::size_t eq = line.find('=');
        out[line.substr(0, eq)] = line.substr(eq + 1);
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string trim(const std::string& s) {
    std::size_t a = 0;
    std::size_t b = s.size();
    while (a < b && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[a]))) ++a;
    while (b > a && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[b - 1]))) --b;
    return s.substr(a, b - a);
}

std::map<std::string, std::string> parse_config(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<std::string, std::string> out;
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string line;
    while (std::getline(in, line)) {
        std::string t = trim(line);
        if (t.empty() || t[0] == '#') continue;
        std::size_t eq = t.find('=');
        if (eq == std::string::npos) continue;
        std::string key = trim(t.substr(0, eq));
        if (key.empty()) continue;
        out[key] = trim(t.substr(eq + 1));
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Trim the whole line first. Then an empty line or a line starting with `'#'` can be skipped with `continue` before you look for the `=`.
--- hint
`find('=')` gives the first `=`, or `std::string::npos` when there is none. The key is everything before it and the value everything after, each trimmed. `out[key] = …` replaces an earlier value, so the later line wins.
--- check case | Comments, blanks and bad lines skipped
parse_config("# engine\n mode = burn \nthrust=90\n\nlabel =\nbad line\nmode=coast")
=> std::map<std::string, std::string>{{"label", ""}, {"mode", "coast"}, {"thrust", "90"}}
--- check case | Only the first = splits
parse_config("expr = a=b")
=> std::map<std::string, std::string>{{"expr", "a=b"}}
--- check case | An empty key is skipped, an indented comment too
parse_config(" = 5\n   # note = 1\nok=1")
=> std::map<std::string, std::string>{{"ok", "1"}}
--- check case | Empty text
parse_config("")
=> std::map<std::string, std::string>{}

+++ practice | Add up one column of a table
--- task
Write two functions for a small comma-separated table. No `main`.

- `std::optional<int> parse_field(const std::string& field)` returns the whole number in `field`, allowing spaces around it, or `std::nullopt` if the field is not exactly one whole number. `parse_field(" 42 ")` is 42; `parse_field("7x")`, `parse_field("")` and `parse_field("4 2")` are empty.
- `int sum_column(const std::string& table, std::size_t column)` goes through the lines of `table`, splits each at the commas, and adds up the fields at index `column` that `parse_field` accepts. A line that is too short, or a field that does not parse, is skipped.

For `"id,alt\n1, 1200\n2,n/a\n3,800\n4"`, `sum_column(table, 1)` is 2000: the header `alt` and `n/a` do not parse, and line `4` has no index 1.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> parse_field(const std::string& field) {
    std::istringstream in(field);
    int n;
    in >> n;
    return n;
}

int sum_column(const std::string& table, std::size_t column) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> parse_field(const std::string& field) {
    std::istringstream in(field);
    int n;
    if (!(in >> n)) return std::nullopt;
    in >> std::ws;
    if (!in.eof()) return std::nullopt;
    return n;
}

int sum_column(const std::string& table, std::size_t column) {
    std::istringstream lines(table);
    std::string line;
    int total = 0;
    while (std::getline(lines, line)) {
        std::istringstream row(line);
        std::string field;
        std::size_t i = 0;
        while (std::getline(row, field, ',')) {
            if (i == column) total += parse_field(field).value_or(0);
            ++i;
        }
    }
    return total;
}
--- hint
`parse_field` needs both checks from the lesson: the read into an `int` worked, **and** after `>> std::ws` the stream is at `eof()`.
--- hint
For each line, split it with `std::getline(row, field, ',')` in a loop while counting the index. When the index equals `column`, add `parse_field(field).value_or(0)`. A short line never reaches that index, so it adds nothing.
--- check test | Fields that parse
parse_field(" 42 ") == 42 && parse_field("-7") == -7 && parse_field("0") == 0
--- check test | Fields that do not
parse_field("7x") == std::nullopt && parse_field("") == std::nullopt && parse_field("4 2") == std::nullopt && parse_field("n/a") == std::nullopt
--- check case | A column with a header, junk and a short line
sum_column("id,alt\n1, 1200\n2,n/a\n3,800\n4", 1)
=> 2000
--- check case | The first column
sum_column("id,alt\n1, 1200\n2,n/a\n3,800\n4", 0)
=> 10
--- check case | A column nobody has
sum_column("1,2\n3,4", 5)
=> 0

+++ practice | Squash the whitespace
--- task
Write `std::string squash(const std::string& s)`. It returns the words of `s` with exactly one space between each pair of words, and no whitespace at either end. Tabs and newlines count as whitespace too. No `main`.

`squash("  main   engine\t\tstart \n")` is `"main engine start"`. Text with no words, like `""` or `" \t\n"`, gives `""`. A single word comes back alone: `squash(" go ")` is `"go"`.
--- starter
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string squash(const std::string& s) {
    std::istringstream in(s);
    std::string word;
    std::string out;
    while (in >> word) out += word + " ";
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string squash(const std::string& s) {
    std::istringstream in(s);
    std::string word;
    std::string out;
    while (in >> word) {
        if (!out.empty()) out += " ";
        out += word;
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Reading with `>>` already skips every kind of whitespace, so it hands you the words one by one.
--- hint
Put the space **before** every word except the first: add it only when `out` is not empty yet. Then there is never a space at the end.
--- check case | Runs of spaces, tabs and newlines
squash("  main   engine\t\tstart \n")
=> "main engine start"
--- check case | A single word
squash(" go ")
=> "go"
--- check case | Only whitespace
squash(" \t\n")
=> ""
--- check case | Empty text
squash("")
=> ""

+++ practice | Debug: 12abc is not a number
--- task
**Bug report:** "`parse_whole("12abc")` gives 12. It should give no value, because `12abc` is not a whole number."

`parse_whole` should return the whole number in `s`, with spaces allowed around it, or `std::nullopt` if `s` is anything else. Fix it so the whole text must be the number. No `main`.
--- starter

#include <sstream>
#include <string>

// The whole number in s, spaces allowed around it, or no value.
std::optional<int> parse_whole(const std::string& s) {

--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

// The whole number in s, spaces allowed around it, or no value.
std::optional<int> parse_whole(const std::string& s) {
    std::istringstream in(s);
    int n;
    if (!(in >> n)) return std::nullopt;
    in >> std::ws;
    if (!in.eof()) return std::nullopt;
    return n;
}
--- hint
`>>` stops at the first character that cannot be part of a number, and the read still succeeds. So something must check what is left afterwards.
--- hint
After the read, skip trailing spaces with `in >> std::ws`, then return `std::nullopt` unless `in.eof()` is true.
--- check test | Junk after the number is refused

--- check test | Nothing to read
parse_whole("") == std::nullopt && parse_whole("   ") == std::nullopt && parse_whole("abc") == std::nullopt

+++ practice | Stretch: sum up a mission log
--- task
Each line of a mission log is a time, a level, then a message, separated by spaces: `"12:04:33 WARN battery low"`. The level is `INFO`, `WARN` or `ERROR`.

```cpp
struct LogSummary {
    int info;
    int warn;
    int error;
    std::string first_error;
};
```

Write `LogSummary summarize_log(const std::string& log)`. No `main`.

- Count the lines of each level.
- `first_error` is the time of the first `ERROR` line, or `""` if there is none.
- Skip any line that does not have both a time and a level, or whose level is not one of the three. The message may be missing.

For `"12:00:01 INFO boot\n12:00:05 WARN temp high\n\n12:01:10 ERROR pump\nnoise\n12:02:00 ERROR valve\n12:03:00 DEBUG x"`: 1 info, 1 warn, 2 errors, and `first_error` is `"12:01:10"`.
--- starter
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

struct LogSummary {
    int info;
    int warn;
    int error;
    std::string first_error;
};

LogSummary summarize_log(const std::string& log) {
    LogSummary s;
    return s;
}
--- solution
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

struct LogSummary {
    int info;
    int warn;
    int error;
    std::string first_error;
};

LogSummary summarize_log(const std::string& log) {
    LogSummary s{0, 0, 0, ""};
    std::istringstream lines(log);
    std::string line;
    while (std::getline(lines, line)) {
        std::istringstream in(line);
        std::string time;
        std::string level;
        if (!(in >> time >> level)) continue;
        if (level == "INFO") {
            ++s.info;
        } else if (level == "WARN") {
            ++s.warn;
        } else if (level == "ERROR") {
            if (s.error == 0) s.first_error = time;
            ++s.error;
        }
    }
    return s;
}
--- hint
Read the log a line at a time with `getline`, and give each line its own `std::istringstream`. `in >> time >> level` is true only when both words were there.
--- hint
Start the summary with every count at 0: `LogSummary s{0, 0, 0, ""};`. An `ERROR` line records its time only while the error count is still 0.
--- check test | Counts and the first error
[] { LogSummary s = summarize_log("12:00:01 INFO boot\n12:00:05 WARN temp high\n\n12:01:10 ERROR pump\nnoise\n12:02:00 ERROR valve\n12:03:00 DEBUG x"); return s.info == 1 && s.warn == 1 && s.error == 2 && s.first_error == "12:01:10"; }()
--- check test | No errors
[] { LogSummary s = summarize_log("09:00:00 INFO a\n09:00:01 INFO b"); return s.info == 2 && s.warn == 0 && s.error == 0 && s.first_error == ""; }()
--- check test | A level with no message still counts
[] { LogSummary s = summarize_log("  10:00:00   WARN  \n10:00:02 ERROR"); return s.warn == 1 && s.error == 1 && s.first_error == "10:00:02"; }()
--- check test | Empty log
[] { LogSummary s = summarize_log(""); return s.info == 0 && s.warn == 0 && s.error == 0 && s.first_error.empty(); }()

=== cpp2-11b | Building text with std::ostringstream
--- teach
Last lesson you took text apart: splitting it, trimming it, and reading numbers out of it. This lesson goes the other way and builds text up, with numbers written exactly the way you want them.

### Writing to a string

Picture writing on a notepad instead of shouting across the room. `std::cout` sends text straight to the screen. A **`std::ostringstream`** (output string stream, from `<sstream>`) collects the text instead, and hands it over as a `std::string` when you ask. You write to it with `<<`, read "put to", exactly as you write to `std::cout`. Then **`.str()`** gives you everything written so far:

```cpp
std::ostringstream out;
out << "ALT=" << 4200 << " m";
std::string line = out.str();   // "ALT=4200 m"
```

Numbers turn into text on their own, the same way they do when you print them.

### A fixed number of decimal places

Readings often need a set number of decimal places. The header `<iomanip>` (input/output manipulators) has the tools. **`std::fixed`** says "write numbers with a decimal point, never in [[the e-notation style|scientific]]". **`std::setprecision(n)`**, with `std::fixed`, says "exactly `n` digits after the point". The number is rounded to fit:

```cpp
std::ostringstream out;
out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1) << 3.14159;
// out.str() is "3.1"
```

These settings [[stay on the stream|sticky]] until you change them, so every later `double` written to `out` also gets one decimal place. They even add zeros: with `std::setprecision(2)`, the value 7.0 is written `7.00`.

**Watch out:** `std::setprecision` without `std::fixed` means something else: the number of *significant* digits, counted from the first non-zero digit. With `std::setprecision(2)` alone, 3.14159 becomes `3.1`, which looks right, but 1234.5 becomes `1.2e+03`. Always put `std::fixed` in front when you mean decimal places.

### A list with separators

To write a list like `3, 1, 4`, put the separator **before** every item except the first. Then there is no stray separator at either end:

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {3, 1, 4};
std::ostringstream out;
for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) {
    if (i > 0) out << ", ";
    out << v[i];
}
// out.str() is "3, 1, 4"
```

An empty vector writes nothing, and one value is written alone. You will use these tools again at the end of this course, when [[a report gets its own function|comes-back]].

::: context scientific What 1.2e+03 means
`1.2e+03` is *scientific notation*, the computer way of writing $1.2 \times 10^3$, which is 1200. The `e` stands for "exponent": the power of ten. Very large and very small numbers are easier to read this way: the distance to the Moon, about 384,400 km, is 3.844e+05 km. Without `std::fixed`, a stream switches to this style whenever a number has more digits before the point than the precision allows, or is very small, so a column of readings can suddenly change style halfway down. `std::fixed` stops that.
:::

::: context sticky Settings that stay
`std::fixed` and `std::setprecision` change the stream itself, not just the next number, so they stay until you change them again. That is handy for writing a whole table. It is also a trap if you share `std::cout` with other code: set it to one decimal place, and every number printed afterwards has one decimal place. A fresh `std::ostringstream` for each piece of text keeps the settings from leaking. One manipulator is different: `std::setw`, which sets a column width, applies only to the next item.
:::

::: context comes-back Where this comes back
The design lesson at the end of this course has you tidy up a program that builds a report of readings. Its summary line uses exactly these tools: an `std::ostringstream`, `std::fixed` and `std::setprecision(1)`. Building the text in a string first, instead of printing it straight away, also makes a function easy to test, because a check can compare the string it returns.
:::
--- task
Write two functions that build text. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `std::string reading(const std::string& name, double value)` returns the name, a colon and a space, then the value with exactly one decimal place. `reading("ALT", 4200.0)` is `"ALT: 4200.0"`, `reading("TEMP", -3.14159)` is `"TEMP: -3.1"`, and `reading("V", 2.96)` is `"V: 3.0"`.
- `std::string join(const std::vector<int>& v, const std::string& sep)` returns the values written one after another with `sep` between them. `join({3, 1, 4}, ", ")` is `"3, 1, 4"`, `join({7}, "-")` is `"7"`, and `join({}, ",")` is `""`.

Build both strings with a `std::ostringstream`.
--- starter
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string reading(const std::string& name, double value) {
    return "";
}

std::string join(const std::vector<int>& v, const std::string& sep) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string reading(const std::string& name, double value) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << name << ": " << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1) << value;
    return out.str();
}

std::string join(const std::vector<int>& v, const std::string& sep) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) out << sep;
        out << v[i];
    }
    return out.str();
}
--- hint
In each function, make a `std::ostringstream out;`, write to it with `<<`, and finish with `return out.str();`.
--- hint
For `reading`, write `name`, then `": "`, then `std::fixed` and `std::setprecision(1)`, then `value`, all in one chain of `<<`.
--- hint
For `join`, loop over the indexes. Before each value except the first (`if (i > 0)`), write `sep`; then write `v[i]`.
--- check case | A whole number gets one decimal place
reading("ALT", 4200.0)
=> "ALT: 4200.0"
--- check case | A long decimal is cut to one place
reading("TEMP", -3.14159)
=> "TEMP: -3.1"
--- check case | Rounding can carry into the whole number
reading("V", 2.96)
=> "V: 3.0"
--- check case | join puts the separator between values
join({3, 1, 4}, ", ")
=> "3, 1, 4"
--- check case | join of one value has no separator
join({7}, "-")
=> "7"
--- check case | join of nothing is empty
join({}, ",")
=> ""
--- check source | Uses an ostringstream
ostringstream

+++ practice | A percentage with one decimal place
--- task
Write `std::string percent(int part, int whole)`. It returns `part` as a percentage of `whole`, with exactly one decimal place and a `%` sign after it. Build it with a `std::ostringstream`. No `main`.

- `percent(3, 7)` is `"42.9%"`, because 3 ÷ 7 × 100 is 42.857…
- `percent(1, 4)` is `"25.0%"`, and `percent(3, 2)` is `"150.0%"`.
- When `whole` is 0 there is no percentage: return `"n/a"`.
--- starter
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string percent(int part, int whole) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << std::setprecision(1) << part * 100 / whole << "%";
    return out.str();
}
--- solution
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string percent(int part, int whole) {
    if (whole == 0) return "n/a";
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1) << 100.0 * part / whole << "%";
    return out.str();
}
--- hint
`part * 100 / whole` is whole-number arithmetic, so the decimals are gone before anything is printed. Start the sum with a `double`: `100.0 * part / whole`.
--- hint
One decimal place needs both `std::fixed` and `std::setprecision(1)`. And check `whole == 0` before you divide.
--- check case | Rounded to one place
percent(3, 7)
=> "42.9%"
--- check case | A whole number still shows .0
percent(1, 4)
=> "25.0%"
--- check case | Over 100
percent(3, 2)
=> "150.0%"
--- check case | Nothing out of nothing
percent(0, 0)
=> "n/a"
--- check case | Zero out of something
percent(0, 5)
=> "0.0%"

+++ practice | One row of a spreadsheet
--- task
Write `std::string csv_row(const std::vector<double>& values, int places = 2)`. It returns the values separated by commas, with no spaces, each written with exactly `places` decimal places. No `main`.

- `csv_row({1.5, -0.25, 10})` is `"1.50,-0.25,10.00"`, because `places` defaults to 2.
- `csv_row({3.14159, 2.0}, 3)` is `"3.142,2.000"`.
- `csv_row({7.6}, 0)` is `"8"`: 0 places rounds to a whole number.
- `csv_row({})` is `""`.
--- starter
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string csv_row(const std::vector<double>& values, int places) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    for (double v : values) out << v << ",";
    return out.str();
}
--- solution
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string csv_row(const std::vector<double>& values, int places = 2) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(places);
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < values.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) out << ",";
        out << values[i];
    }
    return out.str();
}
--- hint
The settings stay on the stream, so set `std::fixed` and `std::setprecision(places)` once, before the loop.
--- hint
Write the comma before every value except the first, and give `places` its default in the parameter list: `int places = 2`.
--- check case | Two places by default
csv_row({1.5, -0.25, 10})
=> "1.50,-0.25,10.00"
--- check case | Three places
csv_row({3.14159, 2.0}, 3)
=> "3.142,2.000"
--- check case | Zero places rounds
csv_row({7.6}, 0)
=> "8"
--- check case | No values
csv_row({})
=> ""

+++ practice | A stock report from a map
--- task
Write `std::string stock_report(const std::map<std::string, int>& stock)`. It returns every item as `name=count`, in the map's alphabetical order, separated by a semicolon and a space. An empty map gives `"(empty)"`. Build it with a `std::ostringstream`. No `main`.

`stock_report({{"nut", 0}, {"bolt", 12}, {"washer", 5}})` is `"bolt=12; nut=0; washer=5"`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string stock_report(const std::map<std::string, int>& stock) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    for (const auto& [name, count] : stock) out << name << "=" << count << "; ";
    return out.str();
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string stock_report(const std::map<std::string, int>& stock) {
    if (stock.empty()) return "(empty)";
    std::ostringstream out;
    bool first = true;
    for (const auto& [name, count] : stock) {
        if (!first) out << "; ";
        out << name << "=" << count;
        first = false;
    }
    return out.str();
}
--- hint
A map has no index `i` to test with `i > 0`. Keep a `bool first = true;` instead, write the separator only when it is `false`, and set it to `false` after each item.
--- hint
Check `stock.empty()` first and return `"(empty)"`.
--- check case | Items in alphabetical order
stock_report({{"nut", 0}, {"bolt", 12}, {"washer", 5}})
=> "bolt=12; nut=0; washer=5"
--- check case | One item has no separator
stock_report({{"fuse", 3}})
=> "fuse=3"
--- check case | Negative counts are written as they are
stock_report({{"debt", -2}, {"cash", 7}})
=> "cash=7; debt=-2"
--- check case | Empty
stock_report({})
=> "(empty)"

+++ practice | A list the way people write it
--- task
Write `std::string english_list(const std::vector<std::string>& items)`. It joins the items the way people write a list in English, with `"and"` before the last one. No `main`.

- No items: `""`
- One item: `"Mars"`
- Two items: `"Mars and Venus"`
- Three or more: commas between all but the last two, then `" and "`: `"Mars, Venus and Earth"`, `"a, b, c and d"`.
--- starter
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string english_list(const std::vector<std::string>& items) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < items.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) out << ", ";
        out << items[i];
    }
    return out.str();
}
--- solution
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string english_list(const std::vector<std::string>& items) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < items.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0 && i + 1 == items.size()) {
            out << " and ";
        } else if (i > 0) {
            out << ", ";
        }
        out << items[i];
    }
    return out.str();
}
--- hint
The separator before item `i` depends on where `i` is: nothing before the first item, `" and "` before the last one, and `", "` before any other.
--- hint
Item `i` is the last one when `i + 1 == items.size()`. Adding instead of subtracting means an empty list never wraps round.
--- check case | Three items
english_list({"Mars", "Venus", "Earth"})
=> "Mars, Venus and Earth"
--- check case | Four items
english_list({"a", "b", "c", "d"})
=> "a, b, c and d"
--- check case | Two items
english_list({"Mars", "Venus"})
=> "Mars and Venus"
--- check case | One item
english_list({"Mars"})
=> "Mars"
--- check case | No items
english_list({})
=> ""

`money` should write an amount of dollars with a `$` in front and exactly two decimal places. Find the cause and fix it; it is one missing piece. No `main`.

#include <string>

// Dollars with exactly two decimal places: 1234.5 is "$1234.50".
std::string money(double dollars) {

    out << "$" << std::fixed << std::setprecision(2) << dollars;
