+++ practice | Stretch: a telemetry frame
--- task
A ground station prints each telemetry frame on one line. Write `std::string frame(int t, const std::map<std::string, double>& values)`. No `main`.

- It starts with the time: `T`, then `+` for 0 or more seconds or `-` for negative seconds, then the number of seconds without its sign, with zeros in front to make at least 4 digits. So 12 is `T+0012`, -5 is `T-0005`, 0 is `T+0000` and 123456 is `T+123456`.
- Then, for each value in the map's order, `" | "`, the name, `=`, and the value with exactly one decimal place.

`frame(12, {{"vel", -3.24}, {"alt", 1200.46}})` is `"T+0012 | alt=1200.5 | vel=-3.2"`, and `frame(-5, {})` is `"T-0005"`.
--- starter
#include <iomanip>
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string frame(int t, const std::map<std::string, double>& values) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << "T+" << t;
    for (const auto& [name, v] : values) out << " | " << name << "=" << v;
    return out.str();
}
--- solution
#include <iomanip>
#include <map>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string frame(int t, const std::map<std::string, double>& values) {
    std::string digits = std::to_string(t < 0 ? -t : t);
    while (digits.size() < 4) digits = "0" + digits;
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << "T" << (t < 0 ? "-" : "+") << digits;
    out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1);
    for (const auto& [name, v] : values) out << " | " << name << "=" << v;
    return out.str();
}
--- hint
Build the time's digits as text first: `std::to_string` of `t` without its sign, then put `"0"` in front while it is shorter than 4 characters.
--- hint
Write the sign with the conditional operator, then set `std::fixed` and `std::setprecision(1)` once before writing the values.
--- check case | Two values, in the map's order
frame(12, {{"vel", -3.24}, {"alt", 1200.46}})
=> "T+0012 | alt=1200.5 | vel=-3.2"
--- check case | Negative time, no values
frame(-5, {})
=> "T-0005"
--- check case | Time zero
frame(0, {{"v", 2.0}})
=> "T+0000 | v=2.0"
--- check case | More than four digits
frame(123456, {{"alt", 0.04}})
=> "T+123456 | alt=0.0"

=== cpp2-12 | Debugging: off by one, out of range
--- teach
Last lesson you built text with a string stream. This one is a debugging lesson: you get a bug report and a broken function, and your job is to find the cause and fix it there. The bug is the most common one in loops.

### The fence-post mistake

A fence 10 meters long, with a post every meter, needs 11 posts, not 10: one at each end. Counting the gaps when you meant the posts, or the other way round, is so easy that it has a name. An **off-by-one** error means a loop runs one time too many, or one time too few. It is also called a [[fence-post error|fence-post]].

In C++ it is dangerous, because `v[i]` does no checking, as you saw in the basics vector lesson. Reading one past the end does not stop the program. It reads [[whatever memory happens to be there|past-the-end]] (undefined behavior) and carries on with a wrong number.

### A method for loops that misbehave

**1. Reproduce small.** Pick an input so small that you can work out the right answer by hand. `{1, 2, 3, 4}` is better than a thousand readings.

**2. Trace the boundaries.** Write down the first and last value of every loop variable, and the largest index it touches. For a `v` of size 4, the valid indexes are 0 to 3. Anything that reaches 4 is a bug:

```cpp
for (std::size_t i = 0; i <= v.size(); ++i) {   // i runs 0, 1, 2, 3, 4
    sum += v[i];                                // v[4] is past the end
}
```

`<=`, "less than or equal", lets `i` reach `v.size()` itself. `<`, "less than", stops one earlier, at the last real index.

**3. Check the edge inputs.** Try an empty vector, a vector of one element, and the sizes where the loop condition flips from true to false. Bugs cluster at the edges.

**4. Make out-of-range loud while you look.** Swap `v[i]` for `v.at(i)`. `at` checks the index, and when it is out of range it [[stops the program at once|at-cost]] with an `std::out_of_range` error, instead of handing back garbage.

### Windows of values

The function in this lesson's bug report adds up **windows**: every run of `k` values that sit next to each other. For `{1, 2, 3, 4}` and `k` of 2, the windows are `{1, 2}`, `{2, 3}` and `{3, 4}`, and their sums are 3, 5 and 7.

A window starting at index `i` uses `v[i]` up to `v[i + k - 1]`: exactly `k` values. A vector of size 4 has 4 − 2 + 1 = 3 windows of size 2, starting at 0, 1 and 2.

### The sneakier trap: unsigned wrap-around

`v.size()` gives a `std::size_t`, which is **unsigned**: it cannot be negative. So when an answer would go below zero, it does not. It [[wraps round to a huge number|wrap-around]] instead:

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {1, 2};
std::size_t k = 3;
std::size_t gap = v.size() - k;   // not -1: 18446744073709551615
```

A loop like `for (i = 0; i <= v.size() - k; ++i)` then runs almost forever, reading far outside the vector.

### Fix the cause

The cause-level fix is to write the condition so it never subtracts at all. `i <= v.size() - k` and `i + k <= v.size()` say the same thing when nothing wraps, because you move `k` to the other side. The second form only adds, so it cannot go below zero, and when `k` is bigger than the vector it is false straight away.

Then handle the impossible cases up front. A window of size 0 holds nothing, so when `k == 0`, return an empty result before the loop.

**Watch out:** do not patch the symptom. Adding `if (k > v.size()) return {};` in front of the wrong condition makes the crash go away for today's input, but the risky subtraction is still there, one careless edit away from wrapping again. Rewrite the condition itself. And do not wait for the compiler: `-Wall -Wextra` gives no warning for either version, so the tracing is up to you.

::: context fence-post Why "fence post"
The name comes from exactly the puzzle in the explanation: a straight fence of 10 sections needs 11 posts. In code, it shows up whenever you mix up counting things with counting the gaps between them. A range from index 3 to index 7, both included, holds 7 − 3 + 1 = 5 values, not 4. The window count in this lesson, `size - k + 1`, has the same "+ 1" for the same reason.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="30" y1="50" x2="330" y2="50" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="3"/>
  <g fill="#1d6fd1">
    <rect x="26" y="30" width="8" height="40"/><rect x="101" y="30" width="8" height="40"/>
    <rect x="176" y="30" width="8" height="40"/><rect x="251" y="30" width="8" height="40"/>
    <rect x="326" y="30" width="8" height="40"/>
  </g>
  <g font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">
    <text x="67" y="25">gap 1</text><text x="142" y="25">gap 2</text><text x="217" y="25">gap 3</text><text x="292" y="25">gap 4</text>
  </g>
  <text x="180" y="95" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4 gaps need 5 posts</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context past-the-end What sits one past the end
A vector's elements sit side by side in one block of memory. The bytes right after the last element belong to something else: spare room the vector has reserved, or another variable entirely. Reading there gives you whatever those bytes hold, and writing there can quietly damage other data. Tools called sanitizers catch many of these reads while the program runs; with g++ or clang you turn one on with `-fsanitize=address`. They slow the program down, so they are used while testing, not in the final build.
:::

::: context at-cost Why [] does not check
Checking every index takes a comparison each time, and in a tight loop over millions of values C++ lets you skip it. `at` is the checked version for when you want safety over speed. Flight software aims to make the check unnecessary instead. NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" rules require every loop to have a fixed upper bound that a checking tool can prove, and ask code to check that the values handed to a function are valid. A loop whose bound can wrap around to 18 quintillion breaks both rules.
:::

::: context wrap-around A counter that rolls over
Picture a car's trip counter rolling back from 000.0 to 999.9 when it goes backwards: an unsigned number going below 0 wraps to its largest value. On a 64-bit computer that is 18,446,744,073,709,551,615, which is 2⁶⁴ − 1. So 2 − 3 gives that number, not −1. C++ defines this wrap-around for unsigned numbers on purpose, so there is no warning. Many experts, including C++'s creator Bjarne Stroustrup, have said that making sizes unsigned was a mistake. C++20 added `std::ssize(v)`, which gives the size as a signed number that can go below zero.
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** "`window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2)` should be `{3, 5, 7}`, the sums of each pair of neighbors, but the numbers are wrong. And when `k` is bigger than the list, the program crashes."

`window_sums(v, k)` returns the sum of every window of `k` values next to each other, in order. If `k` is 0 or bigger than `v.size()`, it returns an empty vector. `window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 4)` is `{10}`, and `window_sums({5, -2, 8}, 1)` is `{5, -2, 8}`.

There are two bugs. Find both and fix their causes, not the symptoms. No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <vector>

// The sum of every run of k consecutive values:
// window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2) is {3, 5, 7}.
std::vector<int> window_sums(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i <= v.size() - k; ++i) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (std::size_t j = 0; j <= k; ++j) {
            sum += v[i + j];
        }
        out.push_back(sum);
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

// The sum of every run of k consecutive values:
// window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2) is {3, 5, 7}.
std::vector<int> window_sums(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    if (k == 0) return out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i + k <= v.size(); ++i) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (std::size_t j = 0; j < k; ++j) {
            sum += v[i + j];
        }
        out.push_back(sum);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Reproduce small: trace `window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2)` by hand. For `k` of 2, which values of `j` does the inner loop run through, and how many values does it add each time? It should be exactly `k`.
--- hint
Now the crash: when `k` is bigger than `v.size()`, what is `v.size() - k` for an unsigned type? Rewrite the outer condition so nothing is subtracted, by moving `k` to the other side.
--- hint
The inner loop should be `j < k`, and the outer one `i + k <= v.size()`. A window of size 0 makes no sense, so start the function with `if (k == 0) return out;`.
--- check case | Pairs of neighbours
window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2)
=> std::vector<int>{3, 5, 7}
--- check case | k equal to the size gives one sum
window_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 4)
=> std::vector<int>{10}
--- check case | k of 1 gives the values back
window_sums({5, -2, 8}, 1)
=> std::vector<int>{5, -2, 8}
--- check case | k of 0 gives nothing
window_sums({1, 2, 3}, 0)
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | k bigger than the list gives nothing
window_sums({1, 2}, 3)
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | Differences between neighbours
--- task
Write `std::vector<int> differences(const std::vector<int>& v)`. It returns each value minus the one before it, in order: `v[1] - v[0]`, then `v[2] - v[1]`, and so on. No `main`.

`differences({3, 7, 4, 4})` is `{4, -3, 0}`. A vector of size `n` gives `n - 1` differences, so one value gives `{}`, and so does an empty vector.

Write the loop so that it never subtracts from `v.size()`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> differences(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        out.push_back(v[i + 1] - v[i]);
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> differences(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 1; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        out.push_back(v[i] - v[i - 1]);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Trace `{3, 7}` by hand: how many differences should there be, and which indexes does the starter's loop touch?
--- hint
Start the loop at `i = 1` and compare each `v[i]` with `v[i - 1]`. Then `i < v.size()` is the whole condition, and an empty or one-value vector never enters the loop.
--- check case | Three differences
differences({3, 7, 4, 4})
=> std::vector<int>{4, -3, 0}
--- check case | Two values give one difference
differences({10, 2})
=> std::vector<int>{-8}
--- check case | One value gives none
differences({5})
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | Empty gives none
differences({})
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | The best window, maybe
--- task
Write `std::optional<int> max_window_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k)`. It returns the biggest sum of `k` values next to each other. If there is no window at all, because `k` is 0 or bigger than `v.size()`, it returns `std::nullopt`. No `main`.

`max_window_sum({2, -1, 5, 1, -4}, 2)` is 6 (from 5 + 1), and `max_window_sum({-3, -1, -2}, 2)` is -3 (from -1 + -2): the answer can be negative.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> max_window_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k) {
    int best = 0;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i <= v.size() - k; ++i) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (std::size_t j = i; j < i + k; ++j) sum += v[j];
        if (sum > best) best = sum;
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

std::optional<int> max_window_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k) {
    if (k == 0 || k > v.size()) return std::nullopt;
    std::optional<int> best = std::nullopt;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i + k <= v.size(); ++i) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (std::size_t j = i; j < i + k; ++j) sum += v[j];
        if (!best || sum > *best) best = sum;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Three things go wrong in the starter: `v.size() - k` wraps round when `k` is bigger, the best starts at 0 even when every sum is negative, and there is no "no window" answer.
--- hint
Return `std::nullopt` up front when `k == 0 || k > v.size()`. Loop while `i + k <= v.size()`, and let the best be an optional that starts empty, so the first window always becomes the best.
--- check test | The best pair
max_window_sum({2, -1, 5, 1, -4}, 2) == 6
--- check test | All negative
max_window_sum({-3, -1, -2}, 2) == -3
--- check test | k equal to the size, and k of 1
max_window_sum({4, -2, 3}, 3) == 5 && max_window_sum({4, -2, 3}, 1) == 4
--- check test | No window
max_window_sum({1, 2}, 3) == std::nullopt && max_window_sum({1, 2}, 0) == std::nullopt && max_window_sum({}, 1) == std::nullopt

+++ practice | Letter pairs in a word
--- task
Write two functions that look at neighbouring letters. No `main`.

- `std::vector<std::string> bigrams(const std::string& s)` returns every piece of two letters next to each other, in order: `bigrams("orbit")` is `{"or", "rb", "bi", "it"}`. A word with fewer than two letters has none.
- `int count_doubles(const std::string& s)` returns how many positions hold the same character as the next one: `count_doubles("balloon")` is 2 (the `ll` and the `oo`), and `count_doubles("aaa")` is 2.

Use `substr` from the basics course for the pieces, and write every loop condition so it only adds.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> bigrams(const std::string& s) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i <= s.size() - 2; ++i) out.push_back(s.substr(i, 2));
    return out;
}

int count_doubles(const std::string& s) {
    int n = 0;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < s.size(); ++i) {
        if (s[i] == s[i + 1]) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> bigrams(const std::string& s) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i + 2 <= s.size(); ++i) out.push_back(s.substr(i, 2));
    return out;
}

int count_doubles(const std::string& s) {
    int n = 0;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i + 1 < s.size(); ++i) {
        if (s[i] == s[i + 1]) ++n;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
`s.size() - 2` wraps round to a huge number for `""` and `"a"`. Move the 2 to the other side: `i + 2 <= s.size()`.
--- hint
In `count_doubles`, `s[i + 1]` must exist, so the loop runs while `i + 1 < s.size()`.
--- check case | Pairs in orbit
bigrams("orbit")
=> std::vector<std::string>{"or", "rb", "bi", "it"}
--- check case | One letter has no pairs
bigrams("a")
=> std::vector<std::string>{}
--- check case | Empty has no pairs
bigrams("")
=> std::vector<std::string>{}
--- check test | Doubles counted, and none at the very end
count_doubles("balloon") == 2 && count_doubles("aaa") == 2 && count_doubles("ab") == 0
--- check test | Short strings have no doubles
count_doubles("") == 0 && count_doubles("z") == 0

+++ practice | The last n readings
--- task
Write `std::vector<int> last_n(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t n)`. It returns the last `n` values of `v`, in their original order. If `n` is bigger than the size, it returns all of `v`, and if `n` is 0 it returns `{}`. No `main`.

`last_n({4, 8, 15, 16, 23}, 2)` is `{16, 23}`, and `last_n({4, 8}, 5)` is `{4, 8}`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> last_n(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t n) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (std::size_t i = v.size() - n; i < v.size(); ++i) out.push_back(v[i]);
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> last_n(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t n) {
    std::size_t start = n >= v.size() ? 0 : v.size() - n;
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (std::size_t i = start; i < v.size(); ++i) out.push_back(v[i]);
    return out;
}
--- hint
The first index to copy is `v.size() - n`, but only when `n` is smaller than the size. Otherwise that subtraction wraps round.
--- hint
Work out the start first: `0` when `n >= v.size()`, and `v.size() - n` otherwise. Then copy from there to the end.
--- check case | The last two
last_n({4, 8, 15, 16, 23}, 2)
=> std::vector<int>{16, 23}
--- check case | More than there are
last_n({4, 8}, 5)
=> std::vector<int>{4, 8}
--- check case | Exactly the size
last_n({4, 8}, 2)
=> std::vector<int>{4, 8}
--- check case | Zero
last_n({4, 8}, 0)
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | From an empty vector
last_n({}, 3)
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | Debug: an extra chunk of nothing
--- task
**Bug report:** "`chunk_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2)` gives `{3, 7, 0}`. The last 0 should not be there. `chunk_sums({1, 2, 3}, 2)` is right: `{3, 3}`."

`chunk_sums(v, size)` cuts `v` into chunks of `size` values, in order, and returns the sum of each chunk. The last chunk may be shorter. A `size` of 0 gives `{}`. Trace the boundary, find the cause and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

// Sums of consecutive chunks of `size` values; the last chunk may be shorter.
std::vector<int> chunk_sums(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t size) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    if (size == 0) return out;
    for (std::size_t start = 0; start <= v.size(); start += size) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (std::size_t j = start; j < start + size && j < v.size(); ++j) sum += v[j];
        out.push_back(sum);
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

// Sums of consecutive chunks of `size` values; the last chunk may be shorter.
std::vector<int> chunk_sums(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t size) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    if (size == 0) return out;
    for (std::size_t start = 0; start < v.size(); start += size) {
        int sum = 0;
        for (std::size_t j = start; j < start + size && j < v.size(); ++j) sum += v[j];
        out.push_back(sum);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Write down every value of `start` for `{1, 2, 3, 4}` and `size` 2: 0, 2, then 4. Is there a chunk that starts at index 4?
--- hint
A chunk must start at a real index, so the outer condition is `start < v.size()`, not `<=`.
--- check case | An even split
chunk_sums({1, 2, 3, 4}, 2)
=> std::vector<int>{3, 7}
--- check case | A shorter last chunk
chunk_sums({1, 2, 3}, 2)
=> std::vector<int>{3, 3}
--- check case | One big chunk
chunk_sums({5, -1, 2}, 10)
=> std::vector<int>{6}
--- check case | Nothing to chunk
chunk_sums({}, 3)
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | A size of 0
chunk_sums({1, 2}, 0)
=> std::vector<int>{}

+++ practice | Stretch: find the peaks
--- task
A **peak** in a list of readings is a value that is strictly bigger than each neighbour it has. A value in the middle has two neighbours; the first and last values have only one each. Write `std::vector<std::size_t> peaks(const std::vector<int>& v)` that returns the index of every peak, in order. No `main`.

- `peaks({1, 3, 2, 5, 4})` is `{1, 3}`.
- `peaks({9, 1, 7})` is `{0, 2}`: the ends count when they beat their one neighbour.
- A single value has no neighbours at all, so it counts as a peak: `peaks({4})` is `{0}`.
- Equal neighbours stop a peak: `peaks({2, 2})` is `{}`. An empty list gives `{}`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::size_t> peaks(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::vector<std::size_t> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 1; i < v.size() - 1; ++i) {
        if (v[i] > v[i - 1] && v[i] > v[i + 1]) out.push_back(i);
    }
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::size_t> peaks(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::vector<std::size_t> out;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        bool beats_left = i == 0 || v[i] > v[i - 1];
        bool beats_right = i + 1 == v.size() || v[i] > v[i + 1];
        if (beats_left && beats_right) out.push_back(i);
    }
    return out;
}
--- hint
Visit every index from 0, and ask two questions: does it beat its left neighbour, and does it beat its right neighbour? A side with no neighbour counts as beaten.
--- hint
`i == 0 || v[i] > v[i - 1]` only reads `v[i - 1]` when `i` is not 0, because `||` stops as soon as its left side is true. The right side is the same idea with `i + 1 == v.size()`.
--- check case | Peaks in the middle
peaks({1, 3, 2, 5, 4})
=> std::vector<std::size_t>{1, 3}
--- check case | Peaks at both ends
peaks({9, 1, 7})
=> std::vector<std::size_t>{0, 2}
--- check case | A single value is a peak
peaks({4})
=> std::vector<std::size_t>{0}
--- check case | Equal neighbours are not peaks
peaks({2, 2})
=> std::vector<std::size_t>{}
--- check case | Empty
peaks({})
=> std::vector<std::size_t>{}
--- check case | A plateau is not a peak
peaks({1, 5, 5, 1, 3})
=> std::vector<std::size_t>{4}

=== cpp2-13 | Debugging: overflow and uninitialised values
--- teach
Last lesson, "Debugging: off by one, out of range", you chased loops that ran one step too far and a subtraction that wrapped round to a huge number. Those bugs often end in a crash, which at least tells you something is wrong. This lesson is about two bugs that give you a number that is quietly **wrong**, with no crash and no error message to point at them.

### Bug one: a number too big for its box

Picture the mileage counter in an old car. It has six digits, so after 999,999 miles it rolls over to 000,000. The car did not travel backwards; the counter ran out of room.

An `int` is a box of fixed size too. It holds whole numbers up to 2,147,483,647, about 2 billion. Going past the top is called **integer overflow**: a result too big for the type that holds it.

```cpp
int a = 2000000000;    // 2 billion: fits
int b = 2000000000;    // fits
int c = a + b;         // 4 billion does not fit in an int
```

For an `int`, which can be negative (a **signed** type), overflow is **[[undefined behavior|ub-overflow]]**: the C++ rules make no promise at all about what happens. In practice you usually get a [[large negative number|wrap-around]]. Here `c` usually comes out as -294,967,296. But the compiler is also allowed to assume overflow never happens, and plan the rest of the program around that, so the wrong answer can change from one build to the next.

### The fix: a box big enough for the result

The cure is to pick a type big enough for the **result**, not only for the inputs. The basics course met `long long`, which goes up to about 9 × 10¹⁸ (a 9 with 18 more digits after it). Two `int`s that each fit can still add up to a total that does not, so think about the biggest total, not the biggest single value. Real systems have failed because a count kept growing until it [[outgrew its type|boeing-counter]].

### Where overflow hides: std::accumulate

A classic hiding place is `std::accumulate`, from the `<numeric>` header, which you met in the `<algorithm>` lesson. It adds up a range, and it does the adding in the **type of its starting value**, the last thing you pass it:

```cpp
std::vector<int> v = {2000000000, 2000000000};
std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0);     // int arithmetic: overflows
std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0LL);   // long long: 4000000000
```

The only difference is the starting value. `0` on its own is an `int`. The letters `LL` stuck on the end, read "long long", make `0LL` a [[long long zero|literal-types]], so every step of the adding happens in the big box.

**Watch out:** declaring the function as returning `long long` does not help. The damage is done inside, during the adding, before the result is converted to `long long` on the way out. The same is true of a plain sum:

```cpp
long long r = a + b;                            // still overflows: a + b is int arithmetic
long long s = static_cast<long long>(a) + b;    // 4000000000
```

In the second line, `static_cast<long long>(a)` turns `a` into a `long long` first, so the `+` happens in the big box.

### Bug two: a variable with no starting value

`int lowest;` does not mean zero. A local variable made without a starting value (an **initializer**) holds [[whatever was left in that memory|leftover-memory]]. Reading it before you store something in it is undefined behavior too: the program may seem to work today and fail tomorrow.

```cpp
int lowest;            // no initialiser: leftover junk
int count = 0;         // initialised: really 0
```

### How to hunt both

- **Read the warnings.** Compile with `-Wall` ("warn all") and `-Wextra` ("warn extra"), the way this checker does. Compilers flag many uninitialised reads, but [[not every one|warnings-not-enough]], so a clean compile is not proof.
- **Test the edges** where these bugs live: very large values, lists that are all negative or all positive, and a list with a single element.
- **Fix the cause.** For "smallest so far", start from the first real element, `temps[0]`. Do not start from 0, which breaks on all-positive data: for `{5, 7, 9}` nothing is below 0, so you would get 0, a temperature that is not even in the list. And do not start from a guess like 1000, which breaks the day every reading is above it.

```cpp
int lowest = temps[0];   // a value that is really in the list
```

::: context ub-overflow What "undefined" lets the compiler do
Because signed overflow is undefined, the compiler may assume it never happens, and use that to make code faster. For example, it may decide that `x + 1 > x` is always true for an `int x` and delete the check completely, even though with wrap-around it would be false for the largest `int`. So a test you wrote to catch overflow can vanish from the program. That is why the fix is to prevent overflow with a big enough type, not to detect it afterwards. Unsigned types, like `std::size_t` from the last lesson, are different: their wrap-around is defined by the rules, which is why that bug was predictable.
:::

::: context wrap-around Why too big turns negative
An `int` is 32 bits. In the pattern computers use for signed numbers, called two's complement, the counting runs up to 2,147,483,647 and the very next bit pattern means -2,147,483,648, the most negative `int`. So on real machines, stepping past the top usually lands you at the bottom, like the car's mileage counter rolling over. 4,000,000,000 is 4,294,967,296 too much for 32 bits, and 4,000,000,000 − 4,294,967,296 = −294,967,296.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 140" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="180" y="20" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">int values in a row, and the step past the top</text>
  <line x1="30" y1="70" x2="330" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <circle cx="40" cy="70" r="5" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="180" cy="70" r="5" fill="#6c7a93"/>
  <circle cx="320" cy="70" r="5" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="40" y="95" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">−2,147,483,648</text>
  <text x="180" y="95" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="320" y="95" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2,147,483,647</text>
  <path d="M 320 62 C 300 30, 60 30, 40 62" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <path d="M 40 62 L 36 52 L 47 55 Z" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="180" y="42" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">+1 wraps to the bottom</text>
  <text x="180" y="125" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">usual result on real machines; the C++ rules promise nothing</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context boeing-counter A counter that grounded airliners
In 2015 the US Federal Aviation Administration ordered a fix for the Boeing 787. A software counter in the generator control units, which manage the plane's electrical power, would overflow if the units stayed powered on for 248 days without a restart. When that happened, the units could go into a safe mode and the aircraft could lose all its electrical generators, even in flight. The number 248 is no accident: counting hundredths of a second, a signed 32-bit counter runs out after 2,147,483,647 ÷ 100 ÷ 86,400 ≈ 248.6 days. Until the software was fixed, airlines had to switch the power off and on at regular intervals.
:::

::: context literal-types Numbers written in code have types too
A number typed straight into code is called a **literal**, and each one has a type. `0` is an `int`, `0LL` is a `long long`, and `0.0` is a `double`. That matters for `std::accumulate` in both directions. Add up a vector of `double`s with a starting value of `0` and every step is cut back to a whole number: summing `{0.5, 0.5, 0.5}` gives 0, not 1.5. Start from `0.0` and you get 1.5. The rule is the same one: the starting value picks the type of the adding.
:::

::: context leftover-memory Why C++ does not clean up for you
A function's local variables live in a scratch area of memory called the stack, which is reused over and over as functions are called and return. A new variable gets space that some earlier function was using, and whatever that function left there is still sitting in it. Setting every new variable to zero would cost a little time, and C++ was designed not to spend time you did not ask for. Variables declared outside every function are different: they always start at zero. Coding rules for cars and aircraft, such as MISRA, forbid reading any variable before it has been given a value.
:::

::: context warnings-not-enough Warnings help, but do not catch everything
With the same flags, g++ warns that `lowest` in this lesson's starter "may be used uninitialized", but clang++, the compiler this app runs, says nothing about it: the read happens inside a loop, which is harder to analyze. So treat warnings as free help, and still test. NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" rules for safety-critical code ask for every warning to be turned on from the first day, and for the code to compile with no warnings at all.
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** "`total_bytes` of three 1.5 GB files (1,500,000,000 bytes each) comes out as 205,032,704 instead of 4,500,000,000. And `coldest({5, 7, 9})` returns nonsense instead of 5."

Fix both functions at the cause:

- `long long total_bytes(const std::vector<int>& sizes)`: the sum of the sizes, and 0 for an empty list.
- `int coldest(const std::vector<int>& temps)`: the smallest temperature. The list is never empty.

No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

// Total size of all files, in bytes. A single file can be up to 2 GB.
long long total_bytes(const std::vector<int>& sizes) {
    return std::accumulate(sizes.begin(), sizes.end(), 0);
}

// The lowest temperature in the list (never empty).
int coldest(const std::vector<int>& temps) {
    int lowest;
    for (int t : temps) {
        if (t < lowest) lowest = t;
    }
    return lowest;
}
--- solution
#include <numeric>
#include <vector>

// Total size of all files, in bytes. A single file can be up to 2 GB.
long long total_bytes(const std::vector<int>& sizes) {
    return std::accumulate(sizes.begin(), sizes.end(), 0LL);
}

// The lowest temperature in the list (never empty).
int coldest(const std::vector<int>& temps) {
    int lowest = temps[0];
    for (int t : temps) {
        if (t < lowest) lowest = t;
    }
    return lowest;
}
--- hint
What type is the `0` passed to `accumulate`? That is the type the adding happens in.
--- hint
Change the starting value to the `long long` zero, `0LL`. For `coldest`: what value does `lowest` have before the loop?
--- hint
Start `lowest` from something that is really in the list: `int lowest = temps[0];`. The loop stays as it is.
--- check case | Three 1.5 GB files
total_bytes({1500000000, 1500000000, 1500000000})
=> 4500000000LL
--- check case | No files is 0 bytes
total_bytes({})
=> 0LL
--- check case | coldest of all-positive temperatures
coldest({5, 7, 9})
=> 5
--- check case | coldest of all-negative temperatures
coldest({-3, -8, -1})
=> -8
--- check case | coldest of one reading
coldest({42})
=> 42

+++ practice | Areas and squares too big for an int
--- task
Write two functions whose answers can be far bigger than an `int` holds, although every input fits in one. No `main`.

- `long long area_mm2(int width_mm, int height_mm)` returns the area of a rectangle in square millimetres. A solar panel 60,000 mm by 50,000 mm has an area of 3,000,000,000.
- `long long sum_squares(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns the sum of every value times itself. `sum_squares({3, -4})` is 25, and `sum_squares({50000, 50000})` is 5,000,000,000. An empty list gives 0.
--- starter
#include <vector>

long long area_mm2(int width_mm, int height_mm) {
    return width_mm * height_mm;
}

long long sum_squares(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    long long total = 0;
    for (int x : v) total += x * x;
    return total;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

long long area_mm2(int width_mm, int height_mm) {
    return static_cast<long long>(width_mm) * height_mm;
}

long long sum_squares(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    long long total = 0;
    for (int x : v) total += static_cast<long long>(x) * x;
    return total;
}
--- hint
`width_mm * height_mm` multiplies two `int`s, so it is done in an `int`, and overflows, before the result is turned into a `long long` on the way out.
--- hint
Turn one side into a `long long` before multiplying: `static_cast<long long>(width_mm) * height_mm`. The same fix goes inside the loop: `x * x` is `int` arithmetic too.
--- check case | A big solar panel
area_mm2(60000, 50000)
=> 3000000000LL
--- check case | A small plate
area_mm2(12, 30)
=> 360LL
--- check case | Squares of big values
sum_squares({50000, 50000})
=> 5000000000LL
--- check case | Negative values square to positive
sum_squares({3, -4})
=> 25LL
--- check case | Nothing
sum_squares({})
=> 0LL

+++ practice | Add, but only if it fits
--- task
Instead of letting a sum overflow, check first. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `bool fits_in_int(long long x)` returns `true` when `x` is between -2,147,483,648 and 2,147,483,647, both included: the range of an `int`.
- `std::optional<int> checked_add(int a, int b)` returns `a + b` when the answer fits in an `int`, and `std::nullopt` when it does not.

`checked_add(2000000000, 100000000)` is 2,100,000,000, but `checked_add(2000000000, 200000000)` is empty. Do the sum in a `long long`, so that the sum itself never overflows, then check it.
--- starter
#include <optional>

bool fits_in_int(long long x) {
    return true;
}

std::optional<int> checked_add(int a, int b) {
    int sum = a + b;
    if (sum < a) return std::nullopt;
    return sum;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>

bool fits_in_int(long long x) {
    return x >= -2147483648LL && x <= 2147483647LL;
}

std::optional<int> checked_add(int a, int b) {
    long long sum = static_cast<long long>(a) + b;
    if (!fits_in_int(sum)) return std::nullopt;
    return static_cast<int>(sum);
}
--- hint
Checking after an `int` addition is too late: the overflow has already happened, and it is undefined behavior, so the compiler may even remove your check.
--- hint
Add in a `long long`: `static_cast<long long>(a) + b`. Compare it with `-2147483648LL` and `2147483647LL`, and only then turn it back with `static_cast<int>`.
--- check test | fits_in_int at the edges
fits_in_int(2147483647LL) && fits_in_int(-2147483648LL) && !fits_in_int(2147483648LL) && !fits_in_int(-2147483649LL) && fits_in_int(0)
--- check test | Sums that fit
checked_add(2000000000, 100000000) == 2100000000 && checked_add(-5, 3) == -2 && checked_add(2147483647, 0) == 2147483647
--- check test | Sums that do not fit, both ways
checked_add(2000000000, 200000000) == std::nullopt && checked_add(-2000000000, -200000000) == std::nullopt && checked_add(2147483647, 1) == std::nullopt

+++ practice | Smallest, largest and total, maybe
--- task
Write a function that returns three numbers about a list, or nothing for an empty list. The starter declares:

```cpp
struct Stats {
    int min;
    int max;
    long long sum;
};
```

Write `std::optional<Stats> stats_of(const std::vector<int>& v)`. For an empty `v` it returns `std::nullopt`. Otherwise it returns the smallest value, the largest value and the total. No `main`.

`stats_of({7, 2, 9})` gives min 2, max 9 and sum 18. The total of `{2000000000, 2000000000}` is 4,000,000,000. And for `{5, 6}` the min is 5: never start a "smallest so far" at 0.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

struct Stats {
    int min;
    int max;
    long long sum;
};

std::optional<Stats> stats_of(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    Stats s{0, 0, 0};
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < s.min) s.min = x;
        if (x > s.max) s.max = x;
        s.sum += x;
    }
    return s;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

struct Stats {
    int min;
    int max;
    long long sum;
};

std::optional<Stats> stats_of(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return std::nullopt;
    Stats s{v[0], v[0], 0};
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < s.min) s.min = x;
        if (x > s.max) s.max = x;
        s.sum += x;
    }
    return s;
}
--- hint
Deal with the empty list first, then start both the smallest and the largest at `v[0]`, a value that is really in the list.
--- hint
`s.sum` is already a `long long`, so `s.sum += x` adds in the big box. Start it at 0.
--- check test | Three values
[] { auto s = stats_of({7, 2, 9}); return s && s->min == 2 && s->max == 9 && s->sum == 18; }()
--- check test | All positive: the min is not 0
[] { auto s = stats_of({5, 6}); return s && s->min == 5 && s->max == 6; }()
--- check test | All negative: the max is not 0
[] { auto s = stats_of({-5, -6}); return s && s->min == -6 && s->max == -5 && s->sum == -11; }()
--- check test | A total past two billion
[] { auto s = stats_of({2000000000, 2000000000}); return s && s->sum == 4000000000LL; }()
--- check test | Empty
stats_of({}) == std::nullopt

+++ practice | The spread across the whole int range
--- task
Write `long long spread(const std::vector<int>& v)`. It returns the largest value minus the smallest, and 0 for an empty list. No `main`.

`spread({4, -3, 10})` is 13. The inputs are `int`s, but the answer may not fit in one: for the smallest and largest `int`, -2,147,483,648 and 2,147,483,647, the spread is 4,294,967,295.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

long long spread(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    int lo = 0;
    int hi = 0;
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < lo) lo = x;
        if (x > hi) hi = x;
    }
    return hi - lo;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <vector>

long long spread(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return 0;
    int lo = *std::min_element(v.begin(), v.end());
    int hi = *std::max_element(v.begin(), v.end());
    return static_cast<long long>(hi) - lo;
}
--- hint
There are two traps. Starting `lo` and `hi` at 0 is wrong for a list that is all positive or all negative. And `hi - lo` is `int` arithmetic, which overflows for the widest lists.
--- hint
Check `v.empty()` first. Take the real smallest and largest values (`std::min_element` and `std::max_element` from the const correctness lesson), and subtract in a `long long`: `static_cast<long long>(hi) - lo`.
--- check case | A mixed list
spread({4, -3, 10})
=> 13LL
--- check case | All positive
spread({7, 9, 8})
=> 2LL
--- check case | The whole int range
spread({-2147483647 - 1, 0, 2147483647})
=> 4294967295LL
--- check case | One value
spread({-5})
=> 0LL
--- check case | Empty
spread({})
=> 0LL

+++ practice | Debug: the average of big readings
--- task
**Bug report:** "`average({2000000000, 2000000000})` comes out negative instead of 2,000,000,000. And `hottest({-12, -3, -7})` gives 0, but 0 is not even in the list."

`average` returns the mean of the readings, and 0.0 for an empty list. `hottest` returns the largest reading, and is never called with an empty list. Find the cause of each bug and fix it; each fix is one line. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

// The mean of the readings, or 0.0 for none.
double average(const std::vector<int>& readings) {
    if (readings.empty()) return 0.0;
    int sum = 0;
    for (int r : readings) sum += r;
    return static_cast<double>(sum) / readings.size();
}

// The largest reading (never called with an empty list).
int hottest(const std::vector<int>& readings) {
    int best = 0;
    for (int r : readings) {
        if (r > best) best = r;
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

// The mean of the readings, or 0.0 for none.
double average(const std::vector<int>& readings) {
    if (readings.empty()) return 0.0;
    long long sum = 0;
    for (int r : readings) sum += r;
    return static_cast<double>(sum) / readings.size();
}

// The largest reading (never called with an empty list).
int hottest(const std::vector<int>& readings) {
    int best = readings[0];
    for (int r : readings) {
        if (r > best) best = r;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Test the edges: the total of two big readings, and a list where every reading is below the starting value.
--- hint
The sum needs a bigger box, `long long sum = 0;`. And the largest so far should start from a real reading, `readings[0]`.
--- check test | The mean of two big readings
std::abs(average({2000000000, 2000000000}) - 2000000000.0) < 1e-3
--- check test | A small mean, and no readings
std::abs(average({1, 2}) - 1.5) < 1e-9 && average({}) == 0.0
--- check case | All negative
hottest({-12, -3, -7})
=> -3
--- check case | One reading
hottest({42})
=> 42

+++ practice | Stretch: the best stretch of the flight
--- task
A probe records its change in altitude every minute, which can be up or down. Write `long long best_stretch(const std::vector<int>& v)`: the biggest total of a run of **one or more** values next to each other. No `main`.

- `best_stretch({-2, 5, -1, 6, -8, 3})` is 10, from 5, -1 and 6.
- If every value is negative, the best run is the single biggest value: `best_stretch({-4, -2, -9})` is -2.
- Totals can pass 2 billion, so add in a `long long`. An empty list gives 0.

A strong answer walks the list once. Keep the best total of a run that ends at the current value: it is either the current value alone, or the current value added to the best run that ended just before it.
--- starter
#include <vector>

long long best_stretch(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    long long best = 0;
    long long current = 0;
    for (int x : v) {
        current += x;
        if (current < 0) current = 0;
        if (current > best) best = current;
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

long long best_stretch(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return 0;
    long long best = v[0];
    long long current = 0;   // the best total of a run ending at x
    for (int x : v) {
        if (current < 0) {
            current = x;
        } else {
            current += x;
        }
        if (current > best) best = current;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
The starter resets to 0 and starts the best at 0, so it can never give a negative answer. Start the best from a real value, `v[0]`, after checking for an empty list.
--- hint
For each value, the best run ending there is the value alone when the run before it had a negative total, and the run before plus the value otherwise.
--- check case | The best run in the middle
best_stretch({-2, 5, -1, 6, -8, 3})
=> 10LL
--- check case | All negative
best_stretch({-4, -2, -9})
=> -2LL
--- check case | The whole list
best_stretch({2, 3, 4})
=> 9LL
--- check case | Past two billion
best_stretch({2000000000, -1, 2000000000})
=> 3999999999LL
--- check case | Empty
best_stretch({})
=> 0LL

=== cpp2-14 | Problem solving: two that add up
--- teach
In the last lesson, "Debugging: overflow and uninitialised values", you were handed a broken program and a bug report. This lesson hands you only a problem. There is no code yet: you design the **[[algorithm|algorithm-word]]**, the step-by-step method, yourself.

### A reliable way in

Engineers attack a new problem in four steps:

1. **Restate** the problem in your own words and pin down exactly what the answer looks like.
2. **Work examples** by hand, including the awkward ones: the same value twice, negative numbers, no answer at all.
3. **Brute force first.** What is the obvious solution, and how slow is it?
4. **Find the pattern** that removes the wasted work.

### Step 1: restate it

Given a list of numbers and a target, find two **different positions** whose values add up to the target.

For `{2, 7, 11, 15}` and target 9, the answer is positions 0 and 1, because 2 + 7 = 9. Notice the answer is the positions, not the values.

### Step 2: work some examples

- `{3, 3}` with target 6: positions 0 and 1. The same value may appear twice.
- `{3, 2, 4}` with target 6: positions 1 and 2 (2 + 4). Not 0 and 0: 3 + 3 is 6, but that uses one position twice.
- `{1, 2, 3}` with target 100: no answer.

### Step 3: brute force

**Brute force** means trying every possibility. Here that is every pair of positions, with two loops, one inside the other:

```cpp
for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)
    for (int j = i + 1; j < n; ++j)
        if (nums[i] + nums[j] == target) { /* found */ }
```

Starting `j` at `i + 1` means each pair is tried once and never a position with itself. That is about n²/2 pairs (n² is read "n squared", n times n), so [[the work grows very fast|pairs-grow]]. For 10 numbers it is nothing. For 200,000 numbers it is 20 **billion** pairs, far too slow.

### Step 4: find the pattern

Walk through the numbers once, from left to right. When you are standing on value `x` at position `j`, you need some **earlier** value equal to `target - x`. That missing partner is called the **complement**.

So the question at each step is: "have I already seen `target - x`, and where?" Keep a notebook of every earlier value and the position where you first saw it. Then the question is one lookup. In plan form:

```
seen = {}                         value -> first position it appeared
for j, x in numbers:
    if (target - x) in seen: answer is (seen[target - x], j)
    if x not in seen: seen[x] = j
```

This is a plan, not C++ yet. `for j, x in numbers` means "for each position `j` and the value `x` sitting there", and `seen` is the notebook.

Trace `{2, 7, 11, 15}` with target 9. At position 0, `x` is 2 and you need 7: the notebook is empty, so write down "2 is at 0". At position 1, `x` is 7 and you need 2: the notebook says 0. The answer is `{0, 1}`.

Two small choices do a lot of work:

- **Look before you write.** Checking the notebook *before* adding `x` stops a number pairing with itself. In `{3, 2, 4}` with target 6, if you wrote "3 is at 0" first and then looked for 3, you would find position 0 and answer `{0, 0}`.
- **Keep only the first position.** Keeping the first place each value appeared gives the smallest `i` for that `j`. And because `j` walks upward and you stop at the first match, the answer you return has the smallest `j` too.

This walks the list once, so the time grows in step with the length of the list. In the [[notation programmers use|big-o]] that is O(n), against O(n²) for brute force. 200,000 numbers take a blink.

### The C++ tools

The notebook is a `std::unordered_map<int, int>`: value to first position. From the maps lesson you know a [[lookup in it is fast|hash-lookup]] on average, and that `find` or `contains` looks without inserting:

```cpp
std::unordered_map<int, int> first_seen;
auto it = first_seen.find(target - x);
if (it != first_seen.end()) {
    // found: it->second is the position
}
```

`find` gives back an iterator, which equals `end()` (read `!=` as "is not equal to") when the key is missing. The arrow `->` means "follow the iterator, then take the member", so `it->second` is the stored value, here the position.

To write in the notebook, `m.emplace(key, value)` inserts only when the key is missing. It **never overwrites**, so the first position stays:

```cpp
first_seen.emplace(2, 0);   // 2 is at 0
first_seen.emplace(2, 5);   // ignored: 2 is already there
```

**Watch out:** `first_seen[x] = j` would overwrite, replacing the first position with the latest one. That breaks the "smallest `i`" rule.

### Returning two numbers

The answer is two numbers, so the function returns a **`std::pair<int, int>`** (from the `<utility>` header): [[two values carried as one|pair-type]]. `return {i, j};` builds one, and `.first` and `.second` read it back:

```cpp
std::pair<int, int> p = {0, 1};
std::cout << p.first << " " << p.second << "\n";   // prints 0 1
```

One last detail: positions go into a pair of `int`s, so loop with an `int j` and compare it with `static_cast<int>(nums.size())`. Comparing an `int` straight with `nums.size()`, which is unsigned, earns a warning with this checker's flags.

::: context algorithm-word A word from a Baghdad mathematician
"Algorithm" comes from the name of Muhammad ibn Musa al-Khwarizmi, a mathematician who worked in Baghdad in the 800s. His book on calculating with the Hindu-Arabic numerals, the digits 0 to 9 we use today, was translated into Latin, and his name, written Algoritmi, became the word for a step-by-step method of calculating. The word "algebra" comes from the title of another of his books.
:::

::: context pairs-grow Counting the pairs
Draw the list along the top of a grid and again down the side. Each square is one pair of positions. The squares on the diagonal pair a position with itself, and the squares below it repeat pairs from above, so only the squares above the diagonal are real pairs. For n numbers that is n × (n − 1) ÷ 2. For 5 numbers it is 10. For 200,000 numbers it is 19,999,900,000, about 20 billion. Double the list and the work goes up four times.

```svg

  <text x="18" y="115" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle" transform="rotate(-90 18 115)">i = 0 to 4</text>
  <g stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1">

  <text x="238" y="63" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">10 real pairs</text>

  <text x="238" y="98" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">with itself: skip</text>
  <rect x="215" y="120" width="16" height="16" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="238" y="133" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">repeats: skip</text>
  <text x="215" y="170" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">5 × 4 ÷ 2 = 10</text>
```

:::

::: context big-o Big-O: how the work grows
Programmers describe speed by how the work grows as the input grows, written with a capital O, read "order of". O(n) means the work grows in step with n: twice the numbers, about twice the time. O(n²) means it grows with n squared: twice the numbers, about four times the time. O(1) means the time does not grow with n at all. The notation ignores small constant factors, like the ÷ 2 in n²/2, because the shape of the growth is what decides whether 200,000 items take a millisecond or an hour.
:::

::: context hash-lookup How a hash map finds a key so fast
An `unordered_map` is a **hash table**. It keeps many small buckets, and a function called a hash turns each key into a bucket number. To look up a key, it hashes the key and checks only that one bucket, instead of searching everything. Two keys can land in the same bucket, so a bucket sometimes holds a few entries; that is why the speed is O(1) "on average" rather than always. The table grows and spreads its keys over more buckets as you add them, to keep the buckets short.
:::

::: context pair-type A pair is a tiny struct
`std::pair<int, int>` is a ready-made struct with two members, named `first` and `second`. The two types can differ: `std::pair<std::string, int>` holds a name and a number. You have used pairs already without seeing the name: every element of a `std::map` or `std::unordered_map` is a pair, which is why `it->first` is the key and `it->second` the value. When the two parts have real meanings, like a low and a high reading, a struct with named members reads better; the design lesson next shows that.
:::
--- task
Write `std::pair<int, int> two_sum(const std::vector<int>& nums, int target)`. It returns positions `{i, j}` with `i < j` and `nums[i] + nums[j] == target`.

- If there are several answers, return the one with the **smallest `j`**, and for that `j` the smallest `i`.
- If there is no answer, return `{-1, -1}`.

The last check has 200,002 numbers, so it needs the one-pass idea with a `std::unordered_map`, not brute force. No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::pair<int, int> two_sum(const std::vector<int>& nums, int target) {
    return {-1, -1};
}
--- solution
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::pair<int, int> two_sum(const std::vector<int>& nums, int target) {
    std::unordered_map<int, int> first_seen;
    for (int j = 0; j < static_cast<int>(nums.size()); ++j) {
        auto it = first_seen.find(target - nums[j]);
        if (it != first_seen.end()) return {it->second, j};
        first_seen.emplace(nums[j], j);
    }
    return {-1, -1};
}
--- hint
For each position `j`, the question is "have I already seen `target - nums[j]`, and where?" Make a `std::unordered_map<int, int> first_seen;` to remember value to position.
--- hint
Look up before you insert, so a number never pairs with itself: `first_seen.find(target - nums[j])`, and if that is not `first_seen.end()`, return `{it->second, j}`. `emplace` does not overwrite an existing key, so the first position of each value is kept.
--- hint
The loop body is three lines: `auto it = first_seen.find(target - nums[j]);`, then `if (it != first_seen.end()) return {it->second, j};`, then `first_seen.emplace(nums[j], j);`. After the loop, `return {-1, -1};`.
--- check case | The classic example
two_sum({2, 7, 11, 15}, 9)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 1}
--- check case | Not the first element
two_sum({3, 2, 4}, 6)
=> std::pair<int, int>{1, 2}
--- check case | The same value twice
two_sum({3, 3}, 6)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 1}
--- check case | Smallest j wins, then smallest i
two_sum({2, 2, 2, 5, 3}, 4)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 1}
--- check case | Negative numbers
two_sum({-4, 10, 7, 1}, -3)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 3}
--- check case | No answer
two_sum({1, 2, 3}, 100)
=> std::pair<int, int>{-1, -1}
--- check case | Empty input
two_sum({}, 0)
=> std::pair<int, int>{-1, -1}
--- check test | 200,002 numbers, answer at the very end
[] { std::vector<int> v; for (int i = 0; i < 200000; ++i) v.push_back(10 + i * 10); v.push_back(1); v.push_back(2); return two_sum(v, 3) == std::pair<int, int>{200000, 200001}; }()

+++ practice | A notebook of first positions
--- task
Write two functions that keep a notebook of where things were first seen. No `main`.

- `std::unordered_map<std::string, int> first_positions(const std::vector<std::string>& words)` maps each word to the index where it **first** appears. For `{"go", "no", "go"}`, `"go"` maps to 0 and `"no"` to 1.
- `int first_repeat_index(const std::vector<int>& v)` returns the index of the first value that already appeared earlier in the list, or `-1` if no value repeats. For `{4, 7, 1, 7, 4}`, the answer is 3: the 7 at index 3 was already seen at index 1.

Walk each list once.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

std::unordered_map<std::string, int> first_positions(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    std::unordered_map<std::string, int> seen;
    for (int i = 0; i < static_cast<int>(words.size()); ++i) seen[words[i]] = i;
    return seen;
}

int first_repeat_index(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

std::unordered_map<std::string, int> first_positions(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    std::unordered_map<std::string, int> seen;
    for (int i = 0; i < static_cast<int>(words.size()); ++i) seen.emplace(words[i], i);
    return seen;
}

int first_repeat_index(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::unordered_map<int, int> seen;
    for (int i = 0; i < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++i) {
        if (seen.contains(v[i])) return i;
        seen.emplace(v[i], i);
    }
    return -1;
}
--- hint
`seen[word] = i` overwrites, so it keeps the **last** position. `emplace` only inserts a key that is missing, so the first position stays.
--- hint
For `first_repeat_index`, look before you write: if `v[i]` is already in the notebook, `i` is the answer. Otherwise write it down.
--- check test | First positions are kept
[] { auto m = first_positions({"go", "no", "go", "no", "stay"}); return m.size() == 3 && m["go"] == 0 && m["no"] == 1 && m["stay"] == 4; }()
--- check test | No words, an empty notebook
first_positions({}).empty()
--- check case | The first repeat
first_repeat_index({4, 7, 1, 7, 4})
=> 3
--- check case | A repeat right away
first_repeat_index({9, 9})
=> 1
--- check case | No repeats
first_repeat_index({1, 2, 3})
=> -1
--- check case | Empty
first_repeat_index({})
=> -1

+++ practice | Count every pair that adds up
--- task
Write `long long count_pairs(const std::vector<int>& v, int target)`. It returns how many pairs of positions `i < j` have `v[i] + v[j] == target`. No `main`.

- `count_pairs({1, 5, 3, 3, 5}, 6)` is 3: the pairs of positions (0, 1), (0, 4) and (2, 3). The two 5s do not pair, because 5 + 5 is 10.
- `count_pairs({2, 2, 2}, 4)` is 3: every pair of positions works.

The last check has 300,000 numbers, so walk the list once. Keep a count of how many times each value has appeared so far; when you reach `x`, every earlier `target - x` makes a pair with it.
--- starter
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

long long count_pairs(const std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

long long count_pairs(const std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    std::unordered_map<int, long long> seen;   // value -> how many times so far
    long long pairs = 0;
    for (int x : v) {
        auto it = seen.find(target - x);
        if (it != seen.end()) pairs += it->second;
        seen[x]++;
    }
    return pairs;
}
--- hint
Trying every `i` with every `j` works on small lists, but 300,000 numbers make about 45 billion pairs: far too slow.
--- hint
Walk once with a `std::unordered_map<int, long long>` of counts. For each `x`, first add the count stored for `target - x` (if there is one), then add one to the count of `x`. Looking before counting stops `x` pairing with itself.
--- check case | Three pairs
count_pairs({1, 5, 3, 3, 5}, 6)
=> 3LL
--- check case | Every pair of equal values
count_pairs({2, 2, 2}, 4)
=> 3LL
--- check case | A value never pairs with itself
count_pairs({3}, 6)
=> 0LL
--- check case | Negative numbers
count_pairs({-2, 8, 4, -3, 9, 2}, 6)
=> 3LL
--- check case | 300,000 equal numbers
[] { std::vector<int> v(300000, 1); return count_pairs(v, 2); }()
=> 44999850000LL

+++ practice | Two crew members for the capsule
--- task
A capsule seat takes exactly two crew members whose masses add up to a target. A `Crew` member has a name and a mass in kilograms:

```cpp
struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int kg;
};
```

Write `std::pair<std::string, std::string> matching_pair(const std::vector<Crew>& crew, int total)`. It returns the names of two different crew members, earlier one first, whose masses add up to `total`. If there are several answers, pick the one where the later member comes as early as possible, and then the earlier member as early as possible, just like `two_sum`. If there is none, return `{"", ""}`. No `main`.

For `{{"Mae", 70}, {"Yuri", 82}, {"Kay", 69}, {"Al", 80}}` and 150, the answer is `{"Mae", "Al"}`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int kg;
};

std::pair<std::string, std::string> matching_pair(const std::vector<Crew>& crew, int total) {
    return {"", ""};
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

struct Crew {
    std::string name;
    int kg;
};

std::pair<std::string, std::string> matching_pair(const std::vector<Crew>& crew, int total) {
    std::unordered_map<int, int> first_seen;   // mass -> first position
    for (int j = 0; j < static_cast<int>(crew.size()); ++j) {
        auto it = first_seen.find(total - crew[j].kg);
        if (it != first_seen.end()) return {crew[it->second].name, crew[j].name};
        first_seen.emplace(crew[j].kg, j);
    }
    return {"", ""};
}
--- hint
It is `two_sum` on the masses. Keep a notebook from mass to the first position where it appeared.
--- hint
When the partner mass is found, turn the two positions back into names: `crew[it->second].name` and `crew[j].name`.
--- check case | The first match by the later member
matching_pair({{"Mae", 70}, {"Yuri", 82}, {"Kay", 69}, {"Al", 80}}, 150)
=> std::pair<std::string, std::string>{"Mae", "Al"}
--- check case | Two members of the same mass
matching_pair({{"Bo", 75}, {"Cy", 90}, {"Di", 75}}, 150)
=> std::pair<std::string, std::string>{"Bo", "Di"}
--- check case | One member cannot sit twice
matching_pair({{"Bo", 75}}, 150)
=> std::pair<std::string, std::string>{"", ""}
--- check case | No match
matching_pair({{"Bo", 60}, {"Cy", 70}}, 150)
=> std::pair<std::string, std::string>{"", ""}

+++ practice | Two values a set distance apart
--- task
Write `std::pair<int, int> pair_with_difference(const std::vector<int>& v, int k)`. It returns positions `{i, j}` with `i < j` whose values differ by exactly `k`, in either direction: `v[j] - v[i]` is `k` or `-k`. No `main`.

- Among all answers, pick the smallest `j`, and for that `j` the smallest `i`.
- A negative `k` means the same as the positive one.
- If there is no answer, return `{-1, -1}`.

For `{5, 1, 9, 3}` and 2: position 3 (the 3) is 2 away from both the 1 at position 1 and the 5 at position 0, so the answer is `{0, 3}`. For `{4, 7, 4}` and 0, the answer is `{0, 2}`.
--- starter
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::pair<int, int> pair_with_difference(const std::vector<int>& v, int k) {
    std::unordered_map<int, int> first_seen;
    for (int j = 0; j < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++j) {
        auto it = first_seen.find(v[j] - k);
        if (it != first_seen.end()) return {it->second, j};
        first_seen.emplace(v[j], j);
    }
    return {-1, -1};
}
--- solution
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::pair<int, int> pair_with_difference(const std::vector<int>& v, int k) {
    if (k < 0) k = -k;
    std::unordered_map<int, int> first_seen;
    for (int j = 0; j < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++j) {
        int best = -1;
        auto below = first_seen.find(v[j] - k);
        if (below != first_seen.end()) best = below->second;
        auto above = first_seen.find(v[j] + k);
        if (above != first_seen.end() && (best == -1 || above->second < best)) best = above->second;
        if (best != -1) return {best, j};
        first_seen.emplace(v[j], j);
    }
    return {-1, -1};
}
--- hint
The partner of `v[j]` can be `v[j] - k` or `v[j] + k`. Look up both, and when both are there, take the smaller position.
--- hint
Turn a negative `k` into a positive one first. With `k` of 0 both lookups are the same value, which is fine.
--- check case | Both partners are there: the smaller i wins
pair_with_difference({5, 1, 9, 3}, 2)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 3}
--- check case | A difference of 0 needs a repeat
pair_with_difference({4, 7, 4}, 0)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 2}
--- check case | A negative k
pair_with_difference({10, 4, 7}, -3)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 2}
--- check case | Negative values
pair_with_difference({-6, 0, -1}, 5)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 2}
--- check case | No answer
pair_with_difference({1, 2, 3}, 10)
=> std::pair<int, int>{-1, -1}
--- check case | Empty
pair_with_difference({}, 1)
=> std::pair<int, int>{-1, -1}

+++ practice | Debug: a number that paired with itself
--- task
**Bug report:** "`has_pair_with_sum({3, 1}, 6)` says `true`, but no two different numbers add up to 6. It seems to use the 3 twice."

`has_pair_with_sum` should return `true` only when two values at **different** positions add up to `target`. Find the cause and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <set>
#include <vector>

// True when two values at different positions add up to target.
bool has_pair_with_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    std::set<int> seen;
    for (int x : v) {
        seen.insert(x);
        if (seen.contains(target - x)) return true;
    }
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <set>
#include <vector>

// True when two values at different positions add up to target.
bool has_pair_with_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    std::set<int> seen;
    for (int x : v) {
        if (seen.contains(target - x)) return true;
        seen.insert(x);
    }
    return false;
}
--- hint
Trace `{3, 1}` with target 6. When `x` is 3, what is already in `seen` at the moment you look for `6 - 3`?
--- hint
Look before you write: check for `target - x` first, then insert `x`. Swap the two lines.
--- check test | A value cannot pair with itself
!has_pair_with_sum({3, 1}, 6) && !has_pair_with_sum({3}, 6)
--- check test | Two equal values at different positions can
has_pair_with_sum({3, 3}, 6) && has_pair_with_sum({1, 4, 3}, 5)
--- check test | No pair, and nothing at all
!has_pair_with_sum({1, 2}, 10) && !has_pair_with_sum({}, 0)

+++ practice | Stretch: a run of readings with a given total
--- task
Write `std::pair<int, int> run_with_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, long long target)`. It finds a run of **one or more** values next to each other whose total is `target`, and returns the first and last position of the run. No `main`.

- Choose the run that **ends** earliest. If several runs end at that same position, choose the longest, the one that starts earliest.
- Values can be negative. If there is no such run, return `{-1, -1}`.

`run_with_sum({3, 4, -2, 5}, 7)` is `{0, 1}`, and `run_with_sum({1, -1, 1}, 1)` is `{0, 0}`.

The last check has 200,000 values, so trying every start and end is too slow. Think in **prefix sums**: `prefix(p)` is the total of the first `p` values, so `prefix(0)` is 0. The run from `i` to `j` totals `prefix(j + 1) - prefix(i)`. So at each `j` the question is the one from the lesson: "have I already seen the prefix `prefix(j + 1) - target`, and where first?" The empty prefix, 0, is seen at position 0 before you start.
--- starter
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::pair<int, int> run_with_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, long long target) {
    return {-1, -1};
}
--- solution
#include <unordered_map>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

std::pair<int, int> run_with_sum(const std::vector<int>& v, long long target) {
    std::unordered_map<long long, int> first_seen;   // prefix total -> first position
    first_seen.emplace(0, 0);
    long long prefix = 0;
    for (int j = 0; j < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++j) {
        prefix += v[j];
        auto it = first_seen.find(prefix - target);
        if (it != first_seen.end()) return {it->second, j};
        first_seen.emplace(prefix, j + 1);
    }
    return {-1, -1};
}
--- hint
Walk `j` once, keeping a running `long long prefix`. Before the loop, write "prefix 0 was seen at position 0" in an `unordered_map<long long, int>`.
--- hint
At each `j`, add `v[j]` to `prefix`, look up `prefix - target`, and return `{it->second, j}` if it is there. Then `emplace(prefix, j + 1)`: `emplace` keeps the first position, which gives the longest run.
--- check case | The run that ends first
run_with_sum({3, 4, -2, 5}, 7)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 1}
--- check case | Ends first beats starts first
run_with_sum({5, 1, 2, 3}, 3)
=> std::pair<int, int>{1, 2}
--- check case | A single value
run_with_sum({1, -1, 1}, 1)
=> std::pair<int, int>{0, 0}
--- check case | A total of 0 with negatives
run_with_sum({2, 5, -5, 1}, 0)
=> std::pair<int, int>{1, 2}
--- check case | No run
run_with_sum({1, 2}, 10)
=> std::pair<int, int>{-1, -1}
--- check case | 200,000 values with no answer
[] { std::vector<int> v(200000, 2); return run_with_sum(v, 1); }()
=> std::pair<int, int>{-1, -1}

=== cpp2-15 | Design: give repeated work a name
--- teach
Last lesson, "Problem solving: two that add up", you designed an algorithm from a blank page. This lesson starts from code that already works, mostly, and gives it a better shape.

### Why copies go wrong

Imagine a checklist copied onto three clipboards. Someone finds a mistake and fixes it on one clipboard, and forgets the other two. Now the three disagree, and nobody knows which is right.

Copy-pasted code is the same: a [[bug waiting to happen|dry]]. Three blocks that "do the same thing" drift apart the first time someone edits one and forgets the others. And the one they forgot is usually the wrong one.

**Refactoring** means [[changing the shape of code|refactoring-word]] without changing what it does. For duplicated blocks, work in four steps.

### Step 1: find what varies

Line the copies up side by side and spot the differences:

```cpp
out << "morning: min " << lo << "\n";   // copy 1
out << "noon: min " << lo << "\n";      // copy 2
```

Here only the data and a label differ. Those differences become the function's **parameters**; everything the copies share becomes its body.

### Step 2: name the idea

A function name should say **what** it computes, not **how**: `summarize`, not `loop_and_min_max`. A reader should be able to guess what `summarize(readings)` gives back without opening it.

### Step 3: return a meaningful type

When a function produces several related values, a small `struct` with named members is the clearest thing to return:

```cpp
struct Range { int low; int high; };
```

It beats the alternatives. Three [[output parameters|out-params]] (references the function writes its answers into) make every call longer and harder to read. A `std::pair` of a `std::pair` makes the reader decode `r.second.first`. A struct lets them write `r.low`.

### Step 4: replace each copy with a call

Swap every copy for a call to the new function, then re-run the checks. They are your [[safety net|regression]]: behavior must not change, except for fixing bugs the duplication was hiding.

### One place to get it right

Here is a whole function that returns a `Range`:

```cpp
Range range_of(const std::vector<int>& v) {        // one place to get it right
    auto [lo, hi] = std::minmax_element(v.begin(), v.end());
    return {*lo, *hi};
}
```

Read it slowly, because two pieces are new:

- `std::minmax_element`, from `<algorithm>`, finds the smallest and the largest in one walk through the range. Like `max_element` in the iterators lesson, it gives back iterators, not values: a pair of them, the first pointing at the smallest element and the second at the largest.
- `auto [lo, hi] = ...` is a **[[structured binding|structured-binding]]**: it unpacks a pair into two new variables with names you choose. Here `lo` is the pair's first part and `hi` its second.

Then `*lo` follows the iterator to the value, and `return {*lo, *hi};` fills the `Range` in member order: `low` first, then `high`. For `{4, -1, 9, 3}` you get low -1 and high 9.

**Watch out:** on an empty vector there is no smallest element, and both iterators are `end()`. Following `end()` with `*` is undefined behavior. So a function like this only works on a non-empty list, and the code that calls it must check `empty()` first.

### Computing apart from presenting

A good split has one function for **computing** and one for **presenting**. The computing one returns numbers:

```cpp
Summary s = summarize(readings);   // numbers: min, max, mean
```

The presenting one turns them into text. It uses a `std::ostringstream` from the string streams lesson, set up with `std::fixed << std::setprecision(1)` so the mean always shows one decimal place:

```cpp
std::string line = format_line("noon", readings);   // "noon: min 10, max 20, mean 15.0\n"
```

You can then test each one on its own, and [[reuse the numbers|compute-present]] somewhere that does not want text at all.

::: context dry Don't Repeat Yourself
Programmers have a name for this rule: **DRY**, short for "Don't Repeat Yourself". It comes from the 1999 book *The Pragmatic Programmer* by Andy Hunt and Dave Thomas, who put it as: every piece of knowledge should have one single, clear home in a system. When a fact lives in two places, sooner or later someone changes one and not the other. DRY is about knowledge, not about lines that happen to look alike: two blocks that look the same today but mean different things can stay separate.
:::

::: context refactoring-word Where "refactoring" comes from
The word borrows from algebra. Factoring rewrites 6x + 6y as 6(x + y): a different shape, the same value. Refactoring does the same to code. Martin Fowler's 1999 book *Refactoring* made the word famous, with a catalog of small, safe steps. One of them, pulling repeated code out into its own function, is exactly what this lesson does. Each step is small enough to check before taking the next, so the program works after every one.
:::

::: context out-params Answers through references
An **output parameter** is a reference parameter that the function writes a result into, the way `capitalize_all` changed the caller's vector in the iterators lesson. `void range_of(const std::vector<int>& v, int& low, int& high)` works, but every caller must first make empty variables to receive the answers, and a reader of a call like `range_of(v, a, b)` cannot tell which arguments go in and which come out. Returning a struct makes the answer obvious, and the compiler usually builds the returned struct straight in the caller's space, so it costs nothing extra.
:::

::: context regression Tests as a safety net
A bug that creeps back into code that used to work is called a **regression**, and checks that you re-run after every change are regression tests. They are what make refactoring safe: change the shape, run the checks, and if any check fails you know that step broke something, while it is still small and fresh. The checks on this page are regression tests for `report`. Flight software teams re-run huge suites of them automatically on every change.
:::

::: context structured-binding Unpacking a pair
Structured bindings arrived in C++17. They work on a `std::pair`, on a struct, and on the elements of a map: `for (const auto& [name, score] : scores)` walks a map and gives each key and value its own name, instead of `.first` and `.second`. The names are yours to choose, and the order follows the parts: first, then second.
:::

::: context compute-present Numbers on board, text on the ground
Spacecraft work this way. The flight computer computes readings and sends them down as compact numbers, called telemetry. Software on the ground turns those numbers into displays, plots and alarm messages. The same numbers can feed a chart, a log file and a warning light, and changing how a screen looks never touches the flight code.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="40" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="50" y="64" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">readings</text>
  <rect x="120" y="40" width="100" height="40" rx="6" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="170" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#ffffff" text-anchor="middle">summarize</text>
  <text x="170" y="73" font-size="11" fill="#ffffff" text-anchor="middle">computes</text>
  <rect x="250" y="40" width="100" height="40" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="300" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">format_line</text>
  <text x="300" y="73" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">presents</text>
  <line x1="90" y1="60" x2="116" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <path d="M 120 60 L 112 56 L 112 64 Z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="220" y1="60" x2="246" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <path d="M 250 60 L 242 56 L 242 64 Z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="235" y="30" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">Summary</text>
  <text x="170" y="105" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">testable alone</text>
  <text x="300" y="105" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">text only here</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
`report` works, mostly, but its three copied blocks hide a copy-paste bug. Refactor it into these pieces:

- `struct Summary { int min; int max; double mean; };`
- `Summary summarize(const std::vector<int>& readings)`: the smallest value, the largest value and the mean (the average: the total divided by how many there are). It is only called with a non-empty list.
- `std::string format_line(const std::string& label, const std::vector<int>& readings)`: returns `"label: min 1, max 5, mean 3.0\n"`, with the real label and numbers and the mean to one decimal place. For an empty list it returns `"label: no readings\n"` instead.
- `report` returns the three lines, morning then noon then night, each built with `format_line`.

Keep at most one loop in the whole file. No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string report(const std::vector<int>& morning, const std::vector<int>& noon, const std::vector<int>& night) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1);

    int lo = morning[0], hi = morning[0];
    long long sum = 0;
    for (int x : morning) { if (x < lo) lo = x; if (x > hi) hi = x; sum += x; }
    out << "morning: min " << lo << ", max " << hi << ", mean " << static_cast<double>(sum) / morning.size() << "\n";

    lo = noon[0]; hi = noon[0];
    sum = 0;
    for (int x : noon) { if (x < lo) lo = x; if (x > hi) hi = x; sum += x; }
    out << "noon: min " << lo << ", max " << hi << ", mean " << static_cast<double>(sum) / noon.size() << "\n";

    lo = night[0]; hi = night[0];
