    sum = 0;
    for (int x : night) { if (x < lo) lo = x; if (x > hi) hi = x; sum += x; }
    out << "night: min " << lo << ", max " << hi << ", mean " << static_cast<double>(sum) / noon.size() << "\n";

    return out.str();
}
--- solution
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Summary {
    int min;
    int max;
    double mean;
};

Summary summarize(const std::vector<int>& readings) {
    Summary s{readings[0], readings[0], 0.0};
    long long sum = 0;
    for (int x : readings) {
        if (x < s.min) s.min = x;
        if (x > s.max) s.max = x;
        sum += x;
    }
    s.mean = static_cast<double>(sum) / readings.size();
    return s;
}

std::string format_line(const std::string& label, const std::vector<int>& readings) {
    if (readings.empty()) return label + ": no readings\n";
    Summary s = summarize(readings);
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1);
    out << label << ": min " << s.min << ", max " << s.max << ", mean " << s.mean << "\n";
    return out.str();
}

std::string report(const std::vector<int>& morning, const std::vector<int>& noon, const std::vector<int>& night) {
    return format_line("morning", morning) + format_line("noon", noon) + format_line("night", night);
}
--- hint
Compare the three blocks line by line. What is different in the last one, and is it meant to be?
--- hint
Move one block into `summarize`: start a `Summary s{readings[0], readings[0], 0.0};`, keep the one loop, and set `s.mean` from the `long long` sum at the end. Then `format_line` checks `readings.empty()` first, calls `summarize`, and writes the text with an `std::ostringstream` set to `std::fixed << std::setprecision(1)`.
--- hint
`report` becomes a single line: three `format_line` calls joined with `+`, as in `format_line("morning", morning) + format_line("noon", noon) + format_line("night", night)`.
--- check case | report gives the three lines, with the night mean right
report({1, 5, 3}, {10, 20}, {-2, 4, 1, 1})
=> "morning: min 1, max 5, mean 3.0\nnoon: min 10, max 20, mean 15.0\nnight: min -2, max 4, mean 1.0\n"
--- check test | summarize returns the numbers
[] { Summary s = summarize({4, -1, 9}); return s.min == -1 && s.max == 9 && std::abs(s.mean - 4.0) < 1e-9; }()
--- check case | format_line with no readings
format_line("noon", {})
=> "noon: no readings\n"
--- check case | report copes with an empty period
report({2}, {}, {7, 8})
=> "morning: min 2, max 2, mean 2.0\nnoon: no readings\nnight: min 7, max 8, mean 7.5\n"
--- check source | report uses format_line
format_line\(\s*"morning"
--- check source absent | At most one loop in the file
\b(for|while)\s*\([\s\S]*\b(for|while)\s*\(

+++ practice | Word statistics, computed and presented
--- task
Split the work into a function that computes and one that presents. The starter declares:

```cpp
struct WordStats {
    int shortest;
    int longest;
    int letters;
};
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `WordStats word_stats(const std::vector<std::string>& words)` returns the length of the shortest word, the length of the longest, and the total number of characters in all the words. For no words, all three are 0.
- `std::string word_report(const std::vector<std::string>& words)` calls `word_stats` and returns a line like `"3 words, 2 to 7 letters, 12 in all"`. For no words it returns `"no words"`.

For `{"go", "orbit", "apogee"}` the stats are 2, 6 and 13, and the report is `"3 words, 2 to 6 letters, 13 in all"`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct WordStats {
    int shortest;
    int longest;
    int letters;
};

WordStats word_stats(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    return {0, 0, 0};
}

std::string word_report(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    return "no words";
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct WordStats {
    int shortest;
    int longest;
    int letters;
};

WordStats word_stats(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    if (words.empty()) return {0, 0, 0};
    int first = static_cast<int>(words[0].size());
    WordStats s{first, first, 0};
    for (const auto& w : words) {
        int n = static_cast<int>(w.size());
        if (n < s.shortest) s.shortest = n;
        if (n > s.longest) s.longest = n;
        s.letters += n;
    }
    return s;
}

std::string word_report(const std::vector<std::string>& words) {
    if (words.empty()) return "no words";
    WordStats s = word_stats(words);
    return std::to_string(words.size()) + " words, " + std::to_string(s.shortest) + " to " +
           std::to_string(s.longest) + " letters, " + std::to_string(s.letters) + " in all";
}
--- hint
`word_stats` only computes numbers. Start the shortest and longest from the first word's length, after checking for an empty list, and add every length to the total.
--- hint
`word_report` never counts anything itself: it asks `word_stats` for the numbers and turns them into text with `std::to_string`.
--- check test | The numbers
[] { WordStats s = word_stats({"go", "orbit", "apogee"}); return s.shortest == 2 && s.longest == 6 && s.letters == 13; }()
--- check test | No words: all zero
[] { WordStats s = word_stats({}); return s.shortest == 0 && s.longest == 0 && s.letters == 0; }()
--- check case | The report
word_report({"go", "orbit", "apogee"})
=> "3 words, 2 to 6 letters, 13 in all"
--- check case | An empty word counts as a word of length 0
word_report({"sun", ""})
=> "2 words, 0 to 3 letters, 3 in all"
--- check case | No words
word_report({})
=> "no words"

+++ practice | Evens and odds in a struct
--- task
Return two lists at once in a struct with named members. The starter declares:

```cpp
struct Parity {
    std::vector<int> evens;
    std::vector<int> odds;
};
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `Parity split_parity(const std::vector<int>& v)` puts every even value in `evens` and every odd value in `odds`, each in their original order. Zero is even, and so is -4. Careful: in C++ `-3 % 2` is -1, not 1.
- `std::string describe(const Parity& p)` returns `"2 even, 3 odd"`, with the real counts.

`split_parity({4, -3, 0, 7, -4})` has evens `{4, 0, -4}` and odds `{-3, 7}`, and `describe` of it is `"3 even, 2 odd"`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Parity {
    std::vector<int> evens;
    std::vector<int> odds;
};

Parity split_parity(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    Parity p;
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x % 2 == 1) p.odds.push_back(x);
        else p.evens.push_back(x);
    }
    return p;
}

std::string describe(const Parity& p) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Parity {
    std::vector<int> evens;
    std::vector<int> odds;
};

Parity split_parity(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    Parity p;
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x % 2 == 0) p.evens.push_back(x);
        else p.odds.push_back(x);
    }
    return p;
}

std::string describe(const Parity& p) {
    return std::to_string(p.evens.size()) + " even, " + std::to_string(p.odds.size()) + " odd";
}
--- hint
Test for even, not for odd: `x % 2 == 0` is true for every even number, negative or not. `x % 2 == 1` misses the negative odd numbers.
--- hint
`describe` only reads the struct: the counts are `p.evens.size()` and `p.odds.size()`.
--- check test | Split in order, negatives too
[] { Parity p = split_parity({4, -3, 0, 7, -4}); return p.evens == std::vector<int>{4, 0, -4} && p.odds == std::vector<int>{-3, 7}; }()
--- check case | describe counts both
describe(split_parity({4, -3, 0, 7, -4}))
=> "3 even, 2 odd"
--- check case | Only odd, negative
describe(split_parity({-1, -5}))
=> "0 even, 2 odd"
--- check case | Nothing
describe(split_parity({}))
=> "0 even, 0 odd"

+++ practice | The lowest and highest sensor
--- task
The starter declares:

```cpp
struct Extremes {
    std::string lowest;
    std::string highest;
};
```

Write two functions over a map from sensor names to readings. No `main`.

- `Extremes extremes(const std::map<std::string, int>& readings)` returns the name with the lowest reading and the name with the highest. If readings tie, take the name that comes first alphabetically. For an empty map, both names are `""`.
- `std::string format_extremes(const std::map<std::string, int>& readings)` returns `"low: pump (-4), high: valve (90)"`, with the real names and readings, or `"no readings"` for an empty map.

Walk the map with a structured binding, `for (const auto& [name, value] : readings)`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>

struct Extremes {
    std::string lowest;
    std::string highest;
};

Extremes extremes(const std::map<std::string, int>& readings) {
    Extremes e{"", ""};
    int lo = 0;
    int hi = 0;
    for (const auto& [name, value] : readings) {
        if (value < lo) { lo = value; e.lowest = name; }
        if (value > hi) { hi = value; e.highest = name; }
    }
    return e;
}

std::string format_extremes(const std::map<std::string, int>& readings) {
    return "no readings";
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>

struct Extremes {
    std::string lowest;
    std::string highest;
};

Extremes extremes(const std::map<std::string, int>& readings) {
    Extremes e{"", ""};
    bool first = true;
    int lo = 0;
    int hi = 0;
    for (const auto& [name, value] : readings) {
        if (first || value < lo) {
            lo = value;
            e.lowest = name;
        }
        if (first || value > hi) {
            hi = value;
            e.highest = name;
        }
        first = false;
    }
    return e;
}

std::string format_extremes(const std::map<std::string, int>& readings) {
    if (readings.empty()) return "no readings";
    Extremes e = extremes(readings);
    int lo = readings.find(e.lowest)->second;
    int hi = readings.find(e.highest)->second;
    return "low: " + e.lowest + " (" + std::to_string(lo) + "), high: " + e.highest + " (" + std::to_string(hi) + ")";
}
--- hint
Starting the lowest and highest readings at 0 breaks when every reading is positive, or every one negative. Let the first pair set both, with a `bool first`.
--- hint
The map gives names in alphabetical order, so replacing only on a strictly lower or higher reading keeps the earliest name of a tie.
--- hint
`format_extremes` needs the two readings back. `readings.find(name)->second` reads a value from a `const` map; it is safe here, because both names are in the map, so `find` never gives `end()`.
--- check test | Lowest and highest
[] { Extremes e = extremes({{"pump", -4}, {"tank", 12}, {"valve", 90}}); return e.lowest == "pump" && e.highest == "valve"; }()
--- check test | All positive, with ties
[] { Extremes e = extremes({{"b", 5}, {"a", 5}, {"c", 9}, {"d", 9}}); return e.lowest == "a" && e.highest == "c"; }()
--- check case | The report
format_extremes({{"pump", -4}, {"tank", 12}, {"valve", 90}})
=> "low: pump (-4), high: valve (90)"
--- check case | One sensor is both
format_extremes({{"fan", 3}})
=> "low: fan (3), high: fan (3)"
--- check case | No readings
format_extremes({})
=> "no readings"

+++ practice | The longest streak of equal readings
--- task
The starter declares:

```cpp
struct Streak {
    int value;
    int length;
};
```

Write `Streak longest_streak(const std::vector<int>& v)`. A **streak** is a run of equal values next to each other. Return the value of the longest streak and its length. No `main`.

- `longest_streak({1, 4, 4, 4, 2, 2})` is value 4, length 3.
- If two streaks tie, return the first: `longest_streak({7, 7, 3, 3})` is value 7, length 2.
- A single value is a streak of 1. For an empty list, return value 0, length 0.
--- starter
#include <vector>

struct Streak {
    int value;
    int length;
};

Streak longest_streak(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    Streak best{0, 0};
    int run = 1;
    for (std::size_t i = 1; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        if (v[i] == v[i - 1]) ++run;
        else run = 1;
        if (run > best.length) best = {v[i], run};
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

struct Streak {
    int value;
    int length;
};

Streak longest_streak(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.empty()) return {0, 0};
    Streak best{v[0], 1};
    int run = 1;
    for (std::size_t i = 1; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        if (v[i] == v[i - 1]) {
            ++run;
        } else {
            run = 1;
        }
        if (run > best.length) best = {v[i], run};
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Test the edges: what does the starter return for `{5}`? The first value on its own is already a streak of 1, but the loop starts at index 1 and never sees it.
--- hint
Deal with the empty list first, then start with `Streak best{v[0], 1};`. Replacing only on a strictly longer run keeps the first of a tie.
--- check test | The longest streak
[] { Streak s = longest_streak({1, 4, 4, 4, 2, 2}); return s.value == 4 && s.length == 3; }()
--- check test | A tie goes to the first
[] { Streak s = longest_streak({7, 7, 3, 3}); return s.value == 7 && s.length == 2; }()
--- check test | One value
[] { Streak s = longest_streak({5}); return s.value == 5 && s.length == 1; }()
--- check test | All different: the first value
[] { Streak s = longest_streak({-2, 8, 3}); return s.value == -2 && s.length == 1; }()
--- check test | Empty
[] { Streak s = longest_streak({}); return s.value == 0 && s.length == 0; }()

+++ practice | Debug: a copy-paste slip in the status report
--- task
**Bug report:** "`status_report(10, 50, 90)` says `power OK`, but power is at 90%, which is FULL."

The starter has three copies of the same decision. Refactor: write `std::string level_line(const std::string& name, int percent)` that returns the name, a space, the level and a newline, where the level is `LOW` below 20, `FULL` at 80 or more, and `OK` in between: `level_line("fuel", 20)` is `"fuel OK\n"`. Then make `status_report` return the three lines, fuel then oxygen then power, each built with `level_line`. The numbers 20 and 80 should appear only once each. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string status_report(int fuel, int oxygen, int power) {
    std::string out;
    if (fuel < 20) out += "fuel LOW\n";
    else if (fuel < 80) out += "fuel OK\n";
    else out += "fuel FULL\n";
    if (oxygen < 20) out += "oxygen LOW\n";
    else if (oxygen < 80) out += "oxygen OK\n";
    else out += "oxygen FULL\n";
    if (power < 20) out += "power LOW\n";
    else if (oxygen < 80) out += "power OK\n";
    else out += "power FULL\n";
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string level_line(const std::string& name, int percent) {
    std::string level = "OK";
    if (percent < 20) level = "LOW";
    else if (percent >= 80) level = "FULL";
    return name + " " + level + "\n";
}

std::string status_report(int fuel, int oxygen, int power) {
    return level_line("fuel", fuel) + level_line("oxygen", oxygen) + level_line("power", power);
}
--- hint
Line the three blocks up. In the third one, which variable does the second test look at?
--- hint
Move one block into `level_line`, with the name and the percentage as parameters. Then `status_report` is three calls joined with `+`.
--- check case | The power line is right
status_report(10, 50, 90)
=> "fuel LOW\noxygen OK\npower FULL\n"
--- check test | The boundaries
level_line("fuel", 19) == "fuel LOW\n" && level_line("fuel", 20) == "fuel OK\n" && level_line("fuel", 79) == "fuel OK\n" && level_line("fuel", 80) == "fuel FULL\n"
--- check case | Power low while oxygen is fine
status_report(85, 50, 5)
=> "fuel FULL\noxygen OK\npower LOW\n"
--- check source | status_report uses level_line
level_line\(\s*"power"
--- check source absent | The limit 20 is written only once
<\s*20[\s\S]*<\s*20

+++ practice | Stretch: a trip report
--- task
A rover drives from point to point on a grid. Each **leg** goes from one point in the path to the next, and its length is the grid distance: the difference in `x` without its sign, plus the difference in `y` without its sign. The starter declares:

```cpp
struct Point { int x; int y; };
struct Trip { int legs; long long distance; int longest_leg; };
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `Trip analyze(const std::vector<Point>& path)` returns the number of legs, the total distance, and the index of the longest leg (leg 0 goes from point 0 to point 1). If legs tie for longest, take the first. A path with fewer than two points has 0 legs, distance 0 and `longest_leg` -1. Coordinates can be as big as an `int` allows, so work out each difference in a `long long`.
- `std::string trip_line(const std::string& name, const std::vector<Point>& path)` returns `"name: 3 legs, 17 m, longest leg 2"`, but `"1 leg"` for a single leg, and `"name: no legs"` when there are none.

For the path (0, 0), (3, 4), (3, 6), (-2, 6), the legs are 7, 2 and 5 long: `analyze` gives 3 legs, 14 and leg 0, and the line for `"R1"` is `"R1: 3 legs, 14 m, longest leg 0"`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Point { int x; int y; };
struct Trip { int legs; long long distance; int longest_leg; };

Trip analyze(const std::vector<Point>& path) {
    return {0, 0, -1};
}

std::string trip_line(const std::string& name, const std::vector<Point>& path) {
    return name + ": no legs";
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Point { int x; int y; };
struct Trip { int legs; long long distance; int longest_leg; };

long long leg_length(const Point& a, const Point& b) {
    long long dx = static_cast<long long>(b.x) - a.x;
    long long dy = static_cast<long long>(b.y) - a.y;
    return (dx < 0 ? -dx : dx) + (dy < 0 ? -dy : dy);
}

Trip analyze(const std::vector<Point>& path) {
    Trip t{0, 0, -1};
    long long longest = -1;
    for (std::size_t i = 1; i < path.size(); ++i) {
        long long len = leg_length(path[i - 1], path[i]);
        t.distance += len;
        if (len > longest) {
            longest = len;
            t.longest_leg = t.legs;
        }
        ++t.legs;
    }
    return t;
}

std::string trip_line(const std::string& name, const std::vector<Point>& path) {
    Trip t = analyze(path);
    if (t.legs == 0) return name + ": no legs";
    std::string legs = std::to_string(t.legs) + (t.legs == 1 ? " leg" : " legs");
    return name + ": " + legs + ", " + std::to_string(t.distance) + " m, longest leg " + std::to_string(t.longest_leg);
}
--- hint
Give the repeated sum its own name: a helper `leg_length(a, b)` that works out both differences in `long long` and adds their sizes without the sign.
--- hint
In `analyze`, walk from index 1 so each leg joins `path[i - 1]` to `path[i]`. The index of the leg you are on is the number of legs counted so far.
--- hint
`trip_line` computes nothing itself: it calls `analyze`, then chooses between `"leg"` and `"legs"` and builds the text with `std::to_string`.
--- check test | Three legs
[] { Trip t = analyze({{0, 0}, {3, 4}, {3, 6}, {-2, 6}}); return t.legs == 3 && t.distance == 14 && t.longest_leg == 0; }()
--- check case | The line for three legs
trip_line("R1", {{0, 0}, {3, 4}, {3, 6}, {-2, 6}})
=> "R1: 3 legs, 14 m, longest leg 0"
--- check case | One leg
trip_line("R2", {{1, 1}, {1, -4}})
=> "R2: 1 leg, 5 m, longest leg 0"
--- check case | A tie goes to the first leg
trip_line("R3", {{0, 0}, {2, 0}, {2, 2}, {0, 2}, {0, 1}})
=> "R3: 4 legs, 7 m, longest leg 0"
--- check case | One point, no legs
trip_line("R4", {{5, 5}})
=> "R4: no legs"
--- check test | Huge coordinates do not overflow
[] { Trip t = analyze({{-2000000000, -2000000000}, {2000000000, 2000000000}}); return t.legs == 1 && t.distance == 8000000000LL && t.longest_leg == 0; }()

=== cpp2-gate | C++, intermediate: mastery gate
--- teach
This gate covers the whole course: const-correct classes that keep their rules, vectors with algorithms and lambdas, iterators, maps and sets, overloading, `enum class`, `std::optional`, string streams, and the debugging habits for copies, off-by-ones, overflow and missing values. Each problem mixes two or more lessons, and there are no hints. The questions at the end check that you understand why the code works, not only that it runs. To get ready, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hardest, from memory, and time yourself: about ten minutes a problem.
--- gate
pass 7
questions 9
minutes 112

+++ problem | An inventory that never goes negative
--- task
Write `class Inventory`, which keeps item counts in a private `std::map<std::string, int>`. Its rule: no count is ever negative. Every member function that only reads must be `const`. No `main`.

- `void add(const std::string& item, int qty)` adds `qty` of `item`. A `qty` of 0 or less is ignored and adds nothing, not even the item's name.
- `int remove(const std::string& item, int qty)` takes away up to `qty` of `item`, never going below 0, and returns how many were really taken. A `qty` of 0 or less takes nothing, and an item that was never added gives 0. An item that reaches 0 stays known, with a count of 0.
- `int count(const std::string& item) const` returns the count, or 0 for an unknown item. It must never add the item.
- `std::vector<std::string> low_stock(int limit) const` returns the known items whose count is at most `limit`, in alphabetical order.

After `add("bolt", 10)`, `add("nut", 3)` and `remove("nut", 5)`: `remove` returned 3, `count("nut")` is 0, and `low_stock(2)` is `{"nut"}`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Inventory {
public:
    void add(const std::string& item, int qty) {}
    int remove(const std::string& item, int qty) { return 0; }
    int count(const std::string& item) { return 0; }
    std::vector<std::string> low_stock(int limit) { return {}; }
private:
    std::map<std::string, int> stock_;
};
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

class Inventory {
public:
    void add(const std::string& item, int qty) {
        if (qty <= 0) return;
        stock_[item] += qty;
    }
    int remove(const std::string& item, int qty) {
        auto it = stock_.find(item);
        if (it == stock_.end() || qty <= 0) return 0;
        int taken = qty < it->second ? qty : it->second;
        it->second -= taken;
        return taken;
    }
    int count(const std::string& item) const {
        auto it = stock_.find(item);
        return it == stock_.end() ? 0 : it->second;
    }
    std::vector<std::string> low_stock(int limit) const {
        std::vector<std::string> out;
        for (const auto& [item, n] : stock_) {
            if (n <= limit) out.push_back(item);
        }
        return out;
    }
private:
    std::map<std::string, int> stock_;   // every count >= 0
};
--- check test | Add, remove more than there is, and count through a const reference
[] { Inventory inv; inv.add("bolt", 10); inv.add("nut", 3); int r = inv.remove("nut", 5); const Inventory& v = inv; return r == 3 && v.count("nut") == 0 && v.count("bolt") == 10; }()
--- check case | low_stock keeps items at 0, in order
[] { Inventory inv; inv.add("washer", 1); inv.add("bolt", 10); inv.add("nut", 3); inv.remove("nut", 5); const Inventory& v = inv; return v.low_stock(2); }()
=> std::vector<std::string>{"nut", "washer"}
--- check test | Unknown items: nothing removed, nothing added
[] { Inventory inv; int r = inv.remove("gear", 4); const Inventory& v = inv; return r == 0 && v.count("gear") == 0 && v.low_stock(100).empty(); }()
--- check test | A zero or negative add is ignored entirely
[] { Inventory inv; inv.add("fuse", 0); inv.add("fuse", -3); const Inventory& v = inv; return v.count("fuse") == 0 && v.low_stock(5).empty(); }()
--- check test | A negative remove takes nothing
[] { Inventory inv; inv.add("cap", 4); int r = inv.remove("cap", -2); return r == 0 && inv.count("cap") == 4; }()

+++ problem | Read a range like 12-40
--- task
Write `std::optional<std::pair<int, int>> parse_range(const std::string& s)`. It reads a range written as two whole numbers with a `-` between them, like `"12-40"`, and returns the pair `{low, high}`. No `main`.

- Spaces are allowed around each number: `" 12 - 40 "` is `{12, 40}`.
- There must be exactly one `-`, each side must be exactly one whole number, and `low` must not be bigger than `high`. `"7-7"` is `{7, 7}`.
- Anything else is `std::nullopt`: `""`, `"12-"`, `"-40"`, `"40-12"`, `"a-3"`, `"1-2-3"`, `"12x-40"`, `"1 2-40"`.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

std::optional<std::pair<int, int>> parse_range(const std::string& s) {
    return std::nullopt;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

std::optional<int> whole_number(const std::string& field) {
    std::istringstream in(field);
    int n;
    if (!(in >> n)) return std::nullopt;
    in >> std::ws;
    if (!in.eof()) return std::nullopt;
    return n;
}

std::optional<std::pair<int, int>> parse_range(const std::string& s) {
    std::size_t dash = s.find('-');
    if (dash == std::string::npos || s.find('-', dash + 1) != std::string::npos) return std::nullopt;
    auto low = whole_number(s.substr(0, dash));
    auto high = whole_number(s.substr(dash + 1));
    if (!low || !high || *low > *high) return std::nullopt;
    return std::pair<int, int>{*low, *high};
}
--- check test | Plain, spaced and equal ranges
parse_range("12-40") == std::pair<int, int>{12, 40} && parse_range(" 12 - 40 ") == std::pair<int, int>{12, 40} && parse_range("7-7") == std::pair<int, int>{7, 7}
--- check test | A side is missing
parse_range("") == std::nullopt && parse_range("12-") == std::nullopt && parse_range("-40") == std::nullopt && parse_range("-") == std::nullopt
--- check test | Backwards, or more than one dash
parse_range("40-12") == std::nullopt && parse_range("1-2-3") == std::nullopt
--- check test | A side that is not one whole number
parse_range("a-3") == std::nullopt && parse_range("12x-40") == std::nullopt && parse_range("1 2-40") == std::nullopt && parse_range("3-4.5") == std::nullopt
--- check test | Zero is a real number
parse_range("0-0") == std::pair<int, int>{0, 0}

+++ problem | Where the traffic light will be
--- task
A traffic light cycles Red for 30 seconds, then Green for 25, then Yellow for 5, and back to Red: one cycle is 60 seconds. The starter declares `enum class Signal { Red, Green, Yellow };`. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `std::string signal_name(Signal s)` returns `"Red"`, `"Green"` or `"Yellow"`.
- `Signal signal_at(Signal start, int seconds)`: the light has **just turned** to `start`. It returns the colour `seconds` seconds later. `seconds` can be 0, very large, or negative (meaning that many seconds earlier).

From the start of Red: 29 seconds later it is still Red, 30 seconds later it is Green, 55 seconds later Yellow, and 60 seconds later Red again. From the start of Green, 1 second earlier (`-1`) it was Red.
--- starter
#include <string>

enum class Signal { Red, Green, Yellow };

std::string signal_name(Signal s) {
    return "";
}

Signal signal_at(Signal start, int seconds) {
    return start;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

enum class Signal { Red, Green, Yellow };

std::string signal_name(Signal s) {
    switch (s) {
        case Signal::Red:    return "Red";
        case Signal::Green:  return "Green";
        case Signal::Yellow: return "Yellow";
    }
    return "";
}

int start_of(Signal s) {
    switch (s) {
        case Signal::Red:    return 0;
        case Signal::Green:  return 30;
        case Signal::Yellow: return 55;
    }
    return 0;
}

Signal signal_at(Signal start, int seconds) {
    int t = start_of(start) + seconds % 60;
    t = ((t % 60) + 60) % 60;
    if (t < 30) return Signal::Red;
    if (t < 55) return Signal::Green;
    return Signal::Yellow;
}
--- check test | Names
signal_name(Signal::Red) == "Red" && signal_name(Signal::Green) == "Green" && signal_name(Signal::Yellow) == "Yellow"
--- check test | Through one cycle from Red, edges included
signal_at(Signal::Red, 0) == Signal::Red && signal_at(Signal::Red, 29) == Signal::Red && signal_at(Signal::Red, 30) == Signal::Green && signal_at(Signal::Red, 54) == Signal::Green && signal_at(Signal::Red, 55) == Signal::Yellow && signal_at(Signal::Red, 60) == Signal::Red
--- check test | Starting from Green and Yellow
signal_at(Signal::Green, 25) == Signal::Yellow && signal_at(Signal::Yellow, 5) == Signal::Red && signal_at(Signal::Yellow, 4) == Signal::Yellow
--- check test | Seconds in the past
signal_at(Signal::Green, -1) == Signal::Red && signal_at(Signal::Red, -1) == Signal::Yellow && signal_at(Signal::Red, -6) == Signal::Green
--- check test | Huge numbers
signal_at(Signal::Red, 2000000000) == Signal::Red && signal_at(Signal::Red, -2000000000) == Signal::Green

+++ problem | Rank the pilots by their average
--- task
Write `std::vector<std::string> rank_by_average(const std::map<std::string, std::vector<int>>& scores)`. Each pilot's name maps to their list of scores. Return the names ordered by average score, highest first. No `main`.

- Pilots whose averages are equal go in alphabetical order. Compare averages exactly: 1/3 and 2/6 are equal.
- A pilot with no scores is left out.
- Scores can each be as big as an `int` allows, so a pilot's total may not fit in an `int`.

For `{"ann": {90, 70}, "bo": {85}, "cy": {}, "di": {80, 80, 80, 100}}` the averages are 80, 85 and 85, so the answer is `{"bo", "di", "ann"}`.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::vector<std::string> rank_by_average(const std::map<std::string, std::vector<int>>& scores) {
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (const auto& [name, list] : scores) names.push_back(name);
    return names;
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Tally {
    std::string name;
    long long total;
    long long count;
};

std::vector<std::string> rank_by_average(const std::map<std::string, std::vector<int>>& scores) {
    std::vector<Tally> tallies;
    for (const auto& [name, list] : scores) {
        if (list.empty()) continue;
        long long total = 0;
        for (int s : list) total += s;
        tallies.push_back({name, total, static_cast<long long>(list.size())});
    }
    std::sort(tallies.begin(), tallies.end(), [](const Tally& a, const Tally& b) {
        // a.total / a.count > b.total / b.count, without dividing
        if (a.total * b.count != b.total * a.count) return a.total * b.count > b.total * a.count;
        return a.name < b.name;
    });
    std::vector<std::string> names;
    for (const auto& t : tallies) names.push_back(t.name);
    return names;
}
--- check case | Highest average first, ties alphabetical, empty lists left out
rank_by_average({{"ann", {90, 70}}, {"bo", {85}}, {"cy", {}}, {"di", {80, 80, 80, 100}}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"bo", "di", "ann"}
--- check case | Averages that are equal as fractions
rank_by_average({{"x", {1, 0, 0}}, {"a", {2, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0}}, {"m", {1}}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"m", "a", "x"}
--- check case | Totals past two billion
rank_by_average({{"big", {2000000000, 2000000000}}, {"mid", {2100000000}}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"mid", "big"}
--- check case | Negative scores
rank_by_average({{"p", {-5, -1}}, {"q", {-2}}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{"q", "p"}
--- check case | Nobody with scores
rank_by_average({{"cy", {}}})
=> std::vector<std::string>{}

+++ problem | The best window, fast
--- task
Write `std::optional<long long> best_window(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k)`. It returns the biggest total of `k` values next to each other, or `std::nullopt` when there is no such window because `k` is 0 or bigger than `v.size()`. No `main`.

- `best_window({4, -1, 6, -8, 3}, 2)` is 5 (from 4 and -1, or -1 and 6: both total 5).
- Totals can pass 2 billion, and can be negative.
- The last check has 200,000 values and a window of 100,000, so adding up every window from scratch is far too slow. When the window slides one step to the right, one value leaves it and one value joins it.
--- starter
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

std::optional<long long> best_window(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k) {
    return std::nullopt;
}
--- solution
#include <optional>
#include <vector>

std::optional<long long> best_window(const std::vector<int>& v, std::size_t k) {
    if (k == 0 || k > v.size()) return std::nullopt;
    long long sum = 0;
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < k; ++i) sum += v[i];
    long long best = sum;
    for (std::size_t i = k; i < v.size(); ++i) {
        sum += v[i] - static_cast<long long>(v[i - k]);
        if (sum > best) best = sum;
    }
    return best;
}
--- check test | A small list
best_window({4, -1, 6, -8, 3}, 2) == 5LL && best_window({4, -1, 6, -8, 3}, 3) == 9LL
--- check test | The whole list, and single values
best_window({4, -1, 6, -8, 3}, 5) == 4LL && best_window({4, -1, 6, -8, 3}, 1) == 6LL
--- check test | All negative
best_window({-7, -2, -9}, 2) == -9LL
--- check test | Totals past two billion
best_window({2000000000, 2000000000, -5}, 2) == 4000000000LL
--- check test | No window
best_window({1, 2}, 3) == std::nullopt && best_window({1, 2}, 0) == std::nullopt && best_window({}, 1) == std::nullopt
--- check test | 200,000 values, a window of 100,000
[] { std::vector<int> v(200000, 1); v[150000] = 1000; return best_window(v, 100000) == 100999LL; }()

+++ problem | Decode a telemetry packet
--- task
A telemetry packet is a line of fields separated by `;`, each written `KEY=VALUE`, like `"ALT=1200;VEL=-3.456;MODE=ASCENT"`. Write `std::string decode(const std::string& packet)`. No `main`.

- Split at every `;`, and at the first `=` in each field. Trim spaces from the key and from the value.
- Keep only the fields whose value is exactly one number (a `double`, spaces around it allowed) and whose key is not empty. Skip everything else, including empty fields and fields with no `=`.
- Write each kept field as the key, a space, and the value with exactly one decimal place, joined by `" | "`, in the order they appear.
- If no field is kept, return `"(no data)"`.

`decode("ALT=1200;VEL=-3.456;MODE=ASCENT;;TEMP= 21.26 ")` is `"ALT 1200.0 | VEL -3.5 | TEMP 21.3"`.
--- starter
#include <cctype>
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>

std::string decode(const std::string& packet) {
    return "(no data)";
}
--- solution
#include <cctype>
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::string trim(const std::string& s) {
    std::size_t a = 0;
    std::size_t b = s.size();
    while (a < b && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[a]))) ++a;
    while (b > a && std::isspace(static_cast<unsigned char>(s[b - 1]))) --b;
    return s.substr(a, b - a);
}

std::vector<std::string> split(const std::string& s, char sep) {
    std::vector<std::string> out;
    std::size_t start = 0;
    while (true) {
        std::size_t at = s.find(sep, start);
        if (at == std::string::npos) {
            out.push_back(s.substr(start));
            return out;
        }
        out.push_back(s.substr(start, at - start));
        start = at + 1;
    }
}

std::string decode(const std::string& packet) {
    std::ostringstream out;
    out << std::fixed << std::setprecision(1);
    bool any = false;
    for (const auto& field : split(packet, ';')) {
        std::size_t eq = field.find('=');
        if (eq == std::string::npos) continue;
        std::string key = trim(field.substr(0, eq));
        if (key.empty()) continue;
        std::istringstream num(field.substr(eq + 1));
        double value;
        if (!(num >> value)) continue;
        num >> std::ws;
        if (!num.eof()) continue;
        if (any) out << " | ";
        out << key << " " << value;
        any = true;
    }
    return any ? out.str() : "(no data)";
}
--- check case | Numbers kept, words and empty fields skipped
decode("ALT=1200;VEL=-3.456;MODE=ASCENT;;TEMP= 21.26 ")
=> "ALT 1200.0 | VEL -3.5 | TEMP 21.3"
--- check case | Spaces around keys, and junk after a number
decode(" rpm = 3000 ; fuel=12kg ;x")
=> "rpm 3000.0"
--- check case | An empty key and an empty value are skipped
decode("=5;v=;w=7")
=> "w 7.0"
--- check case | Nothing usable
decode("MODE=SAFE;;")
=> "(no data)"
--- check case | Empty packet
decode("")
=> "(no data)"

+++ problem | The longest balanced run
--- task
A reaction wheel logs its speed changes, positive and negative. A **balanced run** is a run of one or more changes next to each other whose total is exactly 0. Write `int longest_balanced(const std::vector<int>& v)`: the length of the longest balanced run, or 0 if there is none. No `main`.

- `longest_balanced({3, -1, -2, 4, 0, -4})` is 6: the whole list totals 0.
- `longest_balanced({1, 2, -3, 5})` is 3, from 1, 2 and -3.
- `longest_balanced({0})` is 1, and `longest_balanced({1, 2})` is 0.

The last check has 200,000 values, so trying every start and end is too slow. Use running totals: two positions with the same running total have a balanced run between them.
--- starter
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

int longest_balanced(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

int longest_balanced(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    std::unordered_map<long long, int> first_seen;   // running total -> first position
    first_seen.emplace(0, 0);
    long long total = 0;
    int best = 0;
    for (int j = 0; j < static_cast<int>(v.size()); ++j) {
        total += v[j];
        auto it = first_seen.find(total);
        if (it != first_seen.end()) {
            int length = j + 1 - it->second;
            if (length > best) best = length;
        } else {
            first_seen.emplace(total, j + 1);
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- check case | The whole list
longest_balanced({3, -1, -2, 4, 0, -4})
=> 6
--- check case | A run at the start
longest_balanced({1, 2, -3, 5})
=> 3
--- check case | A run in the middle
longest_balanced({5, 2, -2, 7})
=> 2
--- check case | A single zero
longest_balanced({0})
=> 1
--- check case | No balanced run
longest_balanced({1, 2})
=> 0
--- check case | Empty
longest_balanced({})
=> 0
--- check case | 200,000 values
[] { std::vector<int> v; for (int i = 0; i < 100000; ++i) { v.push_back(1); v.push_back(-1); } v[0] = 7; return longest_balanced(v); }()
=> 199998

+++ problem | Money that is always right
--- task
Write `class Money`, which stores an amount as one whole number of cents. No `main`.

- `Money(int dollars, int cents)` accepts any values, including cents over 99 or negative ones: the amount is `dollars * 100 + cents` cents. So `Money(1, 150)` is $2.50, `Money(0, -5)` is -$0.05 and `Money(-1, 50)` is -$0.50.
- `int cents() const` returns the whole amount in cents.
- `Money plus(const Money& other) const` returns the sum as a new `Money`, and an overload `Money plus(int cents) const` adds that many cents.
- `std::string to_string() const` returns a `$`, the dollars, a point and exactly two digits of cents, with a `-` in front of the `$` for a negative amount: `"$2.50"`, `"-$0.05"`, `"$0.00"`, `"$1234.07"`.
--- starter
#include <string>

class Money {
public:
    Money(int dollars, int cents) : dollars_(dollars), cents_(cents) {}
    int cents() const { return cents_; }
    std::string to_string() const { return "$" + std::to_string(dollars_) + "." + std::to_string(cents_); }
private:
    int dollars_;
    int cents_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>

class Money {
public:
    Money(int dollars, int cents) : total_(dollars * 100 + cents) {}
    int cents() const { return total_; }
    Money plus(const Money& other) const { return Money(0, total_ + other.total_); }
    Money plus(int cents) const { return Money(0, total_ + cents); }
    std::string to_string() const {
        int size = total_ < 0 ? -total_ : total_;
        std::string c = std::to_string(size % 100);
        if (c.size() < 2) c = "0" + c;
        return std::string(total_ < 0 ? "-" : "") + "$" + std::to_string(size / 100) + "." + c;
    }
private:
    int total_;   // the whole amount, in cents
};
--- check test | Cents over 99 and negative cents
Money(1, 150).cents() == 250 && Money(0, -5).cents() == -5 && Money(-1, 50).cents() == -50
--- check test | Text for positive, negative and zero amounts
Money(1, 150).to_string() == "$2.50" && Money(0, -5).to_string() == "-$0.05" && Money(-1, 50).to_string() == "-$0.50" && Money(0, 0).to_string() == "$0.00" && Money(1234, 7).to_string() == "$1234.07"
--- check test | Both plus overloads
Money(2, 50).plus(Money(0, 75)).to_string() == "$3.25" && Money(2, 50).plus(-300).to_string() == "-$0.50" && Money(0, 5).plus(95).to_string() == "$1.00"
--- check test | plus leaves the original alone
[] { const Money m(3, 0); Money n = m.plus(1); return m.cents() == 300 && n.cents() == 301; }()

+++ problem | Merge two sorted lists, once each
--- task
Write `std::vector<int> merge_unique(const std::vector<int>& a, const std::vector<int>& b)`. Both lists are sorted smallest first, and each may hold repeats. Return one list, sorted smallest first, holding every value that appears in either list exactly once. No `main`.

`merge_unique({1, 3, 3, 8}, {2, 3, 9, 9})` is `{1, 2, 3, 8, 9}`. Either list, or both, may be empty.
--- starter
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> merge_unique(const std::vector<int>& a, const std::vector<int>& b) {
    std::vector<int> out = a;
    for (int x : b) out.push_back(x);
    return out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

std::vector<int> merge_unique(const std::vector<int>& a, const std::vector<int>& b) {
    std::vector<int> out;
    std::size_t i = 0;
    std::size_t j = 0;
    while (i < a.size() || j < b.size()) {
        int next = 0;
        if (j == b.size() || (i < a.size() && a[i] <= b[j])) {
            next = a[i];
            ++i;
        } else {
            next = b[j];
            ++j;
        }
        if (out.empty() || out[out.size() - 1] != next) out.push_back(next);
    }
    return out;
}
--- check case | Repeats inside and across the lists
merge_unique({1, 3, 3, 8}, {2, 3, 9, 9})
=> std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3, 8, 9}
--- check case | Negative values
merge_unique({-5, -5, 0}, {-7, 0, 4})
=> std::vector<int>{-7, -5, 0, 4}
--- check case | One list empty
merge_unique({}, {2, 2, 6})
=> std::vector<int>{2, 6}
--- check case | Both empty
merge_unique({}, {})
=> std::vector<int>{}
--- check case | The same list twice
merge_unique({4, 5}, {4, 5})
=> std::vector<int>{4, 5}

+++ problem | How many passes overlap
--- task
A ground station tracks satellite passes. Each pass is a pair `{start, end}` in seconds, and it is a half-open range: the pass is in view from `start` up to, but not including, `end`. A pass whose `end` is not after its `start` is never in view.

Write `int max_overlap(const std::vector<std::pair<int, int>>& passes)`: the largest number of passes in view at the same moment. No `main`.

- For `{{0, 10}, {5, 15}, {10, 20}}` the answer is 2. At second 10 the first pass has just left as the third arrives, so they never overlap.
- For `{{1, 5}, {2, 6}, {3, 7}}` it is 3. With no passes, or only empty ones like `{4, 4}`, it is 0.
--- starter
#include <algorithm>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

int max_overlap(const std::vector<std::pair<int, int>>& passes) {
    return static_cast<int>(passes.size());
}
--- solution
#include <algorithm>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

int max_overlap(const std::vector<std::pair<int, int>>& passes) {
    std::vector<std::pair<int, int>> events;   // {time, +1 for a start or -1 for an end}
    for (const auto& [start, end] : passes) {
        if (end <= start) continue;
        events.push_back({start, 1});
        events.push_back({end, -1});
    }
    std::sort(events.begin(), events.end(), [](const std::pair<int, int>& a, const std::pair<int, int>& b) {
        if (a.first != b.first) return a.first < b.first;
        return a.second < b.second;   // at the same time, ends (-1) come before starts (+1)
    });
    int now = 0;
    int best = 0;
    for (const auto& [time, change] : events) {
        now += change;
        if (now > best) best = now;
    }
    return best;
}
--- check case | Touching passes do not overlap
max_overlap({{0, 10}, {5, 15}, {10, 20}})
=> 2
--- check case | Three at once
max_overlap({{1, 5}, {2, 6}, {3, 7}})
=> 3
--- check case | Nested passes
max_overlap({{0, 100}, {10, 20}, {30, 40}, {15, 35}})
=> 3
--- check case | Given in any order, with negative times
max_overlap({{8, 9}, {-5, 8}, {-10, -4}})
=> 2
--- check case | Empty passes never count
max_overlap({{4, 4}, {9, 2}})
=> 0
--- check case | No passes
max_overlap({})
=> 0

+++ question | A function that changed nothing
--- ask
What does this program print?

```cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>

void bump(std::vector<int> v) {
    for (int& x : v) x += 10;
}

int main() {
    std::vector<int> d = {1, 2};
    bump(d);
    std::cout << d[0] + d[1] << "\n";
}
```
--- answer
3
--- why
`bump` takes its vector by value, so it changes a copy that is thrown away when it returns. The `int&` in the loop only makes the loop change the copy's own elements. The caller's `d` is still `{1, 2}`, and 1 + 2 is 3. Taking `std::vector<int>& v` would print 23.

+++ question | Zero is not missing
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
std::optional<int> r = 0;
std::cout << r.value_or(-1) << (r ? " yes" : " no") << "\n";
```
--- answer
0 yes
--- why
`r` holds a value, and that value happens to be 0. `value_or(-1)` gives the value when there is one, so it prints 0. An optional used as a condition asks "is there a value?", not "is the value non-zero?", so `r` counts as true and it prints `yes`.

+++ question | The search that finds nothing
--- ask
This code prints the first reading over 10:

```cpp
auto it = std::find_if(v.begin(), v.end(), [](int x) { return x > 10; });
std::cout << *it << "\n";
```

What is wrong with it?
--- choice
`find_if` needs `v.rbegin()` and `v.rend()` to search the whole vector.
--- choice correct
When no reading is over 10, `it` is `v.end()`, which is not an element, and `*it` is undefined behavior. Compare `it` with `v.end()` first.
--- choice
The lambda must capture `v` to be able to see the readings.
--- choice
`find_if` returns the index of the match, so `*it` is not allowed on it.
--- why
`find_if` gives back an iterator: a position. When nothing matches, the position is `end()`, the stopping mark one past the last element. Following it with `*` reads memory that is not part of the vector. The lambda only needs its parameter; each element is handed to it.

+++ question | A getter without const
--- ask
`Wallet` has `int cents() { return cents_; }`, with no `const`. Why does `void show(const Wallet& w) { std::cout << w.cents(); }` fail to compile?
--- choice
Because `cents_` is private, and a free function cannot see private members.
--- choice
Because a `const` reference cannot be used to read anything at all.
--- choice correct
Through a `const Wallet&`, only member functions marked `const` may be called, and `cents()` never promised not to change the wallet.
--- choice
Because `show` should take the wallet by value to be allowed to call it.
--- why
The compiler does not look inside `cents()` to see that it only reads. It trusts the promise, and without `const` after the parameter list there is no promise. `show` reaches the private data only through the public getter, which is allowed; the problem is purely the missing `const`.

+++ question | Only looking at a map
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
std::map<std::string, int> fuel;
if (fuel["main"] > 0) {
    std::cout << "ready\n";
}
std::cout << fuel.size() << "\n";
```
--- answer
1
--- why
`fuel["main"]` inserts the key `"main"` with the value 0 when it is missing, even though the code only meant to read it. 0 is not greater than 0, so `ready` is not printed, but the map now holds one pair. `fuel.contains("main")` or `fuel.find("main")` would only look.

+++ question | Adding up halves
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
std::vector<double> v = {0.5, 0.5, 0.5};
std::cout << std::accumulate(v.begin(), v.end(), 0) << "\n";
```
--- answer
0
--- why
`std::accumulate` adds in the type of its starting value, and `0` is an `int`. Each step is cut back to a whole number: 0 + 0.5 becomes 0, again and again. Starting from `0.0` gives 1.5. The same rule is why `0LL` stops an overflow when adding big `int`s.

+++ question | A loop over an empty vector
--- ask
`v` is an empty `std::vector<int>`. What happens when this loop runs?

```cpp
for (std::size_t i = 0; i < v.size() - 1; ++i) {
    total += v[i];
}
```
--- choice
Nothing: the condition is `0 < -1`, which is false, so the loop never runs.
--- choice correct
`v.size() - 1` wraps round to a gigantic number, because `std::size_t` cannot be negative, so the loop runs and reads far outside the vector.
--- choice
The compiler refuses to build it, because `v.size() - 1` could be negative.
--- choice
`v[i]` stops the program with an out-of-range error on the first pass.
--- why
Unsigned arithmetic wraps: 0 − 1 is 18446744073709551615 for a 64-bit `std::size_t`, and there is no warning. `v[i]` does no checking, so it quietly reads garbage. Writing the condition as `i + 1 < v.size()` only adds, so it cannot wrap.

+++ question | What the lambda kept
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
int n = 1;
auto f = [n]() { return n * 10; };
n = 5;
std::cout << f() << "\n";
```
--- answer
10
--- why
`[n]` captures a copy of `n` at the moment the lambda is made, when `n` is 1. Changing `n` afterwards does not reach the lambda's copy, so `f()` returns 10. With `[&n]` it would use the variable itself and return 50.

+++ question | Which overload wins
--- ask
There are two overloads, `std::string f(bool b)` and `std::string f(const std::string& s)`. Which one does `f("hi")` call, and why?
--- choice
The string one, because `"hi"` is text and text is a `std::string`.
--- choice correct
The `bool` one, because `"hi"` arrives as a `const char*`, and turning a pointer into a `bool` is a standard conversion, which beats building a `std::string`, a user-defined conversion.
--- choice
Neither: the call is ambiguous, so it does not compile.
--- choice
The string one, because the compiler always prefers the overload declared last.
--- why
A string literal is a row of `char`s, handed over as a pointer to the first one. Standard conversions rank above user-defined ones, so the `bool` overload wins, and any pointer that is not `nullptr` is `true`. Adding an exact-match `f(const char*)` overload fixes it.

+++ question | The cost of one pass with a notebook
--- ask
`two_sum` walks a list of n numbers once, and for each one does a `find` and an `emplace` on a `std::unordered_map`. What does it cost on average, as the list grows?
--- choice
O(1): hash tables answer in constant time, so the whole function does too.
--- choice
O(log n), like a lookup in a `std::map`.
--- choice correct
O(n): one pass over n numbers, with about the same few steps for each lookup.
--- choice
O(n²): every number is compared with every number before it.
--- why
Each `find` and `emplace` on an `unordered_map` is O(1) on average, but it happens once per number, so the total grows in step with n. The brute force that tries every pair is the O(n²) one: for 200,000 numbers, about 20 billion pairs against 200,000 lookups.

+++ question | The case that ran twice
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
enum class Mode { A, B, C };

int score(Mode m) {
    int s = 0;
    switch (m) {
        case Mode::A: s += 1;
        case Mode::B: s += 10; break;
        case Mode::C: s += 100; break;
    }
    return s;
}

std::cout << score(Mode::A) << "\n";
```
--- answer
11
--- why
The `Mode::A` case has no `break`, so after `s += 1` it falls through into the `Mode::B` case and also does `s += 10`, then stops at that `break`. 1 + 10 is 11. Every case should end in `break` or `return`.

+++ question | Reading a number from 12abc
--- ask
What happens here?

```cpp
std::istringstream in("12abc");
int n;
bool ok = static_cast<bool>(in >> n);
```
--- choice
The read fails, because `12abc` is not a number, so `ok` is false and `n` is 0.
--- choice correct
The read succeeds: `n` is 12 and `ok` is true, and `abc` is still waiting in the stream, so `in.eof()` is false.
--- choice
`n` becomes 12 and the stream skips the rest, so `in.eof()` is true.
--- choice
The program stops with an error, because the text has letters in it.
--- why
`>>` reads as long as the characters can be part of a number and stops at the first one that cannot. That counts as a successful read. To be sure a whole field is a number, also check that nothing but spaces is left: `in >> std::ws`, then `in.eof()`.
